Chapter 1: The Kindling

Toren Voss had been thirteen for six hours, and so far it had not made him any better at splitting wood.

The axe came down and skidded off the round of ash instead of biting it, which was the fourth time it had done that, and the fourth time he had felt the shock of it run up through both wrists and settle somewhere behind his teeth. He set the round upright again. He looked at it. He had been told by three different adults that there was a grain to wood and that a man who could read it never had to swing twice, and he believed them, in the way he believed the road went all the way to Meridian — as a thing that was true for other people.

"You're aiming at the wood," Dessa Cole said.

"That's generally the idea."

"You're aiming at the top of the wood." She was sitting on the concrete lip of the overpass footing with her boots off and her heels drumming, sorting through a tin of dried apple like she expected one of the pieces to be better than the others. "Aim at the ground under it. The wood's just in the way."

Toren swung. The round came apart in two clean halves and one of them tipped off the block and knocked over the stack he'd already made.

"There," said Dessa, not looking up.

It was the fifth of the month and the light was going long and orange across the valley, and their camp sat in the shelter of a highway that had stopped being a highway seventy years ago. Two great spans of it still stood, root-thick pillars going down into the grass, the roadbed above them broken off at both ends like a bridge in a story about a bridge that had failed to be one. The overpass made a roof. Under a roof you could have a fire without the whole valley seeing it, and Odo Prentice, who had walked this route eleven times and had eleven times been paid for it, said that mattered more the further east you went.

Eleven times. Toren had counted the tally marks scratched into the shaft of the man's walking staff on the second day out and had not been able to stop thinking about them since. Eleven times out and eleven times back. Twenty-two crossings of a country that Toren had been told, all his life, in a hundred different tones of voice, was not crossable.

They were five days out of Coldwater. There were eleven days left, if the weather held and the fords were where Odo said they'd be. Eleven days to the wall at Meridian, where there were machines that had survived the Fall and people who knew how to make them work, and where — Toren's father said this the same way every time, flat, like reading a number off a page so it couldn't turn into a hope — where they would look at his grandmother and tell them what could be done.

Behind him, in the cart, Senna coughed.

It was not a dramatic sound. That was the part Toren had never gotten used to. It was a small dry thing, three or four beats, over almost before you registered it, and then she would be quiet for an hour and you could pretend you had imagined it. He had been listening to that cough since he was old enough to listen to anything. It had gotten a little worse every year in a way you could only see if you looked across a long stretch of years, the way you couldn't see the road wearing down but could see that it had.

"Toren." His father's voice, from the far side of the fire. "Enough wood."

"There's more of the ash—"

"There's enough wood." Garrick Voss straightened up from the mule's near foreleg with a hoof pick in one hand and a look on his face that Toren had learned to read at about the same time he'd learned to read. "Come here and hold this animal's head so she stops trying to sit on me."



The mule's name was Patience, which Odo said was a joke that had been funny for about a week eleven years ago.

Toren held her halter and put his forehead against the long warm plane of her cheek while his father worked a stone out of the frog of her hoof, and he watched the rest of the camp assemble itself into evening the way it had every night for five nights.

Ilsa Cole had the cook pot and was doing something to a handful of grain that she would insist was soup. She was a wide, sunburnt, immensely capable woman who had run the mill wheel in Coldwater and who had, Toren was fairly sure, decided somewhere around the second day that she was in charge of feeding all eight of them whether or not anyone had asked. Dessa had her mother's hands and none of her patience.

Milo Faust was on the far side of the fire with a stick, drawing something in the dirt and then rubbing it out and drawing it again. Milo was thirteen too — three months ahead of Toren, a fact he had brought up an unreasonable number of times over the last five days — and he had been Kindled at the end of summer, and what he had gotten out of it was the ability to make a small pale light sit in the cup of his palm. It didn't burn. It didn't do anything. It went out if he sneezed. He had spent two months being the most disappointed person in Coldwater and had recently arrived, with visible effort, at making jokes about it instead.

And Wyck Tanner was up on the concrete, twenty feet above the fire, sitting on the broken lip of the roadbed with his legs hanging into the air and his aunt's voice floating up at him telling him to come down from there.

Wyck did not come down from there.

He was fifteen. He had been Kindled two years, and he had an Ember, and he was, on the strength of those two facts, the most important person in the traveling party as far as Wyck Tanner was concerned. Toren had watched him work it that morning while the others were breaking camp: standing off in the wet grass with the thing lit in his fist, running it out into a blade as long as his arm and holding it, holding it, holding it, jaw set, until the light guttered and he had to shake his hand out. Forty seconds, and then nothing.

"He's going to fall off there," Dessa said, arriving at Toren's elbow with the tin of apple.

"He's not."

"He's going to fall off there and then we're all going to have to be very kind to him about it for the whole rest of the trip." She held out the tin. "Nervous?"

"No."

"Liar."

Toren took a piece of apple. "Little bit."

"It doesn't hurt," she said. "Mine didn't hurt."

"Milo says his hurt."

"Milo says a lot of things." She tipped her head back and looked up at the underside of the overpass, at the rust-stains bleeding down out of the concrete in long streaks like something had wept there for seventy years. "It's more like — you know when you've been asleep on your arm, and you wake up, and there's nothing there, and then it all comes back at once and it's awful and also sort of interesting?"

"That sounds like it hurts."

"That's not hurting, that's sensation." She said the word carefully, the way she said all the words she'd gotten out of books. Dessa had been Kindled at fourteen, a year and change ago, and what she had gotten was small too: she could feel where people were with her eyes shut, out to maybe thirty feet, and she could close a shallow cut if she worked at it for a while and then had to go lie down. In Coldwater they had said that was a waste of a Kindling on a girl who could already find people by shouting. Dessa had said, to their faces, that it was better than what any of them had gotten, since none of them had gotten anything, and then she had been in a certain amount of trouble.

"Dessa." Ilsa, from the fire, without turning around. "Leave him be. It's nearly time."



They did it at last light, because that was when it was done.

Toren did not know why. Nobody did, exactly. His grandmother said the ceremony had been made up out of nothing by frightened people in the first years after the Fall, back when children started coming into things nobody could explain and the adults needed a shape to put around it so they wouldn't scream. She said this without any particular reverence and also, Toren had noticed, without ever once suggesting they skip a single part of it.

The fire was built up. The eight of them made a ring around it, which meant it was a small ring and everybody's shins got too hot. Senna was carried out of the cart by Garrick and Odo together and set on a folded blanket on the concrete footing, and she waved them both off with a hand like a bundle of sticks in a glove and said she was fine, she was fine, get away.

"Eldest speaks," she said.

"You're the eldest," said Odo.

"I know I'm the eldest, Prentice, I was telling you what happens next."

Toren knelt in the dirt in front of the fire because that was the part he remembered from watching it done for other children in Coldwater — you knelt, and you put your hands out palms up on your knees, and you did not close your eyes, and you did not look away from the fire even when it made your eyes water, which it did almost immediately.

His grandmother's voice went out over the top of him. It was not a strong voice anymore. It was very clear.

"There was a world," Senna said, "and it burned. There was a road out of it and most people did not find the road. We found the road. Toren Voss, thirteen years old today, son of Garrick, son of my son — you are on the road because we carried you here. From tonight you carry yourself."

He was supposed to say I'll carry myself. He said it. It came out cracked in the middle and Milo, on the other side of the fire, made a face at him that was not unkind.

"There was a world and it burned," Senna said, "and something came in after through the hole it left, and it woke up something in us that was already there and sleeping. We don't know what it is. We don't know why it's ours. Anyone who tells you they know is selling you something. Toren Voss. Do you take what's yours, knowing that nobody living can tell you what it is?"

"I take what's mine."

"Then take it," his grandmother said, "and be careful with it, because your grandfather wasn't."

That was not part of it. Toren looked up, startled, and saw the old woman's face above the fire, absolutely composed, and saw his father's face beside her, absolutely not.

"Ma," Garrick said.

"He's thirteen, not stupid." Senna settled her hands in her lap. "Give him the thing."



His father came around the fire and knelt down in the dirt facing him, which nobody had done at any Kindling Toren had ever seen, and which made his chest go tight in a way he did not have a word for.

Garrick Voss was a big, slow-moving, quiet man who fixed things. That was the whole of him as far as Coldwater was concerned and as far as Toren had ever needed him to be. He had large square hands with a permanent grey line under every nail, and when he was working out how to do something he made a small sound in his throat, and he was making it now.

He took the Ember out of his coat.

Toren had seen it perhaps twenty times in his life. It lived in a wooden case under the floorboards and it came out when the settlement had a problem, and the problem was usually something with too many legs that had come down out of the hills, and Toren had always been sent inside before the actual solving of it. It was about the length of his father's forearm and about as thick as two fingers, dark grey, tapering slightly at both ends, and it was worn glass-smooth in one band around the middle where four generations of Voss thumbs had sat in exactly the same place.

His father did not hand it to him right away.

"You know what it does," Garrick said.

"Edge and Stride."

"Say the rest."

Toren swallowed. "Edge is the weapon. It'll take whatever shape you can hold in your head, and the bigger the shape the harder it is, and if you get greedy you'll drop it. Stride is — the other one. Speed. Jumping. It runs down your legs and holds you up." He hesitated. "One at a time. Never both. You pick, and you live with what you picked until you can put it down and pick again."

"Why never both?"

"Because that's not how it works."

His father looked at him for a moment across the top of the fire's heat.

"That's what I was told, too," Garrick said, and put the Ember into Toren's hands.

It was warm. That was all, for one long stupid second — it was warm, like a stone out of the sun, and Toren had time to think is that it with a lurch of complete despair —

— and then something behind his eyes opened.

There was no other way to have it. Something opened, some door he had never known was in him because it had never once moved, and past it there was a space, and the space had been there his entire life. It was not full of anything. That was the part he could never explain afterward to anyone, including himself. It was not a light, or a voice, or a power. It was room. It was an enormous quiet room in a house he'd been living in for thirteen years and had never gone all the way to the back of, and the door to it had just come off its latch, and cold air was coming through.

He heard himself make a sound.

His hands closed on the Ember without being told to. The warmth ran up his forearms and into his shoulders and down into his chest and stopped there, low, behind the breastbone, and settled in like something taking a seat. And every hair on his arms stood up, and his eyes flooded, and the fire smeared into a long gold streak, and through the streak he saw the metal in his fists put out light.

It ran the length of the Ember in one clean stroke, root to tip, pale and steady.

"There he is," said Odo Prentice, very quietly.

Toren could not speak. He was thinking, with the small remaining functional part of himself, that everybody had lied to him about this, every single one of them, all of them who had said it's not much and don't expect anything, because this was not not much, this was the biggest thing that had ever happened, this was —

The light went out.

"Oh," he said.

"Yeah," said Wyck from the dark behind the fire, and there was something almost sympathetic in it. "It does that."



Later, when the fire had burned down and Ilsa had made everyone eat the grain, his father walked him out past the pillars to where the grass started.

The valley went dark blue and then black. There were no lights anywhere in it. Toren had never in his life seen a light he did not know the source of.

"I've got one more thing," Garrick said.

He took it out of an inner pocket, out of a scrap of oiled cloth, and held it flat on his palm, and Toren leaned in.

It was a disk. It was about the size of the round bottom of a cup, and it was heavier than it had any business being — Toren knew that before he touched it, from the way it sat in his father's hand. It was some dark metal that wasn't quite any color. Nine narrow channels ran across its face, cut in a pattern that his eye kept trying to make into a shape and failing, so that every time he looked away and back it seemed to have rearranged itself, which he knew was not true and could not stop thinking.

"That's not an Ember," Toren said.

"No."

"What is it?"

"I don't know," his father said. "My father didn't know. His mother didn't know. It's been in this family five generations and not one of us has ever gotten a single useful thing out of it, and every one of us carried it anyway." He turned his hand and offered it. "Take it."

Toren took it.

It was warm too, and that was not the same warmth. The Ember had been warm like a held stone. This was warm like a room you had lived in. He stood in the wet grass in the dark with the thing in his two hands and felt, so strongly that his throat closed, that he had found it — that it had been missing, that he had been looking, that some part of him he did not have access to had been quietly missing this object for thirteen years and had just stopped.

He had never seen it before in his life.

"You feel that," Garrick said. It was not a question.

"What is it?"

"Everybody feels it. Every one of us. My father called it the homesickness." His father was quiet for a moment. Out in the valley something small moved through the grass and went still. "There's a story that comes with it. You want the story?"

"Yes."

"It's a stupid story."

"I want the story."

"It says that one day that disk is going to save the world," Garrick said, "or end it. And that nobody has ever been able to tell which, and that anybody who tells you they can is lying, and that it's the same object either way." He reached out and closed Toren's fingers over it, one at a time, with his big careful mechanic's hands. "Five generations we've handed that down. And every single one of us has handed it down the same way, with the same words, and Toren — I've thought about those words my whole life, and I want you to hear what I actually think."

"All right."

"I think somebody a long time ago got given that thing," his father said, "and loved whoever gave it to them too much to say the true version out loud. So they made it sound like a gift. It isn't a gift. It's a debt. Somebody handed it to us and we've been carrying it ever since and not one of us has ever once been able to put it down." His hands tightened. "If it comes to it — if it ever comes to a choice between that and you — you put it in a river. Do you understand me? You put it in a river and you don't tell anyone where."

Toren stood there with the disk in his fist and the Ember heavy in his coat and the enormous new quiet room open at the back of his head, and he said, "Yes."

He was lying. His father knew he was lying. Neither of them said so.



They left at first light and made good time until midmorning, and then Odo Prentice stopped in the middle of the road with his hand up.

Toren came up beside him. The road here ran along a shoulder of hill with the land dropping away to the left into scrub, and there was a marker at the bend — one of Odo's own, a slab of concrete stood on end with a notch cut in the top edge for each safe crossing.

There was a fresh gouge in it. Not a notch. A gouge, four parallel lines the depth of a finger joint, raked from the top of the stone down across the face of it, through the old marks, cutting them out.

"That's new," said Ilsa.

"That's new," Odo agreed.

Toren became aware, slowly, of a thing he had been hearing for some time without hearing it. He turned his head. He looked up the hill, and down the road, and out across the whole broad bright valley falling away to the south, and there was wind in the grass and there was Patience shifting her feet in the traces and there was nothing else at all.

"Where are the birds," he said.

Nobody answered him. Down the road, in the dry scrub, something clicked twice and stopped.



Chapter 2: What the Road Brought

Odo Prentice had a rule about the scrub, and the rule was that you never went into it.

He said it twice on the way down the hill shoulder, once to the group generally and once to Wyck specifically, and Toren understood by the second time that Odo was frightened and was managing it by giving orders. The scrub started forty feet below the roadbed and went on for a mile and a half of grey-green thornbrush the height of a man, and there was no path through it, and there was no seeing into it, and the road was the only thing in the world that was not it.

"We walk," Odo said. "We walk at the pace of the cart and nobody gets ahead of the cart and nobody gets behind it. Tanner, that means you."

"I'm scouting."

"You're a boy with a stick and a light on it. Get behind the cart."

Wyck's face did something ugly and then closed up, and he fell back, and Toren made a point of not looking at him.

They walked. Patience did not want to. She had gone stiff-legged and high-headed at the marker stone and she stayed that way for the next mile, walking like an animal that expects the ground to be taken away from her, and Garrick walked at her head with a hand flat on her neck the whole time, saying nothing at all in a low continuous murmur.

Toren had his hand in his coat pocket. He had the Ember in it. He had had his hand in that pocket since the marker stone and his fingers had gone numb around the middle of the shaft and he could not make himself let go.

"You're going to wear a hole in that coat," Dessa said.

"Do you feel anything?"

"I feel eight people and a mule and nothing else, and I've been checking every fifty steps, and I'm going to have a headache by noon." She rubbed the bridge of her nose. "It's thirty feet, Toren. Something could be at forty feet and I'd never know."

"But you'd know at thirty."

"I'd know at thirty."

That should have been comforting. Toren worked out, over the following quarter mile, exactly why it was not: because the road was twelve feet wide, and the scrub started at forty.



It came out of the brush at the bend where the road cut back on itself, and it did not make any sound at all until it was already up on the roadbed.

Toren's first thought, and he was ashamed of it later for years, was that it was beautiful.

It came up over the shoulder in one long pour of motion, four limbs and then a fifth and then he understood he had counted wrong, and it was the height of a horse at the back and lower at the front, and its hide was the flat wet grey of slate under rain. The joints were in the wrong places. That was the thing his eye kept catching and dropping and catching again — every limb had one more bend in it than a limb should have, so that when it moved it did not step, it folded and unfolded, and the folding was smooth and unhurried and terrible.

Down the center of its chest there was a seam. As it came up onto the road the seam brightened, a thin hot line the color of a coal split open, and Toren felt the light of it on his face from thirty feet away.

Odo Prentice shouted one word.

It was not a word. It was the front half of one. He got his staff up crosswise in both hands, which was the correct thing, which was the thing a man who had crossed this country twenty-two times would do, and the creature went through the space where he was standing without appearing to change speed at all, and Odo went off the shoulder of the road into the scrub.

He did not come back up.

Ilsa Cole screamed. Patience went straight up in the traces and came down and tried to run and the cart's near wheel came off the roadbed and hung, and Garrick had both hands in the halter and his boots skidding through gravel, hauling her head around, and the cart came back down on the road with a crash that Toren felt through his feet.

Senna was in that cart.

Toren got the Ember out of his pocket.

It lit. That was the one thing that worked — it lit the instant his skin was properly on it, the pale line running root to tip, easy as breathing, and for one heartbeat he thought yes —

— and then it was a warm metal rod in his hand and nothing else.

Edge. He knew what Edge was. He had watched his father hold a blade of light three feet long out of this exact object, standing on the wall at Coldwater, six years ago, when they had forgotten to send Toren inside. He knew what it looked like. He had the shape in his head as clear as anything he had ever thought.

Nothing happened.

He thought it harder. He thought it so hard his eyes watered.

Nothing happened.

The creature turned on the road. It did not turn the way an animal turns. The back end came around first and the front end followed as an afterthought, and the split-coal seam in its chest slid its light across the gravel, and then it was facing the cart, and it clicked twice in its throat.

Something in the scrub clicked back.

Then something else did, further along. Then two more, and then a fourth from up the hill on the other side, above the road, where nobody had been watching at all.

"Off the road!" Garrick roared. "Everybody up the bank! Ilsa, get them up the bank —"



Toren did not go up the bank.

He would think about that later too. He would think about it constantly, in the specific way you think about a thing you cannot change: that his father had given an order, a clear one, the first clear one of the worst hour of his life, and Toren had not gone up the bank because his grandmother was in the cart and the cart was on the road.

He ran toward it instead, and the second one came out of the scrub eight feet in front of him.

It was smaller than the first. It was still bigger than he was. It unfolded up out of the thorn with its head low and its seam banked down to an ember-glow. It looked at him — it had no eyes he could find and it absolutely, unmistakably looked at him — and its head tracked, very slightly, down, to the lit thing in his fist.

It's looking at the Ember.

That was the first real thought he had in the whole fight and it was correct, and he did not know it was correct, and he threw it away immediately because there was no time to have thoughts.

He swung.

He swung a two-foot metal rod at a thing with too many joints, with no blade on it, with no training, with his whole shoulder behind it and his feet in completely the wrong place, and he missed, and the miss spun him most of the way around, and his boot went off the edge of the roadbed.

The world tipped. Gravel went out from under him. He went down hard on his hip and slid, and thornbrush came up and caught him across the ribs and the face, and his hand — the hand with the Ember in it, the hand he had clenched shut the way you clench a hand when you are falling —

Light. A stroke of hot white a hand and a half long, straight out of the end of the shaft, there and gone in less than a second.

It went through the thing's forelimb just below the second joint.

The creature came apart from the wound outward. Not messily. It went to ash — warm grey ash with light still crawling through it, folding in on itself the way a paper does in a fire, and the whole shape of it collapsed into a drift that hung in the air and then came down over Toren in a soft warm rush that smelled like a struck match and tasted, faintly, like the back of his own throat.

He lay in the thorn with his heart going like a bird in a box.

The Ember did that.

He looked at it. It was dark again. It was a warm dull rod in his fist, and thirty seconds ago he had wanted a blade out of it more than he had ever wanted anything, and it had given him nothing at all; and then he had fallen down a bank and not wanted anything except to not be falling, and it had opened up and cut a thing in half.

It did that on its own. It decided.

The idea arrived whole and he believed it entirely, the way you believe the first explanation that fits, and it settled into him and became the floor he stood on for a long time afterward.



Getting back up the bank took nine seconds and cost him the skin off both palms.

The road, when he came over the lip of it, had turned into something he did not recognize.

There were four of them on it now. The big one had the cart — it was up against the tailboard with its forelimbs hooked over, folding and unfolding, working itself in, and the cart was rocking and Patience was screaming, and Garrick Voss was standing on the cart's step with a splitting axe in both hands.

He doesn't have the Ember.

Toren had no time for that thought either. It went in and lodged somewhere and waited.

Nell Tanner was on the upslope with the children behind her — Milo, Dessa, Wyck, all three of them scrambling for the high ground with Ilsa hauling at their coats — and Nell had a bill-hook off the cart and she was standing across the line of the bank. She was fifty-one years old and had run a tannery, and she planted her feet and swung at the thing coming up at her and connected, and it did not matter.

Toren saw the whole of it. That was the worst part. He was forty feet away with a dead rod in his hand and he saw the entire thing happen from beginning to end with perfect clarity and it took less than two seconds and he could not have crossed forty feet in two seconds if he had been the fastest thing alive.

Wyck made a sound Toren had never heard a person make.

"Up! Up up up—" Ilsa Cole had Wyck by the back of the collar and was dragging him bodily backward up the slope with Dessa shoving from below, and Milo had both hands out in front of him and there was a small pale light sitting in his palms, and he was holding it out toward the thing on the bank like a man holding out a lamp, because it was every single thing he had.

The creature ignored him completely. It went past Milo's light. It went up the slope past all three of them without slowing, and Toren, staring, understood in a cold flat way that made no sense to him at all: it isn't going for them.

It was going for Wyck.

Wyck had his Ember out. Wyck had it lit and had it half-shaped, a stub of blade wavering at the end of it, because Wyck had been Kindled two years and had trained on a fence post every day of it and this was the one thing Wyck Tanner could actually do.

"Put it out!" Toren screamed. "Wyck — put it out, put it—"

Wyck did not hear him, or heard him and did the opposite, and the blade steadied and lengthened and got bright, and every clicking thing in the scrub for a hundred yards turned toward the light on that hillside.



Toren ran for the cart.

He had worked one thing out and he had worked it out wrong, and the wrongness of it would take a long time to surface and would cost more than he could imagine standing there in the road. But he had worked something out, and it was this:

It didn't cut when I told it to. It cut when I stopped telling it.

So he ran with the Ember held loose. Not gripped — loose, rolling in his fingers, the way you carry a tool you're not using. He did not picture a blade. He did not picture anything. He got to the tailboard of the cart with the big one's foreclaw eight inches from his grandmother's blanket and he brought the rod up flat under it and thought, as clearly as he could think anything, please.

The Ember put out four inches of light.

Four. Not three feet. Not even the hand and a half from the bank. Four inches of hot white at the tip of the shaft, and it lasted about as long as it took him to shove it upward, and it opened the thing's forelimb across the second joint, and the limb came off in a slur of warm ash and the rest of the creature dropped off the tailboard and staggered sideways down the road.

It did not die. It backed off eight feet on its remaining limbs and stood there, and the seam in its chest brightened, and it clicked, and Toren stood with his back against the tailboard and his grandmother's hand fastening on his shoulder through the slats, and he thought, with a savage lurching joy that made him briefly sick:

It listened to me.

"Toren." Senna's voice at his ear, thin and completely steady. "Boy. Look at me."

"I did it — Gran, I did it, I asked it and it—"

"Look at me."

He turned his head.

His grandmother's face was between two slats of the cart, grey as ash, and her eyes went past him down the road, and Toren turned to follow them.

The scrub was coming apart along a hundred yards of its length. Not at one place. All along it, from the bend behind them to the long straight ahead, the thornbrush was bending and folding and opening, and they were coming up out of it in ones and twos and fours, unhurried, every seam in every chest lit, and there were more of them than Toren could hold in his head at once.

And on the hillside above the road, a hundred feet up and utterly alone, Wyck Tanner stood in the open grass with three feet of white light burning in his fist and every single one of them turning toward it.



Chapter 3: What It Cost

There were two places to be and Toren could only be in one of them.

That was the whole shape of the next four minutes and he understood it immediately, standing with his back against the tailboard and his grandmother's fingers dug into his shoulder through the slats. Up the hill there was Wyck, alone in open grass with a light in his fist. Down here there was the cart, and the cart had one wheel a hand's width from the shoulder of the road, and below the shoulder there was forty feet of drop into thornbrush, and inside the cart there was Senna Voss, who weighed less than a sack of grain and could not stand up.

"Garrick!" Ilsa Cole was already climbing. "Garrick, the boy—"

"I know."

"Then go—"

"I can't leave this cart." Toren's father had the splitting axe in both hands and blood coming out of his hairline into his eye and he did not look up the hill even once. "Ilsa. I can't leave this cart."

She went without another word.

Toren would remember that his whole life: that she did not argue, and did not make him say it twice, and did not look back at her own daughter. She went up a forty-degree grass bank in her mill apron with a bill-hook in one hand, at fifty-three years old, because somebody had to and the somebody was her.

She got most of the way.

Dessa was screaming from the upper slope. Milo had both arms around her waist and his heels dug in and the little useless light still burning in one hand because he had forgotten to put it out. And Ilsa reached Wyck and did the only correct thing, the thing Toren had been screaming at him to do for thirty seconds: she hit the boy's wrist and knocked the Ember out of his hand and it went dark in the grass, and the whole hillside's worth of turned heads lost the thing they had been turning toward.

They kept coming anyway. They were already coming. Momentum is not attention.

Toren looked away.

He did not decide to. His body did it, turned his head hard down and to the left, and by the time he could make himself look back there was warm grey ash coming down through the grass in a slow drift and Wyck Tanner was on his knees in it with both hands pressed flat to the ground.



"Toren." His father's voice, absolutely level. "The wheel."

"Dad—"

"The wheel. Get the stone under it. Now, son."

There was a wedge stone on a rope hanging off the cart's side rail for exactly this, for hills, and Toren's hands were shaking so badly it took him three tries to get it free and two more to work it into the gap in front of the tire. The near wheel was over the edge as far as its rim. Every time Patience shifted, the whole cart leaned another finger's width and the gravel under the wheel poured away down the slope in a hissing runnel, and there was a point coming — Toren could see it as clearly as he could see his own hands — where the cart would stop being a cart on a road and become a weight on a hill.

"Cut the mule loose," Senna said from inside.

"What?"

"Cut the mule loose, Garrick, she'll take the whole thing over—"

"Not yet."

Three of them came up the roadbed at once.

Toren's father stepped off the cart step into the road and met them with a splitting axe, and Toren, crouched at the wheel with a stone in both hands, finally had the thought he had been carrying since the top of the hour.

He gave me the Ember last night.

Garrick Voss had been an Ember-user for twenty-six years. He had held three feet of light on the wall at Coldwater and Toren had watched him do it. And last night at a fire under a broken overpass he had put the thing into his son's hands, because that was what you did on the day, because he had thought there were eleven quiet days of road left, and this morning he had walked out onto it with a tool for cutting firewood.

The axe was not nothing. That was the thing. Toren's father was a big man who had swung that axe every winter of his adult life and he knew exactly what the head weighed and exactly where it wanted to go. He took the first one across the throat-joint and it came apart in ash, and he stepped through the drift of it and took the second one low, in the fold of a limb, and it went down and did not get up.

The third one put him against the cart.

Toren heard his father's back hit the boards. He came up off the wheel with the Ember lit and loose in his fingers, thinking please, please as hard as he could think anything, and got four inches of white light, and drove it into the thing's flank from behind, and it went to ash, and Garrick got an arm around him and put him bodily behind the wheel.

"That's good," his father said. He was breathing wrong. "That's good, that's — where did you learn that?"

"I didn't."

"That's good," Garrick said again.

And then the big one came back for the tailboard.



Toren never got a clean look at what happened in the next ten seconds, because for six of them he was under the cart.

He knew this much. The big one — the first one, the one with a forelimb missing at the second joint, which had been sitting eight feet down the road for two full minutes doing nothing at all — came in low and fast at the cart's rear and took the tailboard off its pins. He knew that his father got between it and the gap and shoved, with the whole of himself, with his boots skidding, and that for a moment it was just a large man pushing against a large animal on loose gravel, ordinary and physical and almost stupid, the way the biggest moments turn out to be.

He knew that the cart's near wheel came off the road.

He knew the sound the cart made going over the shoulder, and the sound his grandmother did not make, and the long tearing crash of it going down into thorn, and Patience's scream as the traces took her sideways off the edge after it.

And he knew that he came out from under the axle into open road with no cart to stand behind and nothing in his hand but a rod that gave him four inches of light when it felt like it, and that there were eleven of them on the road with him, and that his father was down on one knee in the gravel with one hand flat on the ground and the other arm hanging.

The eleven of them turned toward the lit thing in Toren's fist.

It's the light. It's always been the light. It's not us, it's the—

Something came down the hillside.



Afterward, when Toren tried to put it in order, he could never get it to sit still. He would have it clear for a second and then it would slide, the way the pattern on the disk slid when he looked away and back.

There was a man on the bank above the road. He was there and then he was not on the bank, he was forty feet down the slope, and forty feet is not a distance a person crosses without steps in between. There was light around his legs — not on them, around them, a bright bracing frame that ran from hip to heel and put a hard blue-white edge on every stone he touched. He hit the roadbed on the outside of one boot, and the gravel went out from under him the way it had gone out from under Toren, and he did not fall. He turned the slide into the turn.

He had an Ember in his right hand. Toren saw him roll it once against his palm, thumb over the grip, a small unhurried motion like a man checking the time — and then it lit, and it kept going, and it did not stop at three feet.

It came out as a spear. Not a blade: a spear, seven feet of it, tapering to a point so thin the far end was hard to look at.

He put it through the big one from the side without slowing down.

The thing came apart in a wash of ash that went up over the man and past him, and he was already moving, and the frame of light was still burning around his legs.

Toren's mouth was open. He could not close it.

You can't do that.

He knew four Ember-users. He had known them all his life. He had listened to every one of them say the same sentence in the same tone — one at a time, you pick and you live with it — and he had said it back to his father twelve hours ago, kneeling in the dirt, and he had been told he had it right.

The man on the road had both.

He went down the length of the roadbed and he did not fight in exchanges. That was the other thing Toren could not make fit. Every fight he had ever seen — Wyck on his fence post, his father on the wall — had been a thing you did in beats: set, swing, recover, set. This man never set. He came out of a long low glide with the spear already dropping to half its length for the close work, and the shortening was the strike, and then it was two short blades, one in each hand, and the second hand had been empty a moment before and Toren had no idea where the second blade had come from or what it was made of.

Four of them went to ash in the time it took Toren to draw two breaths.

The rest broke off the road and went for the bank, for the high grass, for the three children standing in it — and the man stopped dead in the middle of the road, turned his shoulders uphill, and put his left hand out.

Nothing came out of his hand. That was what Toren's eye insisted on, and his eye was wrong. The air changed. Fifty feet of hillside went hard and thick and wrong, the grass all leaning at once as if a wind had come from directly above it, and the things on the slope stopped as though they had run into a wall that had always been there.

It lasted about three seconds.

In the third second the man's arm began to shake.

Then the pressure let go, and the grass came back up, and the man was already crossing the distance with that bracing light around his legs, and it was finished on the hillside in a way Toren mostly did not watch, because he had finally turned around and seen his father in the road.



Garrick Voss was sitting with his back against the cart's broken step, in the place where the cart had been.

"There you are," he said.

Toren got down in the gravel.

"Don't." His father's good hand came up and caught him by the front of the coat before he could get at the wound, and it was strong, and that was the worst thing, that the hand was still strong. "Don't. Listen. Your grandmother's in the scrub."

"Dad—"

"She's in the scrub and she's alive, I heard her, and you're going to go and get her out. That's the job." Garrick's eyes came around and found him, and focused, with visible effort. "Where's your Ember."

"I've got it."

"Show me."

Toren showed him. His father looked at it — the family Ember, glass-smooth in a band around the middle where four generations of thumbs had sat, twelve hours in the fifth generation's hand — and something went out of his face that Toren did not understand until much later, and then he nodded once.

"Toren. The other thing. The disk."

"I've got that too."

"River," his father said.

"Dad—"

"River." The hand tightened in his coat and then, over about two seconds, did not. "If it comes to it. That's all. That's all I wanted."

There was more that Toren said. He could never afterward remember any of it, and he was fairly certain none of it was answered.



The man came down the road.

He stopped eight feet away, which was, Toren would understand eventually, a deliberate distance. He put the Ember away inside his coat without looking at it. Both hands were shaking and he shoved them in his pockets to stop it.

He was not old. He was somewhere in the country between thirty and fifty where road-worn men stop giving a number. He had a flat-planed, unremarkable face gone rough with a week of not shaving, dark hair cut short by someone in a hurry, and a long grey coat with the elbows gone through and re-sewn in a different grey. There was a leather wrap on the grip of the Ember he'd put away, worn through in one place near the base, showing metal.

He looked at Garrick Voss for a while. He did not say anything about him.

"How many of you," he said.

Toren couldn't make words.

"Boy. How many of you were there."

"Nine," Toren said. "Eight. Nine with — the guide. Odo."

The man's eyes went up the hill, where Dessa was coming down through the grass with Milo behind her and Wyck behind Milo, all three of them moving like people underwater. He counted them, watching them come down through the grass, and then stopped.

"That's four of you," the man said.

"My grandmother's in the scrub. She's alive."

Something went across the man's face, fast, and was gone. He did not look pleased about it.

"Any grown," he said. "Any grown at all. An uncle. Anyone with a claim on you."

Dessa reached the road. She stood at the edge of it with her arms wrapped around her own ribs and her mother's ash in the weave of her coat and she said, in a voice that was almost normal, "No."

The man stood in the middle of the road with his hands in his pockets and his shoulders down and looked at the four of them, one at a time, unhurried, the way a man looks at a load he has just been told is his.

"No," he said, to nobody, and it did not sound like an answer to her.

He turned and went down the shoulder into the thornbrush after the cart without another word, and eleven minutes later he came back up carrying Senna Voss in both arms, and her leg was broken in two places and there was ash all through her white hair, and she was awake, and she was looking at him with a very strange expression.

"What's your name," she said.

"Rook."

"That's not a name."

"It's what I'm called," the man said, and set her down in the road as gently as if she were something he had been asked to look after, and did not say anything else for a long time.



Chapter 4: After

The ash did not blow away.

That was the first thing Toren noticed once there was room in him to notice anything. It lay on the roadbed in long soft drifts where the things had come apart, warm grey with a slow crawl of light still going through it, and the wind came up the valley and moved the grass on both banks and did not move the ash at all. It sat there. It was going somewhere else, slowly, downward, the way water goes into sand.

"Don't stand in it," Rook said.

Dessa was standing in it. She had been standing in it for some time with her boots buried past the laces, and when he spoke she came back from wherever she had been and looked down.

"It's warm," she said.

"It's not warm. You're feeling it because you're rift-touched and it's going into the ground under you. It'll make your head ache tomorrow and you'll think it's grief." He crouched by Senna and did not look up. "Go and stand on clean road."

Dessa did not move.

"Cole," Rook said, and it was not sharp, and it worked. She stepped out of the drift and stood on the gravel with her arms around her ribs. There was ash on her coat that had nothing to do with the road. She had not brushed it off. She was not going to.



Senna Voss's left leg was broken below the knee and again above the ankle, and she had a boot-print of a bruise coming up across her whole left side from the cart's rail, and she was, by any accounting Toren had ever heard of, an old woman who had been thrown forty feet down a hillside inside a wooden box.

She was also entirely awake and had opinions.

"You can put it in a splint," she said. "I've had a splint before."

"Splint's for after." Rook had her boot off and both hands on her shin, one above the break and one below. "Hold still and don't help."

"I'm not helping."

"You're talking."

Toren knelt where he could see. It seemed important to see. He had a vague, half-formed notion that he was supposed to be watching over her, that somebody now had to, and that in the absence of any better applicant it was going to be him, and he was thirteen years old and had been Kindled for eighteen hours.

Rook put one hand flat on the road, in the ash.

Toren saw it happen and never once, in all the months after, worked out what he had actually seen. The drift under the man's palm went dim. Not scattered — dim, the crawling light in it dulling out from his hand in a slow spreading circle a foot across, and everything that had been in it going somewhere, and the somewhere was Rook's arm. And then his other hand, the one on Senna's shin, got faintly warm-looking around the edges the way air over a fire does, and Senna Voss said one flat syllable through her teeth and gripped the road with both hands.

It took a long time. The ash under Rook's left hand went from grey to a colorless nothing that the wind, finally, picked up and took.

"That's the ends together," Rook said, sitting back. "The rest is you and it's going to take six weeks and it's going to hurt every day of it. Do you understand me?"

"I've been dying for twenty years," said Senna. "I know how long things take."

Rook looked at her properly for the first time.

"Reactor?"

"Scavenger run. Eighty miles north of here, before this one's father could walk." She nodded at Garrick and then, without any change of expression at all, looked at the sky instead. "Meridian's got a machine that can see the whole of me at once. That's where we were going."

"I know where you were going. It's the only place anybody's going on this road." He stood up. His hands were shaking again and he put them away again. "Boy. Come here."

Toren came.

"Hands."

He put them out. The palms had come off on the gravel bank and had been bleeding and had stopped and started twice, and he had not thought about them once.

Rook did not put his hand in the ash this time. He took his Ember out of his coat, rolled it once against his palm the way he had on the road, and laid it flat across Toren's two upturned hands, and held it there.

It was warm — the deep steady warmth of the Kindling fire, not the awful crawling heat of the drift — and it went in through the torn skin and out the backs of his wrists, and the raw scoured feeling in both palms banked down into a dull ache and then into nothing much at all. Rook took the Ember away. The cuts were still there. They had stopped mattering.

"That's a different thing," Toren said. "What you did for her — that was different."

"It was."

"Which one's the real one?"

Rook looked at him for about two seconds longer than the question deserved.

"Get the shovel off the cart," he said. "If the cart's got a shovel."



There was a shovel. It was the only thing in the wreck that came out unbroken.

They buried three people in the long grass above the road because the roadbed was too hard and the scrub was full of thorn, and it took the rest of the day and most of what any of them had left. Rook dug. He did not ask any of them to and he did not stop any of them when they took turns, and Wyck took a turn that went on far too long, and nobody said anything about that either.

Odo Prentice they never found. Rook went into the scrub twice, the second time for the better part of an hour, and came back out and shook his head once, and set the man's staff upright in the ground at the end of the row and drove it in with a stone. Eleven notches, and the fresh gouge across all of them.

Toren's father went in the first grave. His grandmother sat on the ground beside it the whole time with her splinted leg out in front of her and her hand flat on the turned earth, and she did not cry, and Toren, who was crying so hard he could not see, understood dimly that this was a thing she was doing on purpose and would pay for later.

Ilsa Cole went in the second. Dessa said the words. She got most of the way through them.

Nell Tanner went in the third, and Wyck said nothing at all, and when Milo put a hand on his shoulder he shrugged it off so hard he nearly went over.

Patience they did not bury. Rook went down into the thornbrush before he did anything else that afternoon, alone, and Toren heard nothing from down there, and Rook came back up wiping his hands on the grass and said the mule was seen to. He said it in a way that closed the subject. Milo asked anyway, because Milo always asked, and Rook told him the truth in one short sentence, and Milo went and sat by himself on the concrete for twenty minutes.

Toren thought that was cruel until much later, when he understood that every other adult he had ever known would have lied.



They ate at dusk, sitting in a rough circle in the grass above the road with a fire that Rook built too small and would not let them build up.

There was food. That was almost the strangest part of the whole day. The cart had gone over the edge, but the cart had been packed by Ilsa Cole, and Ilsa Cole had packed it in six separate bundles distributed around the bed on the theory that you never put everything in one place, and four of the bundles had survived the fall. There was flour and dried apple and a great deal of salt fish nobody wanted and a sack of the mill's own coarse meal.

Rook made a flat pan-bread out of the meal and a little water and cooked it on a stone.

And then, before he gave any of it out, he broke a piece off the first one, put it on a flat rock at the edge of the fire, and set it aside.

He did not say anything about it. He did not look at it. He handed the rest around, one piece each, and started on his own, and the piece on the rock stayed there.

Toren watched it the whole meal.

"Is that for something?" Milo asked. Of course he did.

"No," said Rook.

"It's just — you put it—"

"I know what I did."

Milo, who was thirteen and had a light in his hands that did nothing and had spent two months learning to laugh about it, said, "Right," and ate his bread, and let it go, and Toren found that he liked Milo Faust a great deal more than he had liked him yesterday.

The piece of bread was still on the rock in the morning. Toren looked for it when he woke. It had been rained on.



"I'm not taking you east," Rook said.

They were all looking at him. He was standing with his back to them, feeding the fire down to nothing, and he had clearly been building up to this for some hours and had decided that the only tolerable way to do it was to get it out in one piece.

"There's a relay station two days north-east of here on the loop road. Tallow Gate. Walled, garrisoned, they take road-traffic and they've got a charter and a warden and a duty of care, and they're two days from here at the speed your grandmother's going to manage, which is slow." He put the last stick on. "I'll walk you to the gate. I'll say what happened. And then that's where I stop."

Nobody spoke.

"I'm not going to Meridian," Rook said. "I'm not on that road. I was never on that road, I was crossing it, and I was a mile and a half out when I heard your mule, and I came because I was close enough to." He turned around. His face had nothing on it at all. "That's the whole of it. I want you to have it plain now instead of getting it in pieces over two days and thinking I'm working up to something."

"You're leaving us at a wall," said Wyck.

"I'm leaving you at a garrison, with a warden, with your grandmother's name and your dead written down in a ledger, which is more than you'd have got out of anybody else on this road today." Rook did not raise his voice. It was somehow worse. "You want me to pretend it's better than that? It isn't. It's the best thing available and it's still bad. You're going to be alive at the end of it. That's the only part of this I can actually do anything about."

"You could take us."

"No."

"You could—"

"No," Rook said, and there was a fractional thing in it — not anger, something under anger, something Toren did not have a name for and would spend a long time trying to name — and Wyck stopped.

Senna Voss said, from the ground, "How far can you carry an old woman before it starts costing you?"

Rook looked at her.

"About two days," he said.

"Then we'd better start."



They started a little after seven in the morning, in a grey light with the rain still deciding.

They had one shovel, four bundles, a splinted grandmother, a bill-hook, a splitting axe, three Embers between five people and only two people who could do anything with one. And they had a man in a re-sewn grey coat walking forty feet out in front of them, never once looking back to see if they were keeping up, which they were, because he had set the pace to the slowest of them without ever saying that was what he was doing.

Toren walked at the head of the litter poles with his father's Ember in his coat pocket and his hand around it.

He had worked something out during the burying and he had not said it to anyone and was not going to.

His father had been an Ember-user for twenty-six years. His father had stood on a wall at Coldwater with three feet of light in his fist. And two nights ago, at a fire under a broken overpass, on a road, five days out, with eleven days still to go, his father had knelt down in the dirt in front of him and given it away.

It's a debt, Garrick Voss had said, about a different object entirely, and Toren had not understood him at all.

He understood him now.

He put his other hand in his other pocket, where the disk sat, nine channels warm against his knuckles, feeling exactly like a room he had lived in.

There was a river three miles up the loop road. Rook had mentioned it at first light, in the tone of a man listing obstacles. Toren thought about it for all three miles.

He did not put the disk in it. He did not get anywhere close to putting the disk in it. He stood on the plank bridge with the water going grey-brown underneath and his hand in his pocket for a count of ten while the others went past him, and then he walked on, and the shame of that stayed with him longer than most of the rest of it.



Chapter 5: The Waypoint

Tallow Gate had been a fuel depot once.

You could see the bones of it from a mile out on the loop road: four enormous rust-red cylinders standing in a row inside a ring of earth berm, and a fifth one lying on its side across the only break in the berm, cut open at both ends, so that the way in and out of the settlement was a tunnel through a fallen tank about eighty feet long. Somebody had hung two doors in it. Somebody else had spent a great deal of effort painting a number on the outside of the tank and then given up halfway through the second digit.

Toren had never seen a wall before. Coldwater had a fence.

"Don't stare," said Rook.

"I'm not staring."

"You're all staring. Every one of you, you look like a delegation of owls." He did not slow down. "It's a hole in the ground with a lid on it. Straighten up. Walk like people who've been somewhere."

They straightened up. It made a difference that Toren found faintly insulting.

It had taken them two and a half days, not two, because Senna Voss on a litter went at the speed she went and no faster, and because on the second afternoon there had been a thing in the culvert under the loop road that Rook had gone down and dealt with alone, and had come back up from with ash in his hair and a set to his mouth, and had said nothing about.

Milo had asked. Of course. "How many?"

"One," Rook had said. "One is plenty. One is how everybody dies. Nobody ever gets killed by a hundred of them, they get killed by one they didn't count."

Milo had turned that over for about a mile.

"Is that a lesson?"

"It's a fact," Rook said. "Lessons are the ones you have to pay for."



Warden Ottoline Braid was a small square woman of about sixty with reading glasses pushed up into a great deal of grey hair, and she received them in an office built into the curve of a standing tank, at a desk with three ledgers open on it, and she was, Toren decided within about ninety seconds, the most reasonable person he had ever met.

That turned out to be the problem.

She wrote it all down. That was the first thing she did, before she offered them anything, before she asked Rook a single question about himself: she took a fresh page and she wrote down Coldwater and the date and the milepost, and she wrote down Odo Prentice, guide, twenty-two crossings, and then Nell Tanner and Ilsa Cole and Garrick Voss, in that order, with their ages where anybody knew them.

She asked Dessa how to spell Cole. Dessa spelled it.

"That's in the ledger now," Braid said, "and the ledger goes to Meridian twice a year and it doesn't get lost. Whatever else happens today, that's done and it's permanent. I want you to have that."

Dessa nodded and could not speak.

"Right." Braid put her glasses down. "Now the unpleasant half."



"I can't take the old woman."

"She's got a splinted leg and eight days of road left in her," Rook said.

"She's got twenty years of reactor sickness and a leg, and I'd take the leg." The warden said it without any cruelty at all, which made it much harder to argue with. "I've got eleven beds and I've got the loop road, and the loop road gives me road-injured all year and about forty of them a season, and my charter says I hold a bed for the next one. A chronic case takes a bed and doesn't give it back. If I put her in one, I'm gambling somebody I haven't met yet against somebody I have, and I'm not allowed to do that. It's not that I don't want to. It's that I signed something."

"Then read me the part where it says you can't."

"I'll get it out for you if you like," said Braid, "but you already know I can, or you wouldn't have asked me that way."

Rook was quiet for a moment.

"The four," he said. "Take the four."

"The four I can do." Braid pulled the second ledger toward her. "Bond intake. Seven years, board and keep, and at the end of it they're free with a trade and a letter. That's the standard and I don't negotiate it because the moment I negotiate it once I'll be doing it for everybody." She began ruling a line down the page. "Two Kindled with Embers go on the wall rotation — that's not punishment, that's where the value is, and it's where I can actually keep an eye on them. The rift-touched girl goes to the infirmary; if she can close a cut she's worth more there than anywhere else in this station. The boy with the light —"

"Milo," said Milo.

"— goes to the kitchen and the stores, and don't make a face at me, young man, I started in a kitchen." She kept ruling the line. "They'd be split across three rosters. They'd see each other at meals. I'm telling you that now rather than letting you find it out in a month, because I've had that conversation before and it goes better this way."

"Seven years," said Wyck.

"Seven years, and you'd be alive at the end of them," said Warden Braid, "which is the honest comparison, and I'd rather make the honest one." She looked up. "Understand me. I'm not doing you a kindness. I'm buying seven years of labor off four orphans at a price I set, and I sleep at night because the alternative on this road is worse and because I keep my end. But I'm not going to stand here and pretend it's charity, and if anybody wants to tell me I'm a hard woman I'll agree with them and we can get on."

She turned the ledger around and pushed it across the desk, and set the pen on top of it, and looked at Rook.

"You've got a claim on them or you haven't," she said. "If you have, sign, and I'll take them today. If you haven't, they're vagrant minors on my road and I'll take them anyway in about four days when the food runs out, and it'll be exactly the same page and exactly the same seven years, except that you'll have made them walk to it."

The office was very quiet.

Toren looked at the ledger. He looked at his grandmother, propped in a chair by the door with her splinted leg out and her hands folded, watching all of this with an expression he could not read at all. He looked at Dessa's face, and at Milo's, and at Wyck's, and Wyck was staring at the desk with his jaw set and something in his eyes that was not fear.

He looked at Rook.

Rook had picked up the pen. He was holding it the way you hold a tool you have used ten thousand times, loosely, between two fingers, and he was looking at the ruled line, and his hand was perfectly steady, and he did not move.

Ten seconds. Toren counted them.

"No," Rook said.

He put the pen down on the desk, squarely, alongside the ledger.

"That's a decision," said Braid.

"It is."

"You want to think it over? I'll leave the page open till the morning."

"Leave it open," Rook said. "I'm not signing it in the morning either."



Outside, in the yard between the second and third tanks, with the light going and the whole station smelling of woodsmoke and rendered fat, Rook stood with his back to the four of them for a long time.

Then he said: "Get in a line."

Nobody moved.

"You heard the woman," he said. "Four days, and then she takes you, and she'll be right to. Four days is what I've bought you and it cost me the only clean way out of this that I had, so you're going to spend all four of them doing what I say. Get in a line."

They got in a line.

"Tanner. Ember out."

Wyck lit it. Three feet of white, clean, steady, and he held it, and Toren watched him hold it — and Wyck got past forty and kept going, jaw locked, and got to somewhere around fifty-five before the light guttered and he shook his hand out.

"That's good," said Rook.

Wyck's head came up.

"I mean it. That's two years of somebody making himself do a boring thing every day when nobody was watching, and that's most of what this is. Don't get proud about it. Get proud about it later." Rook was already moving down the line. "Cole. Thirty feet?"

"About."

"Through a wall?"

Dessa blinked. "I've — never tried it through a wall."

"Try it through a wall tonight and tell me tomorrow. Faust."

Milo held up his hand and made the light. It sat in his palm, pale, about the size of a plum, entirely useless.

Rook looked at it for a while.

"How long?"

"Uh — as long as I want, really. It's not hard. It's just—"

"How long."

"An hour? More. I fell asleep with it on once."

"Huh," said Rook, and did not say anything else, and moved on, and Milo stood there with his mouth slightly open, holding a light nobody had ever said huh about before.

"Voss."

Toren got the Ember out and lit it, and it lit, and it stayed a rod.

"Edge."

"I can't always—"

"I didn't ask what you can always. Edge."

Toren thought about a blade with everything he had, and got nothing, and felt his face going hot, and stopped thinking about a blade and let the thing roll loose in his fingers and thought please, and got four inches of white light for about two seconds.

"There," he said, breathing hard, and heard how it sounded, and hated it.

"Again."

He did it again. Three inches. Then nothing, four times running. Then five inches, twice, and then nothing again for so long that his hands started to cramp.

"It only does it sometimes," he said at last.

"Does it."

"It — yes. It does it when it wants to. On the road it did the same thing, it wouldn't do anything when I needed it and then it just —" Toren heard himself and pushed on anyway, because it was the only explanation that fit and he had been building it for three days. "It decided. That's what it feels like. Like it's got its own opinion and sometimes you're lucky."

He waited for Rook to correct him.

He was certain, standing there in the yard with a dead rod in his hand, that this was the moment where the man was going to tell him the real thing — that there was a trick to it, a grip, a word, a way of holding your breath, the actual answer that every Ember-user knew and that nobody had gotten around to telling Toren Voss because his father had died fourteen hours after handing him the thing.

Rook looked at him for a long moment in the failing light.

"Fine," he said. "Again."



They drilled until it was full dark and then they drilled in the dark.

Not fighting. There was almost nothing in it that Toren would have called fighting. Rook walked them up and down the yard between the tanks in a loose diamond — Wyck at the front, Dessa at the left, Toren at the right, Milo in the middle where the light was — and made them stop, and turn, and change which way the diamond faced, over and over, until Milo was staggering.

"Again."

"We've done it eleven times—"

"Then it'll be easy." Rook stood in the middle of the yard with his arms folded. "You four walked down a road three days ago in a clump with a cart in the middle and a man out front who was thinking about fords. When it started, every one of you ran a different direction and two of you ran uphill into open grass, and one of you lit up a beacon on a bare slope with nothing behind him but sky." He let that sit exactly long enough. "Nobody's blaming anybody. I'm telling you what I saw so you know what we're fixing. Again."

They went again.

At some point past midnight, with the four of them sitting against the curve of a cold tank and Milo actually asleep, Toren said, "Why do they come at the light?"

Rook was on the other side of the yard, doing something to his boot.

"They come at worked power," he said. "Ember, magic, doesn't matter which. Anything anybody's doing on purpose. So the rule is: don't light unless you mean it, and if you light, be standing somewhere you chose to be standing."

"Why?"

"Because that's the rule."

"No — why do they come at it?"

Rook finished with his boot and put it back on.

"Go to sleep," he said.

Toren lay awake for a long time with his back against seventy-year-old steel and the disk warm in his coat pocket, and thought about a pen set down squarely alongside a ledger, and about a piece of bread on a rock in the rain, and about the fact that nobody in the yard that night had said out loud what they were drilling for, and that all five of them knew, and that not one of them was going to be the one to say it.



Chapter 6: What He Saw

On the third morning Warden Braid put a mug of something hot into Rook's hand and mentioned, in the manner of a woman mentioning the weather, that she had lost two people at the quarry pumphouse since the spring.

"Water's fine. The water's the only reason this station exists. But there's something living in the pump shed and it's cost me a fitter and a boy, and I can spare four men to go and look at it, and if I send four men I get four men back or I don't, and either way I've got no fitter." She blew across her own mug. "It's half a mile. You could see it from the berm if the fog lifted."

"You're not asking me."

"I'm certainly not asking you," said Braid. "The page is still open on my desk. I mention the pumphouse the way I'd mention there's a hole in the road."

Rook drank his drink and looked out at the fog and did not answer her, and an hour later he told the four of them to eat properly and get their boots on.



The quarry was a grey oval bitten out of the hillside with sixty feet of flat green water standing in the bottom of it, and the pumphouse sat on the lip: a squat concrete box the size of a barn, roof half fallen in, with a sluice channel running out of its lower wall and away down the slope in a straight cut lined with stone.

"Diamond," Rook said. "In through the upper door, sweep left, out through the lower. Voss at point."

Toren's stomach dropped about a foot.

"Point," he said.

"Point."

"Why him?"

It came out of Wyck flat and fast, and everybody heard exactly how long it had been sitting behind his teeth.

Rook turned around.

"Say the rest of it," he said.

"There's nothing to—" Wyck stopped. He was fifteen, and he had been up before all of them every morning for three days doing the boring thing where nobody was watching, and Toren could see him deciding, in real time, that he was going to say it. "I've had an Ember two years. I can hold a full Edge for the better part of a minute. He can hold four inches when it feels like being nice to him. If you want somebody at point who can actually cut something, that's me, and you know it's me, because you said so yourself on the first night."

"I did."

"Then—"

"You're on left," Rook said. "Cole, right. Faust, centre, and don't light until somebody tells you. Voss, point."

Wyck stood there with his jaw working.

"Is there a reason," he said, "or is that just how it is."

"That's just how it is," said Rook.

He said it perfectly evenly and he did not offer a single word more, and Toren stood at the front of the diamond with his father's Ember in his fist and his face burning, because he did not have the reason either, and would have given a great deal for it, and knew exactly how it looked.



They went in at a little before ten in the morning.

The upper door was a rectangle of dark in a wall streaked with seventy years of rust-run. Inside, the floor went down in three broad steps toward the sluice wall, and the bottom step and everything past it was under water — shin-deep at the near edge, knee-deep at the far, still and green-black and absolutely opaque. Two rows of pump housings stood up out of it like the stumps of teeth. Overhead, the collapsed section of roof let in a hard white slab of daylight that fell across the middle of the room and made everything outside it worse.

The far wall had the lower door in it, and beside the lower door the sluice channel came out through a gap the height of a man, with a rusted catwalk running over the top of it.

There was a click in the dark on the left.

Then, unhurried, three more, from three different places.

"Seven," said Dessa. Her voice was very tight and very controlled. "Maybe eight. They're spread all around the low end."

"Spread how?"

"Even. Like they've been waiting."

"They have," Rook said, from somewhere behind Toren's right shoulder, and did not move up.

Toren looked back once.

The man was standing three steps up in the doorway with his arms folded and his Ember still in his coat, and the look on his face was not encouragement and it was not indifference either. It was attention. It was the flat total attention of somebody watching a thing that has already started and cannot now be stopped, and Toren understood, with a cold lurch, that they were going to do this themselves.

"Down the steps," Rook said. "Diamond. Go."



Toren had thought about the water. He had not thought about it hard enough.

He went down the three broad steps and into it and the water took his legs the way deep sand does, and every stride became a thing he had to plan, and the noise of it — the great sloshing racket four people make wading — filled the room and made hearing anything else impossible.

The first one came out of the dark on the left at the height of the second pump housing.

It did not wade. That was the entire lesson and it arrived in about a second and a half. It came across the flooded floor in that long unhurried fold-and-unfold, all its extra joints working, and the water barely marked its passage, and it crossed twenty feet while Toren was crossing six.

Wyck lit up and took it across the throat-joint before it reached the diamond at all, and it went to ash, and warm grey came down across the black water and floated.

"Left," Dessa said. "Two more left — Wyck, two—"

"I see them."

Toren got his own Ember lit and it stayed a rod, and he thought about a blade so hard his ears rang, and it stayed a rod, and something came at his right side out from behind a pump housing.

He did the loose thing.

He had been doing the loose thing for three days on Rook's orders, over and over in the yard, poorly. He rolled the shaft in his fingers, and stopped picturing anything at all, and thought please — and got a foot of white light, and it stayed.

It stayed. He put it through the thing's foreleg fold and the creature came apart and the light was still there, burning steady out of the end of the shaft, twelve inches of it, and he had time to see it and to feel the enormous stupid joy of it go through him from his heels up.

Eight seconds. Longer than he'd ever held it before.

Then it guttered and went out and he nearly fell over.

"Toren!"

Dessa. Right side of the diamond, and the right side of the diamond had gone, because Toren had walked forward out of it in the eight seconds he had spent being delighted with himself, and Dessa was eleven feet behind him and to the right with two of them between her and the group, backed up against a pump housing in water to the knee with no Ember and nothing in her hands but a bill-hook.

"Hold on—"

He could not get to her. He worked that out instantly. Eleven feet of knee-deep water was four seconds and four seconds was too many, and he had one thing and one thing only, and he had known what it was since the road.

They come at the light. The biggest light.

He had watched it happen. He had watched an entire hillside of them turn as one toward three feet of white in Wyck Tanner's fist and go straight past Milo Faust standing in the open with a palm-light. He had watched every living thing on that roadbed swing toward a man in a grey coat the instant he lit up seven feet of spear. Twice. He had seen it twice with his own eyes and it was the only piece of the world he was certain of.

So Toren Voss planted his feet in the water in the middle of the white slab of daylight, and did the loose thing, and gave it everything he had.

The Ember put out light.

Not four inches. Not a foot. Two feet of it, hot white, brighter than anything in the room, and it hurt his hand and his forearm cramped instantly and he held it up over his head like a man holding a lantern in a storm and he roared at them.

And every head in the pumphouse came round.

He saw it. He saw it happen exactly the way he had known it would — six or seven long grey heads turning toward him across the black water, unhurried and simultaneous, the seams down their chests all brightening at once as if a wind had gone through a row of coals.

For about two seconds nothing in that room moved except him.

It was the two seconds Dessa needed. She got off the pump housing and behind it and around, and the pair that had been closing on her lost the line and had to come around the housing the long way, and by then Wyck had pushed right and Milo had shoved himself in front of her, and the right side of the diamond stitched itself back together out of nothing.

It worked. It worked, and Toren felt it work, and in the wash of it he did not notice that not one single Riftspawn in that building had actually come toward him.

They had all looked.

Then every one of them had gone for whatever was closest.



Wyck was closest to the sluice gap.

He was on the left, where Rook had put him, and he had pushed right to cover Dessa the way Toren's plan had needed somebody to, and that had put him with his back to the wall between the lower door and the sluice channel, in a corner about four feet wide, with a stone-lined drop at his heels and nowhere at all to give ground. There were two of them in front of him and one of them had come out of the sluice gap behind, and Wyck's Edge was lit, three clean feet of it, and Wyck Tanner was the nearest worked light in that corner of the room by a distance of about six feet.

Toren was thirty feet away with a burning arm and a lantern held over his head.

He got there in nine seconds. He would take those nine seconds apart, over and over, for a very long time.

By the time he got there, Rook had crossed the whole room.

Toren did not see him come off the steps. He saw the end of it: a hard blue-white frame burning around a man's legs, and a spear-point at half length coming down the wall line, and the thing at the sluice gap going to ash and the drift of it blowing out through the gap and down the channel; and then the two in front of Wyck, one and then the other, in less time than it takes to describe them.

And then Wyck was sitting down in the water with his back to the wall and his Ember gone dark in his hand and his left leg out in front of him at a bad angle, and there was a great deal of red in the black water and it was moving away from him in a slow spreading fan.



Dessa got both hands on it and got nothing.

"I can't — it's too big, I can only do—" She was working. Toren could see her working, her whole face straining with it, and nothing was happening. "It's too big, I can close a cut, this isn't a cut—"

"Move your hands." Rook was already down in the water beside her. "Faust. Light. Right here, right where I'm looking, and hold it."

Milo put his hand out and made the little useless plum of pale light and held it eight inches over Wyck's leg, and it did not waver at all, and he held it there without moving for the next eleven minutes.

Rook took the Ember out of his coat, rolled it once against his palm, and laid it flat along the length of the wound with both hands over it.
