He did not put a hand in any ash. There was ash on the water all around them, a slick grey film of it going soft and coming apart, and he did not touch it. Toren — kneeling in the water with his own arm still cramping and his heart going like a hammer — noticed that and filed it, the way he had filed he doesn't have the Ember on the road, in the place where things go that you cannot use yet.

At the end of it, Wyck's leg had stopped bleeding and was closed along most of its length and was still, unmistakably, a leg that had been opened from above the knee to the hip.

"That's the ends together," Rook said. His hands had started shaking about four minutes in and had not stopped. "The rest is you. Six weeks. You'll walk on it in three if you're an idiot and in five if you're not, and it will hurt every single day, and there is nothing else and nobody has anything else. Do you understand me?"

Wyck's face was grey. "Yeah."

"Say it back."

"Six weeks. Nothing else. Nobody has anything else."

"Good."



They did the debrief in the lower doorway, because Rook would not do it inside and would not do it walking.

He sat Wyck against the frame with the leg out straight, and put the other three in a row where Wyck could see all of them, and he said:

"Voss. Tell me what you did."

"I made myself the biggest light in the room," Toren said. His voice came out wrong. "To pull them off Dessa."

"Did it pull them off Dessa?"

"...It bought her two seconds."

"It did. That's real and I'm not taking it off you." Rook's face did not change. "Now tell me why it only bought two."

Toren opened his mouth and had absolutely nothing in it.

"They looked," Rook said. "Every one of them looked at you. You saw them look and you thought that meant they were coming, because on the road you saw them look and then you saw them come, and you drew a line between the two. That's a fair line. I'd have drawn it at thirteen." He leaned forward slightly. "They don't come at the biggest. They come at the nearest. Looking is free. Moving isn't. On the road, Tanner's blade was the nearest worked thing on that hillside, and I was the nearest worked thing on that road because I came straight up the middle of them. You got both of those right and you got the reason wrong, and the reason is the only part that transfers."

Nobody said anything.

"So." Rook sat back. "You lit up the middle of a room and gave every one of them a free look, and while they were looking, each one of them went for whoever was standing six feet from it. Cole was covered by then. Faust was behind Cole. You were thirty feet from anything. And Tanner was in a four-foot corner with a drop behind him and a lit Ember in his hand, and he was there because your plan needed somebody to be, and nobody went to him, because you'd sent yourself to the middle of the room instead."

Toren could not look at anyone.

"That's the lesson and it cost a leg," Rook said. "That's the price and it's already paid, so use it. Nearest, not biggest. Say it."

"Nearest, not biggest."

"All four of you."

They all said it.

"Why didn't you tell us?" Milo said. It was not a challenge. It was almost a plea. "You knew. You could have just said it on the first night—"

"I could have."

"Then—"

"Because you'd have known it," Rook said, "and knowing it is worth nothing." He got up, slowly, in the way of a man whose hands are still shaking. "You'll never make that mistake again as long as you live. Every one of you just got that for the rest of your lives and none of you paid for it except him." He looked down at Wyck. "That's the arithmetic and it's ugly and I'd do it again, and you're allowed to hate me for it."

"I don't hate you," said Wyck.

He was looking at Toren.

He said it in an absolutely reasonable voice, from the ground, with his leg out straight in front of him, and he did not raise it at all.

"You made a call," he said. "Nobody gave you the call. He put you at point and you took it like it was yours and you made a call and it went through my leg. And tomorrow he's going to put you at point again, isn't he. And you know what the worst part is? You're going to let him."

Toren said, "Wyck—"

"Don't," said Wyck Tanner, and shut his eyes, and that was the end of that for the day and not remotely the end of it.



Toren noticed the disk was gone at the top of the slope.

He put his hand in his pocket the way he had put his hand in that pocket four hundred times in six days, and the pocket had a corner torn out of it, and there was nothing in it, and the world tipped.

He was back down the hill and into the water before anyone could say his name.

He found the pocket-corner. He did not find the disk. He was on his hands and knees in the black water going along the base of the sluice wall with both arms in to the shoulder, sobbing in a hard ugly way he could not stop and had no attention to spare for, and he had been at it for some minutes before he registered that there was somebody in the water beside him.

Rook came up out of the sluice channel with it in his hand.

He was crouched at the head of the cut with the water running past his wrists and the disk flat on his open palm, and he did not hand it over.

Toren stopped.

He was afterward never able to say what it was. Rook's face did not do anything. That was the point; that was the whole thing; the man knelt there in a foot of moving water with a small dark object on his hand and his expression did not alter by any amount that Toren could have described to another person — and every single thing about him was different. His shoulders. The set of his neck. The way he was breathing, which he had stopped doing.

He turned it over once with his thumb, and the nine channels came around into the light, and the light went down them.

His hands began to shake.

They had been steady all the way through the pumphouse until four minutes into Wyck's leg. They had been steady coming up the hill. They started now, holding a warm dark disk in a quarry sluice with nothing in front of him at all, and they got worse for about five seconds and then Rook noticed Toren watching and put a stop to it in the way you stop a shiver, by force.

"Where did you get this," he said.

"My father."

"And him?"

"His father." Toren's own voice sounded a long way off. "Five generations. It's a Voss thing. There's a story that goes with it."

"I'll bet there is," said Rook.

He held it a moment longer.

Then he reached out and put it into Toren's hands and closed both of the boy's fists over it, one finger at a time, with a care that Toren had felt exactly once before in his life, four nights ago, in wet grass under a broken overpass.

"Sew your pocket," Rook said. "Tonight. Two rows of stitches, not one. And don't show that to another living soul on this road, or at Tallow Gate, or anywhere else, and don't tell anyone you have it, and if somebody's already seen it you tell me who."

"Why?"

"Sew your pocket."

"You're not going to tell me anything, are you," Toren said. "Not what it is, not why you looked at it like that, not why you put me at point when Wyck's better than me. Not one thing."

Rook stood up out of the water.

He looked down the sluice channel toward the quarry and the flat green water and the fog that had never once lifted all day, and he was quiet for long enough that Toren thought that was going to be the whole answer.

"We're not going back to that ledger," Rook said at last. "Tell the others. We leave at first light and we're not going east, and your grandmother's coming, and everything gets harder starting tomorrow — the drilling, the hours, all of it. You'll wish it was today by the end of the week."

"Because of the disk."

Rook did not answer that.

He went up the slope in the fog with the water running off his coat, and Toren Voss stood in the sluice with both fists closed around a thing that felt like a room he had lived in, and understood that he had just been given the answer to a question and no part at all of what it meant.



Chapter 7: The Grade

Warden Braid came out to the tank tunnel at first light to weigh Rook's ash, and she did it herself, on her own scale, in front of everybody, which Toren understood later was the point.

She had the balance set up on a barrel head just inside the doors, out of the wind. Rook set the jars on the table one after another and she unstoppered each one and looked into it before she weighed it, tipping the mouth toward the lamp so the light could get down into the grey, and the grey moved.

"Flask," she said. "Flask. Flask. That one's short."

"That one's a Handful and a bit of a second one I didn't get all of."

"Then it's a Handful and I'm writing a Handful, and you can be annoyed about it." She made a mark. "How many?"

"Eight in the shed. I drew five."

"Where'd the other three go?"

"Into the floor of a flooded pump house," said Rook, "at a rate I couldn't keep up with, while I had both hands inside a boy's leg."

Braid grunted and went on weighing.

Toren stood against the curve of the tunnel wall with his hands in his pockets and listened to two adults trade in a currency he had known about his whole life and never once seen anybody actually use. In Coldwater the ash came in on somebody else's cart twice a year, in sealed grey jars that went straight to the smith and the wound-woman, and nobody ever said where it had come from beyond the hunters. He had assumed, in the vague way you assume things you are not required to think about, that the hunters had some method.

The method, it turned out, was a man putting his palm flat on the ground in the dark and drawing it up before it got away.

"Four and a Handful," Braid said. "I'll give you the barrow, the canvas, meal for eleven days, and the rest in salt. Don't tell me that's low. It is low, and it's low because I've got exactly one buyer for ash within sixty miles and she's standing here, and I'd rather say that out loud than have you find it out in Meridian and think I cheated you."

"Take the salt back and give me a second axe."

"Done."



The barrow was a two-wheeled hauling cart with iron-shod rims and a pair of long shafts, the sort of thing a man stands between and pulls, and it had come off the quarry line and was twenty years old and had been kept.

They put Senna in it on a folded canvas with her splinted leg straight out along the boards. They put the bundles in around her. They put Wyck in it too, and that took some doing.

"I can walk."

"You can walk in three weeks if you're an idiot," Rook said, not looking up from the strap he was working, "and five if you're not. It's been four days. Get in the cart."

"I'll slow us down. I'll walk at the back and I'll—"

"Tanner." Rook pulled the strap tight. "There is one road out of here that your leg is going to survive, and it's a graded one, and I'm not putting you on it with a stick out of pride for something you did fine. Get in the cart. You'll walk this afternoon when the ground's good and you'll be in it again by dark and that's how it's going to go for a while."

Wyck got in the cart.

He sat with his back against the rear board and his bad leg out and his face doing nothing at all, and Senna Voss, from the other end, with her own leg out in exactly the same attitude, looked him over for about ten seconds.

"You're going to hate this," she said.

"I don't hate it."

"You're going to hate it by noon and you're going to be very polite about hating it, which is worse." She resettled her shoulders against the canvas. "I've been in a box since the fifth of the month. You'll want to pace yourself."

Wyck did not answer that, but Toren, taking up the near shaft to help push the thing out through eighty feet of fallen fuel tank, saw the boy's mouth move very slightly at one corner, once, and then stop.



Warden Ottoline Braid walked them to the outside of the doors and no further.

"The page is closed," she said. "I want you to hear the exact shape of that. It's closed, it isn't torn out. If any one of you comes back through this gate in a year, or five, and wants it opened, I'll open it and I'll honor what's on it. That's not me being kind. That's me telling you the terms don't expire, so that none of you spends a winter somewhere worse thinking you burned your bridge here at thirteen."

"Not all of us," said Milo. "Wyck's fifteen."

"Congratulations to him too, then." She put her glasses up into her hair. "Go on, then. You're losing the light you haven't got yet."



They went west along the loop road for four hours and then Rook turned off it.

There was no turning. That was the thing Toren kept looking back at: no fork, no marker, not so much as a rut. Rook simply walked off the crown of the road at a place that looked like every other place, down through the wet grass of the shoulder, and out across a flat of thistle and old broken concrete toward a long low bank running away north-west, and after a moment the rest of them followed him because there was nothing else to do.

The bank was a railway.

It took Toren most of a mile to be sure, because there were no rails and no ties and hardly any stone left proud of the grass, and because he had never seen one. But the shape of it was unarguable once you were up on top: a raised bed thirty feet wide running dead straight across country, holding its level where the land fell away and cutting down into the land where the land rose, so that it went through a low ridge in a trench with rock walls instead of going over the top. Somebody had spent an unimaginable amount of somebody else's labor on making one line of ground refuse to go up or down.

"They took the iron off it," Rook said, when Milo asked. "Everybody did, everywhere, in the first ten years. There's not a mile of rail left standing between here and the coast."

"Then what's it for?"

"It's flat," said Rook, in the traces of the cart, "and it's straight, and it doesn't have a town on it."

That last was the answer and Toren knew it was the answer. He walked along the top of a seventy-year-old railway with the whole country opening out grey-green on both sides of him, no fence, no roof, nothing at all between here and the hills, and he understood that they had just stopped being on a road.



Rook drilled them at midday, and it was not like the yard at Tallow Gate.

In the yard it had been the diamond, over and over, turning and stopping, a thing you could do poorly and be shouted at about. Out here he split them up.

"Cole. Fifty paces ahead, on the bank, and I want you holding it — not checking it, holding it — until I call you. Faust, with her, no light. Voss, back down the grade two hundred, sit on the top of the cut where you can see both ways, and if you see anything you don't understand you don't shout, you come and tell me." He set the shafts down. "Tanner. Out of the cart."

"You said—"

"I said you'd walk this afternoon. It's afternoon." Rook pulled a bundle off the cart and dropped it on the grass. "You're going to stand on that leg for as long as you can stand on it and hold a full Edge while you do it, and when the leg goes you're going to sit down and keep holding it, and when the Edge goes we'll see which one of them gave out first. I want to know."

Wyck looked at him.

"Why?"

"Because I don't know," Rook said, "and I'd rather find out on an empty grade at noon than somewhere it matters."



Toren sat on the lip of the cutting two hundred paces back with his knees up and the country going away in both directions and nothing at all happening in it, and discovered that this was the hardest thing anyone had asked him to do since the road.

There was nothing to look at. That was the trouble. There was grass and there was wind in the grass and there was a hawk working the far side of the ridge in long slow passes. After a while his eyes started sliding off all of it and going back down the grade to where he could see the others as four dark marks against the sky, and he had to haul them back and start again. Twice he caught himself thinking about his father's hands and had to stop.

He was still up there when the sun came off the vertical and Rook whistled him in.

He came down the grade at a jog and found Wyck sitting on the bundle in the grass with sweat running off his jaw and three feet of white light standing steady out of his fist, and Rook crouched on his heels eight feet away watching it the way a man watches a pot.

"How long," Toren said.

"Don't talk to him."

The light held. It held while Toren stood there, and it held while Dessa and Milo came back down from the fifty-pace mark, and it held while Rook shifted his weight from one heel to the other, and then it went out all at once the way a candle goes out, and Wyck sagged forward over his own knees and swore once, quietly, at the ground.

"Five minutes and change," Rook said. "Leg went at two."

"It didn't go, it just—"

"It went at two and you held the light for three more minutes on a leg that had gone, which is the part I wanted." Rook stood up. "That's good."

"You said that on the first night."

"It's still true." Rook picked up the near shaft of the cart. "You're Flame, Tanner. You've been Flame about a year. Nobody told you because the man who'd have told you was your aunt's brother-in-law and he made barrels."

Wyck's head came up.

Toren, watching, saw the whole thing arrive in the boy's face — saw it land, and saw what it landed on, and saw Wyck decide in about a second and a half to be furious about it instead of pleased.

"What's Flame."

"A rung."

"That's not—"

"Cole's Ember," Rook said, in exactly the same voice, going down the line as if he were naming the weather. "Voss is Ember and barely. Faust I haven't got a word for yet and I've been looking for four days."

"Hey," said Milo, obscurely delighted.

"What are you," said Wyck.

Rook put the shafts on his shoulders and leaned into them and the cart came up out of the grass, and he was twenty feet down the grade before he answered, and he did not turn around to do it.

"Further up," he said.



It was Senna who gave them the rest of it, that night, at a fire built too small in the bottom of the cut where the rock walls took the light.

Milo had been at it for an hour. He had been at it in the way Milo Faust got at things, from six directions, cheerfully, without any apparent awareness that he was being refused, and Rook had answered exactly none of it and had gone off up the bank to see to the watch line.

"Oh, stop," Senna said at last. "Boy. Come here."

Milo came and sat by the cart wheel.

"Kindled," she said. "Ember. Flame. Fire. Torch. Blaze. And Glory, which nobody has ever met and which they only put on the end so the thing would finish properly."

Milo said, "That's it?"

"That's it. They teach it to children as a song in the west — my mother had it, and her mother, and it's a stupid song." She looked into the fire. "Kindled at the door and Ember in the hand. Flame to hold and Fire to hold longer. Torch to stand the night, and Blaze to—" She stopped. "I don't remember. It doesn't matter. It's a song for making children sit still."

"What's Torch?"

"Torch is as far as anyone gets," Senna said.

Dessa, who had been lying on her back on the far side of the fire with her forearm across her eyes, said, "That's not true, though. He's further up. He said so."

"I said as far as anyone gets." Senna resettled her hands in her lap. "My husband was Ember his whole life. Forty years with a light in his hand you could read by and never once anything you could cut with, and he went out at fifty-four doing something that wanted a man who could cut." She was quiet a moment. "He knew every rung of that ladder by name and it never bought him one inch of the next one. So I'd be careful what you do with the words, all of you. They're a way of talking about people. They're not a way of being one."

Nobody said anything for a while.

Up on the bank, out beyond the firelight, Rook was walking the line of the cut with his hands in his pockets, stopping now and then, going on.



Dessa found Toren at the water, later, filling the cans.

"I did the wall thing," she said.

"What wall thing?"

"At Tallow Gate. He told me on the first night — try it through a wall and tell me tomorrow." She crouched on the stones with her arms around her shins. "I didn't tell him tomorrow. I told him this morning, in the tunnel, while she was weighing the jars."

"Does it work through a wall?"

"It works through a wall. It works through two walls and a tank. It doesn't care about walls at all, and I've had this thing for a year and a half and I never tried it once because when they gave it to me in Coldwater they said she can feel where people are, and I decided that meant line of sight, and then I stopped thinking." She dug a stone out of the gravel and turned it over. "He didn't even look pleased. He said, all right, that's the wall. Now hold it."

"Hold it how long?"

"That's what I said." Dessa threw the stone into the black water. "He said, until it's boring."

They carried the cans back up the grade together. Behind them, the cut went away south-east in a straight black line toward the country they had come out of, and ahead of them it went away north-west toward country nobody had told them the name of, and Toren realized, somewhere in the middle of that walk, that in four days of drilling at Tallow Gate he had believed, without ever once checking, that they were being taught how to stay alive on a road.

Nobody was teaching them a road. There was no road.

They were being taught something, and it had a shape, and the shape had rungs on it.



Chapter 8: What Dessa Held

On the second morning on the grade, Rook stopped walking an hour after they started, put the cart shafts down in the grass, and said, "Cole. You're not marching today."

"I'm fine to march."

"I didn't say you weren't." He got a canvas off the cart and threw it down. "Sit there. Faust, you're on the shafts with Voss. Tanner, back of the cart, eyes behind. We're going to go about a mile an hour today and you're going to hate every part of that except the part where nobody's dead at the end of it."

Dessa sat on the canvas in the grass with her boots off and her hands loose on her knees, and they went on around her, and she held it.

Toren did not understand at first what he was watching, because there was nothing to watch. A girl sat down in the grass. The girl's face did what a face does when someone is doing arithmetic they are not good at. That was the whole of it, and it went on for a very long time.

He took a turn on the shafts and gave it back to Milo and took it again, and the cut walls fell away on both sides and the grade came out into an open country of low hills with standing water in the bottoms of them. Behind him, a girl sat in the grass with her eyes open and her hands on her knees, moving perhaps once in a hundred paces.

At something past eleven in the morning, she stood up, walked six steps to the edge of the bank, and was sick into the grass.



"Four minutes," Rook said.

"That's nothing."

"It's four minutes." He gave her the water can. "What did it feel like at the end?"

"Like—" Dessa wiped her mouth with the back of her wrist. Her nose had begun to bleed, unhurriedly, and she blotted it on her cuff without seeming to notice that she was doing it. "Like holding a full bucket at arm's length. Not heavy. Just — it stops being about the bucket. Your arm starts making the decision without you."

"Good. That's exactly the right complaint."

"It isn't a complaint—"

"It's a complaint and it's the correct one and I've been waiting three days to hear it." Rook crouched down in front of her. "Listen. You've had this a year and a half and every single thing you know about how to use it, you worked out by yourself at fourteen with nobody to ask. That's not an insult, that's a fact about your circumstances, and it means some of what you worked out is wrong and you've never had any way to find out which parts."

"Which parts."

"You throw it," Rook said. "You've been throwing it and catching it. Every fifty steps on that road with Prentice — throw, catch, throw, catch, and a headache by noon, and you told the Voss boy that and you said it like it was the weather." He took the can back off her. "You never once put it down and stood in it."

Dessa looked at him.

"That's the same thing."

"It isn't. It's the opposite thing, and you're going to be sick four more times before your body agrees with me." He straightened up. "Go again."

"Now?"

"When the bleeding stops."



She quit at about three in the afternoon.

That surprised Toren more than anything else in the day. He had been on the shafts for the last mile with his shoulders burning and Milo chattering beside him about nothing whatsoever, and he looked back and Dessa was standing in the middle of the grade with her arms down at her sides, not walking, and when Rook came back along the line she said, in a voice that was almost conversational:

"No. I'm done. I'm not doing another one."

Toren waited for it. He had been at Tallow Gate for the pen set down squarely alongside a ledger; he had been in the pumphouse doorway for that's the arithmetic and it's ugly; he knew by now more or less what the man sounded like when somebody told him no.

Rook said, "All right."

"I'm not being — I'm not sulking, I just—"

"I heard you." He put the shafts down. "We'll stop here. Faust, get the stove out. Voss, water."

And that was all, and he walked away up the bank, and Dessa Cole stood in the middle of the grade with her fists closed and her mouth open and absolutely nothing to do with any of it.

Toren got the cans and went down to the standing water at the bottom of the hollow and Dessa came with him, which she had not been asked to do.

"He's doing something," she said.

"He's letting you stop."

"He's doing something." She kicked a stone off the bank. "He had four days at Tallow Gate to tell me what I'd been doing wrong and he didn't say a word, and then the second we're out here where there's nobody, he — and now I say no and he says all right, like it doesn't cost him anything, like he'd worked out I was going to say it—"

"Maybe he had."

"Then that's worse!"

Toren crouched at the water's edge and let the can fill and did not say what he was actually thinking, which was small and mean and which he had been carrying since eleven in the morning.

What he was actually thinking was: he spent the whole day on you.



He drilled that evening and Rook watched him for about six minutes.

Four inches. Nothing. Five inches. Nothing four times running. A foot, once, for the better part of two seconds, and Toren's whole chest came up off the ground with hope — and then nothing again, eleven times, until his hand cramped shut and he had to work the fingers open with the other hand.

"Right," Rook said, and went to see to the cart.

That was the whole of it. No again. Not even fine.

Toren stood in the grass in the dark with a dead rod in his fist and understood, with a clarity that made his eyes sting, that he had just been given six minutes out of a day, and that the six minutes had been generous.



Rook put Dessa on the first watch at nine at night and told her to hold it the whole way through.

"That's three hours."

"It's three hours."

"I did four minutes this morning and I—"

"You did four minutes of the hard thing this morning," Rook said. He was sitting on the cart's tailboard with a boot off, working at something in the sole of it with a knife. "Sitting in a field with nothing in it, at noon, with all of us in front of you where you knew exactly where we were. That's the hardest version there is. Doing it on a watch is the easy version and you're going to find out why."

"Why?"

"Because there's a reason to."



Toren woke at some point in the night because Milo, beside him, had rolled over and taken the blanket.

The fire was down to a red eye in the bottom of the pit. The cart was a black shape against the sky with Senna's breathing going in and out of it. And out on the edge of the bank, thirty feet off, Dessa Cole was sitting on the canvas with her boots on and her arms around her knees, looking at nothing, absolutely motionless.

He watched her for a while.

There was nothing to see. That was the fourth time that day he had thought it, and this time something in it turned over. She was not doing anything. She had been sitting there for — he did not know how long, but the fire had been built up when he lay down and it was down to an eye now, so a long time — and she was not doing anything, and her face was not doing arithmetic anymore.

Her face was doing nothing at all. Her face looked like the face of somebody on a porch.

Oh, he thought.

And she said, out loud, perfectly conversationally, into the dark: "Rook."

He was up before the word was finished.

"Where."

"Back down the grade. On the bank, on the left side, coming up toward us on the flat." Her head had not moved. "One. It's one."

"How far."

"I don't—" and then, for the first time, something went through her voice. "It's a long way. It's a long way. It's past where I—"

"Distance, Cole."

"Seventy feet. Eighty. It's outside, it's outside my—" She stopped. She started again, and her voice came out flat and level and almost bored, which Toren would remember for the rest of his life as the sound of somebody doing the hardest thing they have ever done and refusing to let it show. "Eighty feet and closing at a walk. Left of the crown. It hasn't clicked."

"Good," said Rook. "Nobody light."



It took about six seconds and Toren saw almost none of it.

There was the shape of a man going away down the grade at a speed that men do not go, with a hard blue-white frame burning around his legs and throwing the ballast stones into sharp relief for twenty feet on either side of him. There was a stroke of white, short — much shorter than the spear on the road, a thing not much longer than a cleaver — that came out and went in and was gone. There was no sound at all except the wind, and the small collapse of a great deal of something soft.

Then there was a man walking back up the grade with his palm held flat and low at his side, unhurried, gathering as he came.

He stopped at the fire and tipped the jar and set it on the tailboard.

"Flask," he said. "Maybe a little under."

Dessa had not moved off the canvas.

"Is that it?" she said.

"That's it."

"That's it? I called it at eighty feet and you went and — and that's—"

"That's what it looks like when it works." Rook stoppered the jar. "You gave me ninety seconds. Ninety seconds means I choose the ground, I choose the distance, I choose whether anybody wakes up. It was never going to get closer than sixty feet to this fire and it was never going to be a fight." He looked at her across the red eye of the coals. "On the road with Prentice you had thirty feet and you were throwing and catching, so what you actually had was thirty feet about every fifty steps, which is nothing, which is why four people are buried above a road."

Nobody said anything.

"That's not on you," Rook said. "You were fourteen and nobody taught you. I'm telling you what changed tonight because you're going to lie down in a minute and feel like nothing happened." He put the jar in the cart. "That's Flame, Cole."

Dessa Cole sat on a piece of canvas on the edge of a seventy-year-old railway embankment with a nosebleed starting again and her hands shaking in her lap.

"Oh," she said.



She could not sleep afterward and neither could Toren, so they sat by the built-up fire with the blanket over both their shoulders like two people on a bench.

"It didn't feel like a door opening," she said.

"What did it feel like?"

"Nothing. That's what I'm telling you. It felt like nothing." She had her hands out to the coals, turning them. "All day it felt like holding a bucket. And then somewhere on that watch it stopped feeling like holding a bucket and it just — it was on. And I didn't do anything to make it be on. I stopped doing something."

Toren thought about that for a while.

"That's what it's like for me," he said. "When it works. When I stop telling it."

"Yeah, but you think that's the Ember being nice to you."

"It is the Ember being—"

"Toren." She turned her head and looked at him, and her eyes had gone red all around from the nosebleeds, and she was almost laughing, and there was something underneath the almost-laughing that he did not have a word for. "I haven't got an Ember. I haven't got anything. There's no device. There's nothing in my hands and nothing in my pockets and nothing anybody gave me at a fire." She looked back at the coals. "It didn't feel like something coming in. It felt like something I was doing."

Across the fire, in the dark on the far side of it, where Toren had assumed for the last hour that there was nobody, a man who had not moved and had not spoken shifted his weight very slightly on the tailboard of a cart.

Neither of them heard it.



Chapter 9: The Same Room

It rained from before light and it did not look like stopping, and at eight in the morning Rook put the cart under the arch where the grade crossed a stream and said they were not walking today.

The arch was a stone bridge with the railway on top of it and the water going through underneath, and there was a dry shelf of gravel along one side of the channel wide enough for six people and a cart if nobody wanted to be comfortable. The rain came down past both ends of it in two grey sheets. Inside, it was dim and loud and almost warm.

Milo got the stove going. Senna had a blanket up to her chin and her leg out along the boards and said the arch was the best room she had been in since the fifth of the month, which was a joke and was also true.

Rook sat on a stone with his back to the wall and let them get settled, and then he said:

"Voss. On the first night at the Gate you asked me a question and I told you to go to sleep."

Toren looked up.

"Ask it again."



"Why do they come at the light?"

"That's not the question. That's the question you asked. Ask the real one."

Toren sat with his hands around a tin cup and thought about it, and the rain came down at both ends of the arch, and he found that he was frightened, which made no sense at all.

"Why do they come at worked power," he said. "Ember or magic. No difference. That's what you said at Tallow Gate — you said Ember, magic, doesn't matter which, and nobody asked you about it, because it's a rule and rules are just true." He turned the cup around. "But it shouldn't work. Wyck's Ember is a piece of metal his aunt bought off a hunter. Dessa hasn't got anything. Milo's light comes out of his hand. Those are three completely different things and something with no eyes is coming at all three of them like they're the same thing."

Nobody moved.

"So the question is why they can't tell the difference," Toren said.

"No," said Rook. "That's still not it. That's the last easy one, though, so I'll give it to you for free." He leaned his head back against the stone. "They can tell the difference between a fire and a fox. They can tell the difference between a cart and a man. There's nothing wrong with what they use for seeing. The question is what's actually the same about the three of you that something with no eyes can pick out at eighty feet in the dark."



"Faust."

"Yes."

"Make your light."

Milo put out his hand and the little pale plum of it came up in his palm, steady as a held stone, and sat there.

"Now tell me what you do to make it come."

"I just—" Milo stopped. He looked at his own hand with an expression Toren had never seen on him. "That's a horrible question."

"It is. Take your time."

Milo took his time. The rain went on. Dessa had pulled her knees up and was watching him the way you watch someone carry something heavy across a room.

"All right," Milo said at last. "You know how you can make your ears go? Not wiggle them. The thing behind your ears, when you're listening hard on purpose and you sort of — open up sideways. There's a muscle that isn't a muscle."

"Go on."

"It's that. I do that, except further back. And then—" He frowned at his own palm. "I want to say I let it out. That's what it feels like from the outside, like pouring. But that's not it. It's more like I stop being in the way of it. I get out of the road and it's already there and it comes up." He looked up. "That's the stupidest thing I've ever said out loud."

"It's the best answer anybody's given me in nine years," said Rook. "Put it out. Cole."

Dessa said, "I'm not going to say anything different from him."

"Say it anyway."

"It's the same." She had gone very still. "It's the exact same. Last night. I stopped doing something and it was already on. I said that to Toren by the fire and I thought I was describing something about me, about being tired, and—" She stopped. She said, in a smaller voice, "It's the same thing he just said."

"Tanner."

Wyck did not answer.

He was sitting against the far wall of the arch with his bad leg out and his arms folded and his jaw set, and he had been that way since the word Flame had come out of Rook's mouth the previous afternoon, and he looked up now with his eyes absolutely clear.

"No," he said.

"No what."

"No, I'm not doing the game." Wyck's voice was level. "I know what this is. You're going to go round the four of us and get us all to say the same thing and then you're going to tell us something clever. I've watched you do it twice now." He shifted his leg with both hands, carefully, the way he had learned to in four days. "And I'll tell you the honest reason I'm not playing, since apparently this is a morning for honest reasons. It's because whatever you're about to say, you're about to say it to them, and I'm the one who's had one of these in my hand every day for two years, and if it turns out the two years weren't the thing that mattered, I'd rather hear it standing up."

Rook looked at him for a while.

"Fair," he said. "Voss."



Toren had the Ember in his hand before he knew he had reached for it.

"You already know mine," he said. "I go loose. I stop telling it. I ask it."

"Ask it what."

"To — " He stopped.

"Say the whole sentence."

"I ask it to please help."

"Right." Rook did not smile. There was nothing in his face at all. "Now leave the asking out of it and tell me what it feels like from the inside. Not what you want. What happens in you when it works."

Toren opened his mouth to say I don't know. Instead, without any warning at all, he was back in wet grass under a broken overpass, his hands closing on a warm grey rod, a door coming off its latch at the back of his own head.

"There's a room," he said.

His voice came out wrong. He pushed on.

"When he gave it to me. That's what it was. There was a — I've never said this to anybody. Everyone says the Kindling's like waking up on your arm, or it's like a light, and it wasn't. It was a room." He was looking at his own hands. "A big quiet empty room at the back of a house I'd been living in for thirteen years and never gone all the way to the back of. And when the Ember works, when it actually works — that's where I am. I'm standing in the doorway of it and I'm not touching anything and I'm not asking for anything, and then there's light in my hand."

The rain came down at both ends of the arch.

"Say what he said," Rook told him. "Faust's line. Say it back."

"He said he gets out of the road and it comes up."

"And the girl?"

"She stopped doing something and it was already on."

"And you're standing in the doorway of a room that was already there," said Rook, "and not one of you is describing a device, and not one of you is describing anything arriving from anywhere."



He let that sit for a long time. Longer than Toren wanted. He picked up the water can and drank and put it down and wiped his mouth on the back of his wrist, and outside the arch the stream came up in the rain by a visible amount, and nobody said anything at all.

"Here it is," Rook said. "I'm going to say it once, flat, and then we're going to spend a month on it and you're all going to be sick of me.

"It's one thing. It has always been one thing. There's no Ember power and no rift power; there's power, and there are two doors onto it, and every person alive was handed one of them at thirteen and spent the rest of their life thinking the door was the house.

"Faust wasn't given anything by a rift. Nothing reached through a hole in the sky and made him a present. He's got a light in his hand because he found a way to stop being in the way of what was already in him, and he found it at thirteen by accident, and he's been apologizing for it for two years because nobody told him what it was.

"Cole's the same and hers is bigger and she's had nobody to ask.

"And Voss." Rook's eyes came round. "That thing in your hand has never decided anything in its life. It has no opinion. It is a piece of shaped metal your great-great-grandmother's people made a tool out of, and it is very good at what it does, and what it does is make it easier for you to find a door you'd never have found on your own. That's all. That's the entire trick of it. Every time you've gone loose and asked it nicely and got four inches of light, you were the one who made the light, and you thanked a stick."

Toren could not feel his hands.

"That's not—"

"Say it's not."

"It cut something on the road," Toren said. His voice had come apart somewhere in the middle. "I was falling. I wasn't asking for anything, I wasn't — I was falling down a bank and it went off in my hand and it killed a thing, and I didn't do that, I couldn't have done that, I'd been standing in the road for a minute before that trying to make it work and it wouldn't—"

"You were standing in the road trying to make it work," Rook said. "Then you fell down a bank and for about one second you stopped trying to make anything do anything at all."

The arch was very quiet.

"I know," Rook said. "It's the worst news you've had since the road. Everybody gets it in that order — you find out you're not being helped, and the first thing you feel is that you've been robbed. Sit in it. It passes."



Milo said, "Then why have the Ember at all?"

"Because I can't walk through a wall." Rook got up off the stone and went to the mouth of the arch and stood in the noise of the water with his hands in his pockets. "I use a door. You use a door. Doors are not a moral failing, they're a door. The only thing I'm telling you to stop doing is bowing to it on the way through."

"Has it got a name?" said Milo. "The — the whole of it. The house. Has somebody got a word for it?"

"It hasn't got a name."

"Everything's got a—"

"It hasn't got a name," Rook said, "because it isn't a system and it isn't a school and nobody owns it. It's just what's true. The names are all on the doors." He watched the rain. "People have died over which door. I'd rather not hand you one more word to be right about."

Senna Voss said, from the cart, "My husband would have given three fingers to hear that."

Rook turned his head.

"He was Ember forty years," she said. "He believed every word of the other version. He believed it the way you believe the floor." She shifted the blanket up. "I'd like to tell you he'd have been glad. He'd have been furious, and then he'd have been up all night, and then about a week later he'd have been glad."



"Right." Rook came back in out of the mouth of the arch. "Here's the rest of it and it's the part nobody ever believes.

"Every one of you understands what I just said. All four of you. Tanner understood it before I finished and he's the one who's angry about it, which means he got it first and hardest." He stood with the water going past behind him. "And not one of you will be able to do a single thing with it. Not today, not this week. You'll all agree with me by supper and you'll all go out and get exactly what you got yesterday, and some of you will decide that means I'm wrong and some of you will decide it means you're broken, and both of those are going to be wrong."

"Then what's the point of telling us?" said Wyck.

"Because knowing it is worth nothing," Rook said, "and you can't start paying for it until you know it."

He picked up the second axe off the cart and tested the head with his thumb.

"You've got a month of being bad at something you understand perfectly," he said. "That's the whole of the next stretch of your lives. I'd tell you it gets better but you'd only ask when."



Toren went out at dusk when the rain had thinned to nothing and the stream had come up over the gravel shelf at the low end, and walked forty paces up the grade so that nobody could see him, and stood in the wet with his father's Ember in his fist.

He knew it now. That was the enormous, appalling thing. He had believed Rook completely somewhere around the middle of the man's third sentence and he had gone on believing him all afternoon, through the cooking and the water and Milo asking eleven more questions and Wyck not saying one word, and it had not moved a hair's breadth since.

It's mine. It was always mine. There's a room and it's in me and nobody gave it to me.

He did not go loose. He did not ask. He stood in the wet grass in the last of the light and reached, the way you reach for a thing on a shelf you have used your whole life, because it was his, because it had always been his, because the man had said so and the man had never once lied to any of them about anything.

The Ember put out four inches of white light and held it for two seconds and went out.

He did it again and got nothing.

He did it eleven more times and got nothing, and then he sat down on the wet ballast on the side of the grade with his arms across his knees, and did not cry, and was there a long while.



Chapter 10: Ballast

The grade climbed for two days after the rain, so gradually that you could only tell by looking back, and on the third morning it came out on a shoulder of open hill where the wind never stopped and somebody had once laid four miles of dead-straight track across the top of the world.

Up here, the grass had never taken. The ballast lay bare from edge to edge — a wide stone bed of fist-sized grey rock, every piece of it broken and angular, laid down by hand seventy years before anyone in the party had been born.

Rook walked out onto it, turned around, and said, "This'll do."

"For what?" said Milo.

"For the part you're all going to remember."



He took the cart off the grade and put it on the grass with Senna in it and the wheel chocked, and he put Dessa on the tailboard with her back against the boards.

"You're not drilling," he said. "You're holding it, and you're holding it on me and on whichever of them is in front of me, and if I hurt one of them worse than I meant to, you say so before he does. He won't say so. That's the whole reason you're up there."

"What am I looking for?"

"You'll know." He was shrugging his coat off. "You've had your hands inside people. If something's wrong inside a person you'll feel it and you won't know how you know. Don't try to know how. Just say it."

Dessa sat up straighter on the tailboard and did not say anything, and Toren, watching her, saw that she had just been handed something and that it was the first thing anybody had handed her since her mother died.

"Tanner. You're first."



The drill was this, and Rook explained it once.

"You stand there. You light up and you hold a full Edge. I come at you." He had a length of ash out of the cart, six feet of it, the shaft he had cut for a cart-pole and not used. "I'm not using mine. I'm using this, and I'm using the middle of it, and I'm not going to open you up. You're going to get hit and it's going to hurt and you're going to have marks tomorrow. What I want to know is whether the light stays on."

"That's it?"

"That's it. And it isn't, and you'll find out which in about four seconds."

Wyck lit up. Three clean feet of white, steady as a lamp.

Rook came at him.

He did not use the light around his legs, not for Wyck, and it still took less than a second to cross fourteen feet and Toren's whole body jumped. The ash shaft came around low and Wyck got his arm down and took it across the forearm and did not move his feet, and the Edge did not so much as flicker.

Rook stepped back.

"Again."

He came again, high, and Wyck took it on the shoulder, and the light held. He came at the bad leg and Wyck pivoted off it onto the good one in a way that cost him something visible, and the light held. He came three more times fast, one after another with no gap, and the light held through every one of them, and on the last one Wyck got the Edge up and turned the shaft aside with it, which nobody had asked him to do.

"Stop," Rook said.

He stood in the middle of the ballast with the shaft down and looked at Wyck Tanner for a while.

"That's as good as I've seen from somebody your age," he said. "I'm saying that in front of all of them on purpose. Two years on a fence post with nobody watching and no reason, and here's what it bought you: I can hit you and nothing happens. Nothing at all. You don't even go pale."

Wyck's chin came up about half an inch.

"Now make it four feet."

"What?"

"Four feet of Edge. Not three. Make it longer."

Wyck's jaw set. The white light out of his fist wavered and went out and came back, three feet again, and stood there.

"Longer."

He tried. Toren could see the whole of it — the shoulders coming up, the breath going in and locking, the entire two years of a boy making himself do a boring thing every day arriving at once and being applied, hard, to a thing that did not care.

Three feet.

"Longer."

"I'm trying—"

"I know. Keep going."

It took about four minutes. At the end of it Wyck Tanner threw his Ember down on the ballast — actually threw it, hard, so that it skipped twice and rang — and stood with his hands in his hair and his back to everybody.

Rook let him have it. He did not say a word. He picked the Ember up off the stones, checked it the way you check a tool somebody's dropped, and set it on the cart's tailboard where Wyck would have to walk past Dessa to get it, and said, "Voss."



Toren got hit eleven times before anything happened in his head at all.

The first one took him across the ribs and he did not even light up before it landed. The second one he was lit for and the light went out in the instant between seeing the shaft move and feeling it, so completely and so far in advance that he understood with a sick lurch that the light had not been knocked out of him. He had put it out. He had put it out himself, a full quarter-second before the shaft arrived, the way you close your eyes before a thing hits you.

"Again."

He went loose. He rolled the shaft in his fingers and thought please and got five inches, and Rook came at him and the five inches went out and the ash caught him low and put him down on the ballast on his hip and his palm.

Bare broken stone. It took the skin off his hand in one pass.

"Get up."

"I'm—"

"You're up or you're done, and you're not done." Rook backed off four paces. "Again."

He went again and got nothing at all. He went again and got four inches and lost them. He tried the other thing, the thing he had been carrying since the arch, the thing he had believed completely for three days and had not once been able to use: it's mine, it's mine, it was always mine, come here —

— and the Ember gave him nothing whatsoever, and Rook's shaft came round and Toren flinched so hard he fell over without being touched.

Rook stopped.

"Say what you just did," he said.

"I told it to come."

"You demanded."

"You said it was mine—"

"It is yours. Demanding is still asking somebody." Rook crouched down on his heels in front of him, on the ballast, at eye level. "Listen to me. You've got two settings and both of them are the same setting. Yesterday you begged a stick. Just now you ordered a stick around. It's still you standing in a road talking to something outside yourself, and it doesn't matter how polite you are about it, because there's nobody out there to talk to. There has never been anybody out there. You're alone in it and that's the good news and you keep hearing it as the bad news."

Toren's eyes were streaming and it was mostly the wind.

"Then what do I do."

"You were doing it in the arch," Rook said, "when you told me about the room."

He stood up and backed off four paces.

"Again."



Here is what Toren worked out, lying on his back on broken stone on the top of a hill with blood coming into his eye from somewhere above it.

He had understood the room for three days. He had thought about the room constantly — in the traces of the cart, at the water, lying awake — and every single one of those times he had thought about the room from somewhere else. He had stood outside it and considered it. He had admired it. He had told himself, over and over, with enormous sincerity, that there was a room at the back of him and it was his.

He had not gone in.

And the reason he had not gone in, the real reason, the one that arrived all at once and unwelcome, was that the room had opened on the night his father knelt down in the dirt in front of him. The last time he had been all the way inside it, there had been two people alive who were dead now, and there was some part of him that had been very carefully not going back in there since the fifth of the month.

"Again," Rook said.

Toren got up.

He did not go loose. He did not reach. He did not ask anything of anybody. He stood on the ballast with his ruined hand at his side and the wind going over the top of the hill and he went back, all the way to the back of the house, and stood in the middle of the enormous quiet room and made himself stay there.

The Ember lit.

It came up out of the end of the shaft in one clean stroke and it did not stop at four inches or five inches or a foot. Fourteen inches of hot white, steady, no wavering at all, and it was still climbing when Rook came at him.

And Toren saw the shaft move, and the room went away, and the light went out, and the ash took him across the face above the left eye and he went down on the stones and did not get up for a while.



"Cole?"

"He's all right." Dessa's voice, from a long way off, and then much closer, and then her hands on his jaw turning his head. "It's the eyebrow. It's just the eyebrow, they bleed like that. Nothing's broken. His hand's bad."

"How bad."

"Skinned. Not deep. He's jammed two fingers."

"Fix the eyebrow. Leave the hand."

"Why leave the—"

"Because he needs to look at the hand for four days," Rook said, "and he doesn't need to look at his own face."



Dessa closed the eyebrow in about three minutes, sitting on the ballast with her thumbs either side of the cut and her tongue between her teeth.

She had done it before. Toren had watched her do it once in Coldwater, on a boy who had gone through a window, and it had taken her most of an afternoon and she had gone and lain down in the dark for four hours afterward and Ilsa had made everybody be quiet.

She finished, and sat back, and looked at her own hands.

"Huh," she said.

"You all right?"

"I'm fine." She flexed her fingers. "That's — I'm fine. That's the thing. I'd normally be — "

She did not finish it. She got up and went and sat back on the tailboard and picked up the holding again, and about ten minutes later Toren saw her look at her own hands a second time.



"Sit down," Rook told him, "and tell me what happened on the last one."

"I got fourteen inches."

"You got about sixteen and it was still going up when I moved. What happened when I moved?"

"I left."

Rook's head came round.

"Say that again."

"I left the room." Toren had his ruined hand cradled in the other one and his voice was thick and his left eye was closing. "The whole time I was standing in it, it was fine. It was — it wasn't even hard. And then you moved and I went out to look at you and there wasn't anybody in the room anymore and the light went out because there wasn't anybody in the room."

Rook sat down on the ballast beside him, which he had not done before.

"That's it," he said. "That's the whole of it and it took you eleven hits, and I was told it in words by somebody patient and it took me most of a year. You're not slow. Nobody's ever said you were."

"But I still can't—"

"You can't do it while anything is happening. That's right. That's the actual problem and now you know its actual shape, which you did not know this morning, and which is worth every mark you're going to have tomorrow." He was looking out along the ballast. "Here's what you did four times today and didn't notice. First time I came at you, you put the light out a quarter second before the stick landed. Last time, the light went out when I moved. That's about a second later in the same event. You didn't get stronger. You stopped leaving quite so early."

"That's not—"

"It's the whole thing," Rook said. "That's the only thing that's ever going to change. Everything else is arithmetic on top of it."

Toren sat on the stones with the wind going over and thought about that.

"How long," he said.

"Don't ask that."

"How long?"

"Months," said Rook. "And it won't be a day you can point at. It'll be some morning when you notice you haven't been leaving for a while."



Milo got his light an inch off his palm at four in the afternoon.

It lasted about a second and a half. It came up out of his hand the way it always did and then sat there in the air an inch above his skin, small and pale and completely unsupported, and Milo Faust made a noise like a man stepping into cold water and it went out.

"Did you see that," he said. "Did you — did anybody—"

"I saw it," said Wyck, from the grass.

He had been sitting there for an hour with his Ember in his hand, not lit. He said it flatly, without turning around, and it was the only thing he had said to any of them since he threw the thing on the stones, and Milo stopped mid-sentence and said, "Oh. Thanks," in an entirely different voice.



They came off the ballast at dusk and made camp in the lee of the bank.

Toren could not use his right hand for anything. He sat by the fire and let Milo cut his food for him and did not have the energy to be embarrassed about it, and his eye closed completely somewhere around nine at night and stayed closed for two days.

Before he lay down, he went out past the cart to where Rook was doing something to the axle by feel in the dark.

"Can I ask one thing?"

"You can ask."

"When it worked. At the end. It was going up and it hadn't stopped." Toren's voice came out very carefully controlled. "How far was it going to go?"

Rook did not look up from the axle.

"I don't know," he said.

"You must—"

"I don't know, Voss. That's the honest answer and you can have it." He wiped his hands on the grass. "Go to bed."



Chapter 11: What Was Taken

Toren's eye opened on the second day after the ballast, about a third of the way, which Milo found very funny and said so at intervals for six hours.

They came down off the high ground through a long shallow cutting where the rock walls sweated and the swallows went in and out of holes in them, and at the bottom of it the grade crossed an old road at a level place, and there was a building.

Rook put his hand up two hundred yards out.

"Cole."

Dessa stopped where she was, in the middle of the grade, and did not sit down and did not close her eyes. She stood with the wind pulling her coat and her face doing nothing at all for about fifteen seconds.

"Nobody," she said. "Not in it, not behind it, not in the trees. Nothing out to — " she turned her head slowly, left to right — "call it two hundred feet, and it's clean all the way out."

"Anything under two hundred that isn't people?"

"There's something small in the roof space. It's a bird."

And they went on, and nobody said anything about it, and Toren realized four steps later that a week ago that exchange would have been impossible and that not one of them had remarked on it, including Dessa.



It had been a wayhouse.

That was Senna who said the word, from the cart, sitting up on one elbow with her hand shading her eyes. It was a long stone building set into the bank with its back to the grade and its face to the road, one storey, a slate roof, a well-housing at the near end and a yard of packed earth in front, and it had burned.

Not recently. The fire had gone through the roof at the east end and taken the rafters there and stopped, or been stopped, halfway along, and the rain had been at it since. There was grass growing in the char. There was a dock leaf a foot high standing up out of the black where the doorframe had fallen.

"Four months," Rook said. "Maybe five."

The door was open.

It was the only thing that Toren registered as strange, and he registered it without knowing why, standing in the yard with the wind going through the empty window holes. The door was off one hinge and hanging, and it was open, and it had been opened. There was no break in it. Nobody had gone through that door who was not let through it or who did not have all the time in the world.

Rook stood in the yard for a while and then went in.



There were three graves at the west end of the yard, past the well.

They were proper graves. That was the thing Toren could not get past, and he stood looking at them for a long time with the wind in his face. Somebody had dug them to a decent depth in hard ground. Somebody had squared the corners. Somebody had gone to the trouble of setting a flat stone at the head of each one and standing it upright and packing it with smaller stones so it would not fall over in a winter, and had then not written anything on any of them.

Milo came and stood beside him and did not say anything for once.

"Who does that," Toren said.

"Somebody with a shovel," said Milo.

"Somebody with time."



Inside, the wayhouse had been a trading post.

You could see the whole of what it had been from the doorway even with the east end open to the sky. There was a long counter of two-inch plank with a hatch in it. There was a stove on a stone pad with its pipe gone. There was a sleeping loft over the west end, part of it fallen. There was a sack of meal on the floor behind the counter, split, four months gone green and sprouted, and a rack of dried fish on a wire, and a stone crock of salt with the lid still on it, and a tin lamp, and a coat on a peg.

And along the back wall there was a rack, floor to shoulder height, built out of heavy timber in a double row of cradles.

The cradles were shaped. They were cut and lined and padded, every one of them the same, for something round and heavy about the size of a small barrel.

They were empty.

Rook went down the length of the rack with two fingers trailing along the timber and did not touch anything else in the building.

"They didn't take the food," Dessa said.

"No."

"There's salt. There's a coat." She was standing in the middle of the floor with her arms wrapped round her ribs. "Anybody who — if it was people on the road, if it was anybody desperate, they'd have taken the salt before they took anything. My mother used to say salt's the first thing that walks."

"It is," said Rook.

"Then—"

"Then it wasn't anybody desperate."



The tally-board was by the door, on the wall that had not burned.

It was a slab of slate the size of a cart's tailgate in a wooden frame, ruled off in columns with a scribe, and it was the sort of thing Toren had seen at the mill in Coldwater: a board where whoever came in wrote what they brought and what they took, so that at the end of a season a man with a ledger could settle it all up.

Most of it was chalk, and most of the chalk had gone with four months of weather coming in the open door. There were ghost-lines of it, half-words, a column of dates in a small clear hand.

The last line was not chalk.

Somebody had cut it into the slate with a knife point, slowly, pressing hard, in letters an inch high:

CINDER CO — 4 csk — PAID OUT

Dessa read it aloud because she was standing closest, and she read it the way you read something before you have understood it, flatly, and then she stopped with her mouth still open.

Nobody moved in the burnt room.

"Read me the dates," Rook said.

"What?"

"The column. On the left. Read me the last date you can still make out."

Dessa leaned in and put her face close to the slate. "It's — fourth month. Fourth month, something. Eleven, maybe. Or seventeen, the top's gone."

"The one above it."

"Fourth month, ninth."

"And above that."

"Third month, twenty-eighth."

Rook stood looking at the board.

Toren was three feet away and watching him, and this was not the sluice. At the sluice, the man's hands had gone and his breathing had stopped and everything about him had changed while his face did nothing. Here his hands were perfectly still. His breathing was ordinary. He read the board the way a man reads a notice about a bridge closure, top to bottom, twice.

And then he said, "Four months," in a voice with nothing in it whatsoever, and Toren understood, with a small cold shock, that the flatness was the tell. That the man was doing arithmetic. That the arithmetic had come out to a number he did not like, and that the number was how far away.

"Who's Cinder Company?" said Milo.

"A hunting company."

"What's that mean?"

"It means men who kill rift-things for money," Rook said. "There are forty of them between here and the coast. Most of them are four brothers and a wagon. Some of them are bigger than that."

"Is that a big one?"

"Yes."

Milo, who asked everything, and who had asked a man on a road how his own mule had died and taken the answer without flinching, said, "You know them."

Rook took the tally-board off the wall.

He did it with no ceremony at all. He got a thumb behind the frame at the top corner and worked it, and the whole thing came off the stone with a crack and a fall of old plaster. He turned it face-in against his chest and carried it out into the yard, and over his shoulder he said:

"Get the water on."



They camped in the yard because the well was good and the wind was less.

Wyck sat with his back against the well-housing and his leg out and watched the door of the wayhouse for a long time, and when Toren came to sit near him he said, without any preamble at all:

"He wasn't surprised."

"He was—"

"He read a thing off a wall that said a company came through here and took four casks and nobody's alive to say what happened, and there are three graves out there with nothing written on them, and he read the dates." Wyck turned his head. His eyes in the firelight were completely steady. "You know what you ask for when you ask for a date? You ask for a date when you already know the who and the what and the only thing left you want is the when."

Toren did not have anything to say to that.

"He picked this grade," Wyck said. "There's no road on it. There's no town on it. He walked off the loop road at a place with no turning, which means he knew it was there, which means he's been on it." He shifted the leg with both hands. "He told you lot we're not going east. Did he ever once say what we are going toward?"



Milo cooked. He had taken the cooking over somewhere in the last four days without anybody deciding it, and he was better at it than he had any business being, and he had gotten into the habit of handing things round in a particular order — Senna, then Wyck because of the leg, then whoever was on watch, then everybody else.

Toren sat by the fire with his cut eye and his bad hand and ate, and did not notice for some time that the tally-board was not leaning against the cart where Rook had set it down.

He looked for it. It took him a while to find, because he was not looking in the right place.

The fire had a piece of wood in it that was not theirs. It was a length of frame-timber, painted, and the paint was going up in a green flame along one edge, and under the timber there was a flat dark shape in the coals that was not burning because slate does not burn, and the letters cut into it an inch high were still there and would still be there in a hundred years.

Toren looked across the fire.

Rook was sitting on the far side of it with his forearms on his knees, doing nothing at all, watching the slate not burn.



Dessa came and sat down beside Toren much later, when the others were asleep and Wyck was on the watch at the top of the yard.

"I've been thinking about the graves," she said.

"Don't."

"No, listen. I've been thinking about them the wrong way round all evening and I've only just got it." She had her chin on her knees. "It's not that somebody was kind. That's what I kept — I kept thinking, whoever did that was at least a bit decent, and it made me feel better, and then about an hour ago I worked out why it doesn't."

"Why doesn't it."

"Because whoever dug those had all afternoon," Dessa said. "Nobody was coming. They weren't hiding and they weren't hurrying and they weren't frightened of anything on this road at all. They took four casks and they wrote it on the board and then they buried three people properly and went to bed."

The fire popped. Out at the top of the yard Wyck shifted his weight off the bad leg, and went still again.

"Toren," Dessa said. "When you found that thing in the sluice — when he found it — what did he say to you?"

"He said sew your pocket."

"That's all?"

"That's all he ever says."

Dessa Cole looked across the fire at the dark shape of a man asleep with his back against a cart wheel, or not asleep, and put her chin back on her knees.

"He's not running away from anything," she said. "I want you to notice that with me, because I don't think I can hold it on my own. He's not running away from anything. He walked us off a road and onto a line that goes somewhere and he's the only one who knows where, and this afternoon he stood in a burned house and found out that something is four months ahead of us."

"He might have wanted it to be behind us."

"He might," said Dessa. "That's exactly the part I don't like."



Chapter 12: Point

Wyck Tanner walked up to the head of the cart at first light with his stick in his hand and his coat already on, and stood there until Rook looked at him.

"I'm walking today."

"You've been walking half of every day since the rain."

"I'm walking all of it." He had the leg braced and his weight mostly off it and he was standing anyway, which was the argument. "Ten days. It's closed. It's stiff and it's ugly and it hurts and it's going to hurt in five weeks too. I can hold a full Edge for five minutes on it sitting down and I can do three standing, and I did three standing on that ballast while you were hitting me with a cart-pole."

Rook looked at him for a while.

"All right," he said.

"And I'm at point."

The yard went quiet in the way a yard does when four people who were doing things have all stopped doing them.

Rook finished what he was doing with the strap. Then he straightened up, and said, without any particular weight on it:

"Voss at point."



It was the worst possible version, Toren thought afterward — not the refusal, but the flatness. If the man had hesitated for one second it would have been survivable. If he had said not yet it would have been something a person could work with.

"Give me a reason," Wyck said.

"I gave you one at the pumphouse."

"You gave me that's just how it is, which isn't one, and I've thought about nothing else for ten days." Wyck did not raise his voice. He had not raised his voice about any of it, not once, which was somehow the most alarming thing about him. "I'll take a bad reason. I'd honestly rather have a bad one. Tell me it's because I lit up on a hillside and got my aunt killed, and I'll take it, and I'll stand where you put me for the rest of my life."

Senna Voss, in the cart, said nothing at all and did not pretend to be asleep.

"It isn't that," said Rook.

"Then what."

"Because you'd be good at it."

Wyck stood there.

"That's a reason to put me there," he said.

"It's a reason to put him there," said Rook, and picked up the shafts, and the cart came up out of the mud of the yard, and that was the end of it.



They went north-west up the grade all that day with the burned wayhouse falling behind them, and it was a good day, and Toren hated how good it was.

The sky came clear about ten in the morning for the first time in a week. The grade ran up through open birch country where the trees had got into the ballast on both sides and made a green tunnel of the cutting, and the walking was easy, and the country did not have a single thing wrong with it.

And they were good at it. That was the thing he could not stop noticing.

Milo came down the line at eleven with the water and gave it out in his order — Senna, then Wyck, then whoever was on watch, then everybody else — and nobody had told him to do that and nobody had noticed him starting and now it was simply the order in which water happened.

Dessa walked in the middle with her hands in her pockets and said "clear" about every ten minutes, not loudly, to nobody in particular, and had been doing it for two days, and the strange thing was the effect it had on Toren's shoulders. He had not known his shoulders were up. He had been walking at point with his shoulders somewhere around his ears since the loop road, and Dessa Cole saying clear in a bored voice took two inches off them every time she did it.

At the top of the long pitch where the grade climbed out of the birch, where the ballast was loose and the cart began to slide, all four of them got on it without a word being said. Milo and Toren on the near side. Dessa on the off. Wyck on the tail with his shoulder into it and his bad leg braced straight out behind him, pushing with the good one, breathing through his teeth. Rook in the traces, leaning.

Nobody thanked anybody. At the top they came off it and went back to where they had been walking and Milo said something about birch bark and the day went on.



At noon Wyck rewrapped Toren's hand.

He did it without being asked and largely without looking at him. Toren had been trying to get the cloth round the palm one-handed with his teeth for about four minutes, and Wyck watched him fail at it twice from six feet away, and then came over and took the end of the cloth out of his mouth and did it properly.

"You've got it too tight at the wrist and too loose where it matters," he said. "Open your fingers. Not that far. There."

"Thanks."

"Yeah."

He wound it, and tucked the end, and pressed the tuck flat with his thumb, and sat back on his heels, and his face had nothing friendly in it at all.

"That's not me being all right about it," he said.

"I know."

"I want to be clear, because Faust is going to see this and get ideas." Wyck got his stick under him and stood up off the bad leg in the careful two-part way he had learned. "You'd have made a mess of your own hand and then you'd have been no good at point tomorrow, and I'm the one who'd be standing where you fell over. That's the whole of it."

"I know," Toren said again.

"Good."

He limped off up the grade, and Toren looked at his own hand, wrapped better than he could have wrapped it, by somebody who had spent ten days learning how a splinted limb wants to be handled, and did not know what to do with any part of that.



That evening Rook drilled the four of them together for the first time since the pumphouse.

It was full dark and the moon was down. He took them off the grade into the birch below the bank, and put them in the diamond — Toren at point, Wyck left, Dessa right, Milo centre — and told them to sweep a line of trees three hundred paces long and come back, and then he walked away and sat on the bank and did not come with them.

"He's not coming," Milo said.

"No."

"He's not coming."

"Faust," said Dessa, "walk."

They walked it.

It took forty minutes and nothing happened and Toren would remember it for years.

Nothing happened, and that was what was extraordinary. They went into standing birch in the dark, four children, one of them with a stick and a bad leg, one of them with a light he had never once used for anything, and they moved through it like a thing with four legs. When Toren turned, the diamond turned, because Dessa felt him turn before she saw him. When Wyck fell behind on a bad bit of ground, Milo shortened up without being told and the shape held. Twice Dessa said a distance and a direction in a flat voice and the two of them nearest went and looked and it was a fox and it was a stump, and neither time did anybody light anything.

At the far end Toren stopped the diamond at a place he did not like, a low fold in the ground with the trees thick above it, and held them there while Dessa put it down and stood in it for nearly a minute.

"Clear," she said. "It's clear. It's a bad-looking hole with nothing in it."

"All right," Toren said. "Back."

And they came back, and Rook was sitting on the bank exactly where they had left him, and he watched them come up out of the trees in formation and break out of it at the top without being told, and he said:

"Good."

That was all. That was the entire debrief. Milo looked around at the others to check that it had actually happened.



Later, at the fire, Milo Faust broke the first pan-bread off the stone, tore a piece off the corner of it, put the piece on a flat rock at the edge of the coals, and set it aside.

Then he handed the rest round in his order.

He did it without any ceremony whatsoever. He did it the way you put the lid back on a crock. Toren was almost certain Milo had no idea he was doing anything at all — that four days of watching a man do a thing had simply put the thing into his hands the way it puts a whistled tune into your head, and that if you had stopped him and asked him he would have gone red and had no answer.

Across the fire, Rook looked at the piece of bread on the rock.

He looked at it for about three seconds, which was two and a half seconds longer than a piece of bread requires, and his face did not change, and then he looked away and ate his own and said nothing.

Dessa saw it. Toren saw her see it. Neither of them said anything, then or afterward, and the piece of bread sat on the rock all night the way it had sat on a rock above a road eleven days before.



Senna waited until the others were down and then told Toren to go and get her the water can, which he did, and then to go away, which he also did, and he went about fifteen feet and sat down behind the cart wheel instead of lying down, and was not proud of it.

"You're going to break that boy," Senna said.

"No," said Rook.

"You put the one who can't hold a light in front of the one who can, in the dark, every day, and you won't say why, and when he asks you for a reason you tell him he'd be good at it." Her voice was very dry. "I ran a house with three sons in it. I know exactly what you're doing and I'd like to hear you say it out loud."

"Standing where you're already good is worth nothing. He'd be safe at point. Voss isn't, and he's the one who needs the hour."

"That's true," said Senna.

"It is."

"It's true and it isn't the whole of it, and you know that, because if it were the whole of it you'd have said it in the yard this morning and saved yourself the week you're about to have." The old woman resettled the blanket. "You could give him something. Not all of it. Anything.
