You've got four of them and three of them would follow you into a river and the fourth one is doing arithmetic in the dark every night, and he's not stupid, and he's going to get an answer whether or not it's the right one."

For a while there was nothing but the fire.

"I know," Rook said.

"Then why—"

"Because the answer isn't mine to hand out," he said, "and because the day I do hand it out, it stops being something he's angry about and starts being something he has to carry, and he's fifteen, and I'd like him to have a while longer."

Senna Voss did not say anything to that for long enough that Toren thought she had gone to sleep.

"That's the first honest thing you've said since the ledger," she said.



Wyck came and found him at the change of the watch, near two in the morning, and sat down on the ballast beside him, and for a while neither of them said anything.

"I've worked something out," Wyck said.

"Wyck—"

"No, it's all right. It's not a fight. I'm too tired and my leg hurts and I genuinely haven't got one in me." He had his stick across his knees. "I've been going at this wrong for ten days. I kept asking why he picks you and I kept answering it with things about me — I'm better, I'm older, I've done the work, he must not rate me. That's all I had. And it's rubbish, because he does rate me, he said so on the ballast in front of everyone and he meant it, and he made me make it four feet because he wanted to see where the end of me was."

Toren's stomach had gone cold.

"So it was never about which of us is better," Wyck said. "It was never about us at all. He's not picking the better one. He's picking you, specifically, and he's been doing it since the first afternoon at the Gate, before he'd ever seen either of us do anything."

The fire had burned right down. Out beyond it the grade ran away north-west in a straight black line under a sky with no moon in it.

"At the pumphouse," Wyck said, "I was sitting in that lower doorway with my leg open to the hip and I was watching you go up the slope, because there wasn't anything else to look at and I couldn't stand up. And you got to the top and you put your hand in your pocket and you went straight back down that hill into a flooded building on the worst day of my life, for something. And he let you. He'd spent four days telling us nobody goes anywhere alone and he let you go, and then he went in after you, and he came out, and neither of you has ever said one word about it to anybody. Not one word. In ten days."

Toren's right hand, wrapped that afternoon by the boy sitting next to him, was flat against his coat pocket. He did not remember putting it there. Under the cloth and under his palm was a double row of stitches he had sewn himself by firelight in a yard at Tallow Gate, two rows, not one, because he had been told two.

"I'm not going to ask you what it is," Wyck said.

"Wyck—"

"I've thought about it all week and I'm not going to, and I want you to understand why, because it's the only part of this I'm actually proud of." He got his stick under him and stood up, the careful way, in two parts. "If I ask you, you'll lie to me. You'll have to. And then I'll have that as well, and I've got enough."

He went off toward the cart, and stopped, and did not turn around.

"Just so you know what it's like from here," Wyck Tanner said. "You've got something. He knows what it is and you know what it is and the three of us don't, and every morning he puts you in front of us in the dark and won't say why, and every single one of us walks along behind you anyway."

He walked back to the fire.

Toren Voss sat on seventy-year-old broken stone with his hand flat over two rows of stitches, and did not go after him, and did not take his hand away.



Chapter 13: The Day Before

"You're not getting hit today," Rook said.

Toren had his coat off and his sleeves pushed up and had spent the walk down off the bank preparing himself, in the private way you prepare for a thing you have already survived eleven times, and he stood in the wet grass with all of that preparation going nowhere.

"Why not?"

"Because hitting you worked and it's finished working." Rook set the ash shaft down against the bank, where Toren could see it, which was not an accident. "Light up."

They were four days north-west of the birch country and the grade had come down out of the hills into flat ground with standing water in the fields on both sides and a wind off it that did not stop at any point in the day. It was the twenty-eighth morning since Tallow Gate. Toren's eyebrow had gone from red to white and had settled into a thin seam that Milo said made him look like a person who had been in a fight with a person, which was, Milo maintained, better than the truth. His right hand worked. It did not close all the way and the two middle fingers ached when the weather turned, and he had stopped noticing either of those things somewhere in the last week.

He lit up.

The room was there. It was always there now, which was the strange part — the thing that had taken eleven hits and a split eyebrow to find was, at eight in the morning in an empty field with nothing happening, no trouble at all. He went to the back of the house and stood in it and the Ember put out light, and the light came up and kept coming and steadied out at about the length of his forearm.

"Twenty inches," Rook said.

"Is that—"

"Hold it. Don't talk."

He held it. The wind came across the field and he stood in it with his arm out and a length of white light burning steadily out of his fist, and nothing in him strained at all. He had time, standing there, to think about how little it cost, and how much that frightened him.

"Now walk to the water and back."

Toren walked to the water and the light went out at the third step.



"Again."

He got four steps.

"Again."

He got two, which was worse than the first time, and he said so, and Rook said again without any change in his voice whatsoever and Toren went again.

By the middle of the morning, he had worked out what was happening and he had worked out a way around it, and he was very pleased with himself for about an hour.

What was happening was this: when he walked, he had to look at the ground. When he looked at the ground he came out of the room to look, the same way he had come out of the room to look at a stick swinging toward his head, and the light went with him. So he stopped looking at the ground. He picked a line of flat grass with nothing in it, and he put his eyes on a fence post two hundred paces off, and he shut the whole of the rest of the field out of his head, and he walked.

He got to the water with twenty inches of Edge still burning.

"Again," said Rook.

He did it again and it worked. He did it a third time with Milo walking beside him talking without pause about a bird he had seen, and it worked, and Milo's entire commentary went past him like weather.

"That's it," Toren said. He was breathing hard, not from effort — from something else. "That's the thing. You stop letting anything in. You just—"

"Cole," Rook said. "Give him a distance."

Dessa, on the tailboard of the barrow with her boots off, did not open her eyes. "Eighty feet, off the left of the grade, coming up on the flat."

The light went out before Toren's feet had stopped.

He stood in the grass with a dead rod in his hand and his heart going and his head whipping round to the left of the grade, where there was nothing, where there had obviously been nothing, where Dessa Cole was sitting on a tailboard with her eyes shut and had said a true thing about an empty field because a man had asked her to.

"There's nothing there," he said.

"No."

"Then why did you—"

"Because you've spent the morning teaching yourself to be deaf," said Rook, "and I'd rather you found out now than at eighty feet."



They stopped at noon and ate standing up.

Toren could not sit down. He walked the length of the barrow twice and came back and stood at the end of it with his bread in his hand and did not eat any of it, and Senna Voss watched him do all of that from inside the cart and said nothing at all, which from her was a considerable act of restraint.

"I don't know what you want," he said at last.

"Yes you do."

"I don't. You told me to get in the room and I got in the room. You told me to hold it and I held it. You told me to hold it walking and I found a way to hold it walking and then you took it off me for finding a way." He heard his own voice going up and did not stop it. "What's the version where I don't do it wrong?"

Rook finished his bread and wiped his hands on his coat.

"You're not trying to stop noticing," he said. "You're trying to notice from inside."

"That's not a—"

"It isn't an instruction. I know." Rook picked up the near shaft of the barrow and looked down the grade north-west, at the flat, at the line of it going away. "There isn't an instruction. There's a thing that's true and there's you being wrong about it in a slightly different place every day until you aren't. This morning you were wrong about the ground. Now you're wrong about the girl. That's a whole step and it took you four hours and I'd have given it a week."

Toren stood there with the bread in his hand.

"Is that good," he said.

"That's good," said Rook, and put the shafts on his shoulders.



Dessa told him about her hands that afternoon, walking, in the tone of somebody mentioning that a strap needed replacing.

"I closed Toren's eyebrow in three minutes on the ballast."

"I know. I watched you."

"I didn't lie down after." She had her hands in her pockets and her eyes on the middle distance and her voice was absolutely level, which Toren, twelve feet ahead on the near shaft, had learned to read as the sound of Dessa Cole being frightened of something. "In Coldwater I did a boy who went through a window and it took me an afternoon and then I was in the dark for four hours and my mother made everyone be quiet. On the ballast it took three minutes and I got up and went back to work and I've done two more since and I don't go down anymore. At all."

"When did you stop?"

"I don't know. That's what I'm telling you. There wasn't a day." She kicked a stone off the crown of the grade. "I noticed on the ballast and I didn't say anything, and then I noticed again about ten minutes later and I still didn't say anything, and that was eight days ago."

"Why didn't you say anything?"

Dessa was quiet for about twenty paces.

"Because if I said it out loud and I was wrong," she said, "then it would have been a thing I wanted."

Rook walked on for a while with the barrow creaking and Senna's blanket shifting in it.

"That's the same as the rest of it," he said. "You stopped throwing it. You've been filling instead of throwing for about a fortnight and you can't feel it happening because nobody can. It'll keep going."

"How far?"

"Don't ask that."

"You say that to him," said Dessa, "and he keeps asking."

"He does," Rook agreed, "and I keep telling him don't, and neither of us has stopped, and that's probably how it should go."



Toren went back to it at four in the afternoon, and the fourth hour was the one.

He did not get there by trying. That was the thing he would think about later. He had tried all morning and most of the afternoon, and had thrown himself at the problem from every angle he had, and at some point around the middle of the day he had simply run out of ways to be clever and had started walking the line with twenty inches of light and no plan at all.

And then Wyck said, from six feet behind him, "Your right foot's turned out."

Toren said, "What?"

"Your right foot. Every fourth or fifth step you turn the toe out and it drops your hip and then you've got to catch yourself." Wyck came up alongside at his own speed, the stick going down and taking weight and coming up. "You've done it since the pumphouse. It's the slope. You learned to walk on a road with a camber on it."

And Toren turned his head and looked at him, and answered him, and thought about his own foot, and looked down at it, and turned it in — and the light did not go out.

He got another eighty paces before he noticed.

When he did notice, it went out at once, and he stopped dead in the middle of the grade and said a word that his grandmother would have had opinions about.

"How long," he said. "How long was that."

"You don't want to know," said Wyck.

"How long?"

"About a minute and a half." Wyck leaned on the stick. "You talked to me and looked at your own boot and went on up the grade like a man going to fetch a bucket. I've never seen you do that. I've never seen anybody do that except him."



Rook came down the grade and stood in front of him and put his hand out flat, and Toren lit up before he was told to, and got two feet of it, clean, and held it.

"Walk."

He walked. It held.

"Look at me."

He looked. It held.

"Tell me what's in the field on your left."

"Water. Two birds in it. A post with — there's a post with wire on it." He heard himself saying it and heard the light still burning and something in his chest went up like a bird out of grass. "It's still on. It's still—"

Rook moved his right hand.

It was not a strike. It was six inches of motion, a man shifting his fingers at his side, and Toren's whole body went to it, and the room emptied, and twenty-four inches of white light stopped existing.

Nobody said anything for a moment.

"Right," Rook said. "That's the shape of it. Sit down."



They sat on the bank with the wind coming off the flooded fields and the light going long.

"There's a word for where you were between about four in the afternoon and a minute ago," Rook said. "It's Flame. You know it. Your grandmother sang half of it at a fire a month ago and Tanner's had it since before the rain."

Toren's throat had gone tight.

"Am I—"

"No." Rook said it without any weight at all. "Flame is when it holds while something's happening. What you did this afternoon is it holds while you're busy, and busy isn't happening, and the difference between those two things is a boy's leg, and you know that better than anyone on this grade."

"Then what am I?"

"You're the day before Flame," said Rook. "And you can be the day before Flame for a year, and men have. That's not me being hard on you. That's the actual size of the step and I'd be lying to you about it if I said a number."

"How long?" Toren said.

"Don't ask that."

"How long?"

Rook looked out across the water.

"Sooner than I'd have said a fortnight ago," he said.

It was not much. Toren carried it up the grade to the camp and it was still there when he lay down, and it was more than he had been given in a month.



Milo's trouble came at dusk, and it came the way Milo's things came, which was pleasantly and by accident.

He had got his light an inch off his palm on the ballast and had been working at it every evening since, and what he had arrived at, that evening, in the grass above the camp, was setting it down.

He put it on a flat stone at the top of the bank and took his hand away and it stayed.

"Look at that," he said. "Look at — Dessa. Dessa. Look at it."

It sat on the stone about the size of a plum, pale and steady and entirely unsupported, and it did not gutter and it did not drift, and Milo Faust walked in a circle around it with both hands in his hair.

"How long will it stay?" Toren said.

"I don't know! I've never — it's just there, it doesn't need me, I could go and—"

"Faust," said Rook.

He had come up out of the camp without any of them hearing him and he was standing eight feet off with his hands in his pockets, and he was looking at the stone.

"That's good," he said. "That's very good and I want you to hear me say it, because in about four seconds I'm going to tell you to put it out and you're going to think the two things cancel."

Milo's face fell about a foot. "Why?"

"What comes at worked power?"

"Everything, but—"

"Everything. And a light you're holding is a light you can drop, and a light sitting on a stone at the top of a camp is a fire on a hill with nobody's hand on it." Rook crouched down by the stone and looked at it from a few inches away, for longer than he needed to. "You've made a thing nobody I've ever met can make and the first true thing about it is that it's dangerous. Both of those are permanent. Put it out."

Milo put it out.

"Tomorrow," Rook said, standing up, "you're going to put it on a stone two hundred paces down the grade in the dark and come back and sit with us, and Cole's going to hold, and we're going to find out what walks up to it."

Milo stared at him.

"That's horrible," he said, with enormous admiration.



Toren woke once in the night and the fire was down and there was nobody at it.

Rook was up on the crown of the grade, forty feet off, standing with his hands in his pockets and his back to the camp, looking north-west.

There was nothing up there. Toren raised himself on one elbow and looked along the line of it, the way he had learned to look at country in the last month, and there was flat ground and standing water and a sky going grey at the bottom edge, and the grade running away straight into all of it, and nothing at all to see.

The man stood there for a long time and did not move and did not go any further, and after a while Toren lay back down.



Chapter 14: The Split

It was Senna who saw it, because Senna had nothing else to do.

She had been in a box since the fifth of the month and she had said so twice a week for five weeks, and what nobody had worked out until that morning was that a woman lying on her back in a barrow for five weeks with her head at the level of the tailboard sees a great deal of country and sees all of it slowly.

"There's two of them," she said.

They had been walking an hour. The grade had come up off the flat onto a low swell of ground and the whole country ahead had opened out: fields gone to rough pasture, a line of willow where water was, and the embankment running away north-west through the middle of it in the same dead-straight line it had held for two hundred miles.

"Two of what?" said Milo.

"Two lines." Senna lifted her chin off the blanket. "Look at the far side of the willows and stop looking at the near side. There's the bank we're on, and there's another one."

Toren looked.

It took him a while, and then he could not stop seeing it. Past the willows the ground rose again, and on the rise there were two banks, not one — the one they were walking on, holding its north-west line, and a second one peeling away from it in a long shallow curve, bending east, holding its own level across the slope until it went behind a shoulder of hill and was gone.

"That's a fork," said Dessa.

"It's a junction," Wyck said. "It's where the line split."

He had stopped in the middle of the grade with his stick planted. He was looking at it the way he looked at things: for longer than anyone else, with his head slightly down, as though the country were a page of writing that could be gotten wrong.

"What's at the bottom of it," he said.

Nobody answered him. Toren turned around, which was what he had already done with the front half of himself, and Rook was thirty paces back with the shafts of the barrow on his shoulders and his eyes on the willows, and he had stopped walking.



He started again about four seconds later and nobody but Toren saw it, and Toren was not sure afterward whether it had been four seconds or one, only that it had been a pause and that in a month of walking behind the man he had never seen him make one.

They went down through the willows and came up the other side and the junction was a mile and a half in front of them by noon.

It was a considerable piece of work.

That was the first thing about it, and it was the thing Toren had not expected. He had been thinking of a fork in the way you think of a fork in a road — a place where the ruts divide and somebody has scratched an arrow on a stone. This was a shape somebody had built. The single bank came up onto a broad flat and opened out into a triangle of level ground the size of the whole of Coldwater, all of it raised, all of it hand-laid, and at the far corners of the triangle two banks went out — the left one holding north-west, the right one swinging east in a curve so long and so gradual that you could stand in the middle of it and believe you were still going straight.

In the middle of the triangle, there was a brick box with no roof on it.

Along the east side there was a round stone pad thirty feet across with four bolt-stumps standing out of it, and a shallow basin in the ground beside that, filled with water and green weed, that had been something before it was a pond.

There was not one piece of iron left anywhere on any of it.

"Mother's mercy," said Senna, from the barrow, in the tone of a woman at a market. "Look at the size of that."



They did not go to it.

Rook took the barrow off the crown of the grade a mile short, on the north side, where a hedge line came up to the bank and made a corner out of the wind, and he put the shafts down in the grass and said, "We'll stop here."

It was one o'clock in the afternoon.

Milo said, "It's one o'clock in the afternoon."

"It is."

"We've done four hours. We do nine."

"We'll do four." Rook went round the barrow and started on the wheel chock without being asked to by anybody, including the wheel. "Faust, get the stove out. Cole, water — there's a ditch behind the hedge and it'll want boiling. Tanner, sit down and get the boot off that leg before I tell you twice."

Toren stood on the grade and looked at the ground Rook had picked.

He had been on that grade for a month. He had watched a man choose a camp every single night of it, and had had the reasons explained to him exactly once and had worked out the rest by watching: high enough to see, low enough not to be seen, water inside two hundred paces, a back you did not have to hold, and never, ever a place where the only way out was the way in.

This was a corner between a hedge and a bank. There was one way out of it and it was along the grade.

Nobody else said anything about it. Dessa went for the water and Milo got the stove out and Wyck sat down on the grass and started on his bootlace, and Toren stood where he was for another moment, and then went and helped with the canvas.



Rook walked out to the junction at five in the afternoon.

He did not say he was going. He put his coat on, which he had taken off, and went up onto the crown of the grade and started along it, and he was four hundred paces out before Milo looked up and said, "Where's he gone?"

"He's walking," said Dessa.

"He's not walking, he's—"

"He's walking, Milo."

Toren went up the bank and sat on the crown of it with his arms round his knees, and watched.

He could see the whole of it from there. He watched the small grey figure go away down the line and get smaller and arrive, after a long time, at the near corner of the triangle, and stop.

And then stand.

Toren had watched Rook stand still on twenty nights. He had watched him stand still on the bank at the wayhouse and in the yard at Tallow Gate and at the mouth of the arch in the rain, and every single one of those times the stillness had been a man looking at something. There was a quality to it that Toren had never had a word for and had absolutely no trouble recognizing: the head moving in small increments, the weight going from one foot to the other, the sense of a thing being read.

This was not that.

The figure at the corner of the junction stood with its hands in its pockets and did not move its head at all. It did that for a length of time Toren could not have estimated afterward and which was long enough for the light to change, and then it walked out into the middle of the triangle to the brick box with no roof, and stood there, and then it came back.



He got in at dusk and ate what Milo gave him and said nothing about any of it.

"Is it a good line?" Milo asked. "The other one. Is it—"

"They're both good lines."

"Where do they go?"

Rook ate.

Milo waited for four or five seconds, which for Milo Faust was a period of tremendous self-discipline, and then said, "Right," and got up and went to see to the stove, and Toren watched Dessa watch him go.



"He's not going to sleep," Wyck said.

It was full dark and the fire was down and the two of them were sitting against the bank with the hedge behind them taking the wind off. Wyck had his bad leg out straight and his hands laced on his stomach and his head back against the earth.

"He does sleep," Toren said.

"He sleeps like a man on a watch. Three hours, on his back, against a wheel, boots on. He's done it every night since the Gate." Wyck did not open his eyes. "He's not doing it tonight. He's been up on that bank since he came in and he's been standing in the same place, and I've been lying here not looking at him for about an hour, and I'd like somebody else to have noticed so I can stop feeling like I'm the mad one."

"I've noticed."

"Good."

The fire ticked. Out past it, the barrow was a black shape with Senna's breathing going in and out of it, and Milo was flat on his back with an arm over his face, and Dessa was up at the top of the camp with her boots on, holding.

"I'll tell you what I keep coming back to," Wyck said. "On the loop road he walked off the crown at a place with no turning. There was no mark. There was nothing. He came off a road at a spot in the grass like a man going through a door in his own house, and he's been on this line ever since, and he's never once looked at it like he wasn't sure."

"I know."

"He looked at it today."

Toren did not say anything.

"That's all," said Wyck. "I'm not building anything out of it. I'm just telling you what I saw, because I'd rather two of us saw it." He shifted the leg with both hands. "The other thing I'd say is that there's a fork out there, and a fork's a choice, and a man who's walked two hundred miles on purpose to get to a choice has already made it. So he isn't standing up there deciding."

"Then what's he doing?"

"I don't know," said Wyck Tanner. "That's the part I don't like."



Toren went up at some point after the middle of the night, because he could not sleep either, and because he had worked out about an hour before that he had not spoken to the man since noon.

Rook was on the crown of the grade with his back to the camp.

He did not turn round. Toren stopped six feet short of him and stood there, and the wind came across the flat, and the two banks out at the junction were faintly visible in the dark as two lines of nothing where the grass did not catch what light there was.

"Go to sleep," Rook said.

"Is something coming?"

"No."

"Then—"

"Voss." The man's voice did not change at all. "There's nothing out there and nothing's coming and nobody's in any danger tonight. Go and lie down."

Toren stood there a moment longer.

He had known this man for thirty-four days. In that time, he had seen him refuse a warden, dig three graves, carry an old woman out of thornbrush, put his hands inside a boy's leg for eleven minutes, cross a flooded building faster than a thought, burn a piece of slate that would not burn, and say I don't know twice, both times about things that mattered.

He had never once seen him look like a man with nowhere to put his hands.

"All right," Toren said, and went back down the bank, and lay on his side in the grass with his coat over him, and did not sleep for a long time either.

When he did sleep the man was still up there.

When he woke at first light, he was still up there, and the fire was cold, and the barrow had not been loaded, and nobody was going anywhere.



Chapter 15: A Mile Short

They did not move that day.

Rook came down off the bank at seven in the morning and said they were staying, and did not give a reason, and then he did the thing that made it worse: he gave them all work.

Dessa went on holding, in shifts, out to two hundred feet and then out past it. Milo got sent down the grade at dusk with his light on a stone, which he had been looking forward to for a day and a half, and came back thrilled and empty-handed because nothing at all had walked up to it. Wyck was put on the leg — standing holds, sitting holds, the leg first and the light second and then the other way round — and got through it without complaint and sat down afterward with his face grey and his jaw set. Toren was sent up the grade two hundred paces to sit on the crown and watch the country, which was the job he had been given on the second day out of Tallow Gate and had not been given since.

It was a full, sensible, well-run day, and every single one of them knew exactly what it was for.

"He's keeping us busy," Milo said at noon, in the tone of a man who has discovered something and is not enjoying it.

"Yes," said Dessa.

"That's — he's never done that. He gives us things because they're the thing. He doesn't give us things so we've got something to do."

"No."

"So why's he—"

"Faust," said Wyck, from the grass, with his eyes shut, "you are going to say every part of this out loud over the next four hours and it will not help, and I'm asking you as a friend to do it further away from me."

Milo went and did it further away from him, which was the closest thing to a kindness anybody managed all morning.



Senna got hold of Toren in the middle of the afternoon by the simple method of telling him to bring her the water can and then not letting go of his wrist.

"Sit down."

"I'm supposed to be—"

"You're supposed to be on a bank looking at an empty field and you've been doing it since seven and the field is still empty. Sit down."

He sat down on the tailboard.

His grandmother had her leg out straight along the boards and the blanket up to her chest and her hands folded on top of it, and she looked, in the flat afternoon light, worse than she had at Tallow Gate. That was a thing Toren had been carefully not looking at for about two weeks. She had got thinner in the face in a way that made her eyes bigger and she coughed the same small dry three-beat cough she had coughed his whole life, and he had started, recently, to hear it at night and lie still and wait for the fourth beat.

"How long has he been standing up there?" she said.

"Since yesterday evening."

"Mm." Senna looked out at the junction. "You know what I keep thinking about? The pen."

"What pen?"

"At the Gate. That woman put a ledger on a desk and put a pen on top of it and he picked the pen up." She resettled her hands. "He held it for a good while. He held it long enough that I had time to work out what it would mean for me if he signed it, which is that I'd have been left in that yard, and four days after that I'd have been left on the road outside it, and I made my peace with that in about the time it takes to boil water, because I'm eighty-one years old and I've had practice."

Toren's throat closed.

"Gran—"

"Don't. I'm not asking you for anything, I'm telling you something." She turned her head and looked at him. "He put the pen down. He didn't sign it and he didn't explain it and he's never once mentioned it since, and I have thought about that pen every single day for a month. Whatever's going on up on that bank, boy — whatever it is — it's the same thing as the pen. I'd put money on it if I had any."



Toren went to him at dusk.

He had been building himself up to it since the middle of the afternoon and by the time he actually went he had stopped building and had simply started walking, which was, he thought afterward, the only way he ever managed anything.

Rook was sitting on the crown of the grade with his forearms on his knees. He had a stone in one hand and he was turning it over, and he did not look up.

Toren took the disk out of his pocket and held it out.

"Take it," he said.

The stone stopped turning.

"Put that away."

"No." Toren's hand was shaking, which made him furious. "You take it. I've thought about it all day and it's the only thing in any of this that's actually mine to do, so take it."

"Voss—"

"You wouldn't sign the ledger and you wouldn't say why. You put me at point every day and you won't say why. You burned a board off a wall and you won't say why. You've been standing on this bank for a day and a night and everybody down there is pretending they can't see you." He was not shouting. He had expected to shout and he was not, and his voice had gone somewhere thin and flat instead. "And every single one of those things started at a sluice with this in your hand. So take it. You carry it. Wyck's had a hole in his life for a month because of it and he doesn't even know that's what it is, and I'm not — I can't—"

He stopped, because his voice had gone.

Rook had not moved.

"And if you won't take it," Toren said, "then there's a river at the bottom of the east line. Dessa saw the willows. I'll walk down there tomorrow and I'll put it in, and that's what my father told me to do with it, and I'll have done one thing he asked."



The wind came across the flat and moved the grass on the bank and went on.

"When did he tell you that," Rook said.

"The night I was Kindled. The same night he gave me the Ember." Toren's arm was still out. He could not seem to put it down. "He said it isn't a gift, it's a debt. He said somebody handed it to us and nobody's ever been able to put it down. And he said if it ever came to a choice between that and me, I should put it in a river and not tell anybody where."

"And what did you say?"

"I said yes."

"Were you lying?"

"Yes," said Toren. "He knew. Neither of us said so."

Rook set the stone down on the ballast beside him, carefully, as though it mattered where it went.

"There was a river three miles up the loop road," he said. "The morning after we buried them. You'd have been on the plank bridge with the litter poles."

Toren's whole chest went cold.

"I stood on it," he said. "The others went past me. I stood there with my hand in my pocket and I didn't take it out, and then I walked on, and I've felt sick about that since the day it happened, and I've never told anybody, and I don't know which half I feel sick about. Whether it's that I thought about it. Or that I didn't."

"You were nine days into being an orphan and you stood on a bridge and had a hard thought and then you walked," said Rook. "There's no version of that you owe anybody an apology for."

"He asked me for one thing—"

"He asked you for one thing and he asked you for it four hours after you'd got the thing in your hand, and he'd had it his whole life and never done it either, and neither did his father, and neither did hers." Rook stood up. "Five of them, Voss. Five generations of your people have stood on a bridge with that in their pocket. Every single one of them walked on. You're not the one who's weak. You're the one who's fifth."



Toren stood on the bank with his hand out and the disk on his palm and the wind coming across it, and understood, about two seconds after the man had stopped talking, that he had just been told something.

"You know what it is," he said.

"No."

"You just said—"

"I said I know what it does to people who carry it." Rook put his hands in his pockets. "I don't know what it is. Nobody does. I've spent a long time around the only people who ever seriously tried to find out and I can tell you exactly what they had at the end of it, which was less than your father had."

"Then tell me that."

"No."

"Then take it—"

"I can't take it." Something came into the man's voice that had not been in it before, and it was not anger, and it was gone again almost at once. "Put it away. Put it in the pocket with two rows of stitches in it that you sewed by a fire in a yard because I told you to, and don't ever offer it to another living soul the way you just offered it to me, because I'm the only one on this road who'd say no."

Toren put it away.

His hand was still shaking. He got it into the pocket and got his hand out of the pocket and stood there with both arms at his sides and nothing at all left in him.

"I'm going to ask you one thing," he said, "and then I'll go down and I won't ask again."

"Ask it."

"Is it my fault?"

Rook turned his head.

"Is what your fault."

"All of it." Toren's voice cracked in the middle and he pushed through the crack. "You were crossing a road. You said so. You weren't on it, you were crossing it, and you'd have walked us to a wall and left us there and gone back to whatever you were doing, and you tried, you had the pen in your hand — and then you pulled that out of a sluice and you didn't go. You've had four of us round your neck for a month and a woman in a barrow and a boy with a leg, and you were supposed to be somewhere, and you're standing on a bank in the dark looking at a fork in a railway, and I'm the reason. Aren't I."



The light had gone out of the west entirely. Down in the corner between the hedge and the bank there was the small red eye of the fire, and three shapes around it, and one of them was standing up.

Rook looked down at them for a long moment.

"Get your coat," he said.

"What?"

"Get your coat. It'll be cold out there and we'll be a while." He came down off the crown of the grade past Toren without stopping. "And tell them we're walking up to the junction and we'll be back before the middle of the night, and tell them in those words, and don't make anything of it, because they'll make enough on their own."



They made a great deal of it, and they did it almost entirely without speaking, which was worse.

Milo said, "Now?" and then, "It's dark," and then nothing else at all, and went and sat down on the far side of the stove with his back to both of them.

Dessa said, "How long?"

"He said before the middle of the night."

"Right." She had her arms crossed. She looked at Toren and then past him at the shape of a man waiting up on the crown of the grade, and then back. "Do you know what it's about?"

"No."

"Do you know what it's about, Toren."

"No," he said, and it was true, and he could hear exactly how it sounded, and there was nothing whatsoever he could do about that.

Dessa nodded once and went and sat down by Senna and did not look at him again.

Wyck was at the top of the camp with his stick, standing.

Toren went past him because there was no way out of the corner except along the grade and Wyck was on it, and he had got two steps past when Wyck said, quietly, "Voss."

He stopped.

"I'm not asking," Wyck said.

"I know."

"I want you to know that I know it's tonight. Whatever it is. He hasn't slept, and he's kept us busy all day like a man getting a house ready, and now he's standing up there with his coat on waiting for you." His voice was completely level. "So I know. And I'm still not asking. And I'd like that on the record, because in about three hours you're going to come back down here knowing something, and I'd rather have said it before than after."

Toren could not think of one single thing to say to him.

"Go on," said Wyck. "He's waiting."



They walked the mile out to the junction in the dark and neither of them said anything the whole way.

The triangle of raised ground was pale under a sky with no moon in it. Rook went straight across the middle of it to the brick box with no roof and put his hand on the wall and went round to the lee side, out of the wind, where there was a step and a fall of old brick to sit on, and sat down.

Toren sat down at the other end of the step.

From there he could see both of the banks going out. The left one ran away north-west, flat and straight and pale, until it stopped being visible. The right one curved east through a long shallow bend and went behind the shoulder of the hill.

Rook took his Ember out of his coat and rolled it once against his palm, which he did when he was about to use it, and then did not light it. He held it in both hands with his forearms on his knees and turned it over slowly, twice, and then held it still.

"You asked me if it was your fault," he said.

"Yes."

"It isn't. And you're going to think it is anyway for a while, because I'm about to hand you a reason to, and I can't help that." He looked out at the left-hand line. "The rest of them are going to know I told you something tonight and they're never going to know what. That's going to cost you with them. I'd take it off you if there was a way and there isn't."

"I know."

"You don't. You will." Rook turned the Ember over once more in his hands. "Before the Fall there were places that did research. Universities, laboratories, whatever you want to call them. Almost all of it went in the first ten years, either burned or taken apart for the copper. A few of them didn't. A few of them were built in the wrong century, out of too much concrete, in a hole in the ground, and those came through, and people found them again, and one or two of them got started up a second time by people who'd decided the question was worth it."

He stopped.

Toren waited.

"There was one of those," Rook said, "in a bowl of hills about four hundred miles north of where you're sitting, and they ran it for eleven years, and I was there for six of them, and the place was called Homura."



Chapter 16: Homura

"Say it back," Rook said.

"Homura."

"That's it. It's not a word from around here. It came with the people who named it and they got it from a long way further back than that, and I'm not going to pretend I know more about it than I do, which is that it means a flame." He turned the Ember over in his hands. "Somebody there thought it was clever. It was. It wore off."

Toren sat on the brick step with his coat pulled round him and did not move.

He had been prepared, walking out along the grade, for a great many things. He had been prepared for a secret and for a warning and for the thing about the disk. He had not been prepared for the man to sound the way he sounded, which was not solemn at all — which was the flat, slightly worn voice of somebody giving directions to a place he has been asked about too often.

"What was it?"

"It was a research station."

"Researching what?"

"Us," said Rook.



He told it in a particular order, and the order was not the order of the story, and it took Toren most of the night to understand why: the man was going around it. He would start somewhere at the edge, in the practical, in the plumbing and the roofs and the hours, and work inward as far as he could get, and then stop and go back out to the edge and start again from a different side.

He started with the buildings.

"Four of them in a bowl of hills with a ridge on three sides, so you couldn't see it from anywhere except directly above. Two above ground and two under. The two above ground were offices and sleeping quarters and a kitchen and they'd been rebuilt about four times, because they were made of the sort of thing people make buildings out of when they're in a hurry. The two underneath were the reason anybody went to the trouble. Concrete a yard thick and no windows and a generator hall with its own water off the hill, and everything in them still worked, because whoever put them there in the first place had been frightened of exactly what happened."

"They had power?"

"They had power. Not much and not all the time. They ran the instruments in the morning because that's when the head was highest in the tank."

"What instruments?"

"Machines that look at the inside of a head," Rook said. "There were three that worked. You lay on your back on a table and they ran you into a tube about so wide" — he put his hands up, a little wider than his own shoulders — "and you stayed there for the better part of an hour and it was very loud and very cold and nothing hurt and nothing happened, and then you got up and went and had your breakfast, and somewhere on the other side of the wall there was a picture of the inside of you with something lit up in it."

Toren sat with that.

"That's it?"

"That's it. That's what it was, nearly all of the time. It was the dullest place I have ever lived in my life." Rook shifted on the step. "People hear research station and they see something out of a story. It was sixty people in a hole in a hill being bored and cold and hungry and doing the same thing every morning for years, and the whole of what it was for happened in a room they weren't allowed into, on paper."



"How did you get there?"

"They came and got me. They had four people whose entire job was walking around the country asking after anyone who could do anything, and one of them turned up at my village when I was nineteen."

"And you went?"

"I went for the money," said Rook, "except there wasn't any money, so I went for the thing they gave instead of money."

"What did they give?"

"A charter." He said it the way Warden Braid had said I signed something. "Protection and medicine for the place you came from, for as long as you stayed and for five years after you left. Written down, witnessed, and they honored it, which not everybody would have. They had a physician out on a circuit and four men with rifles who were not for show, and my village got both of those for six years because I lay in a cold tube every morning and let them take a picture of the inside of my head."

Toren did not say anything.

He was thirteen years old and he had been on a road for five weeks with a dying woman in a cart, and the shape of what he had just been handed arrived in him whole and did not need any explaining at all: a family at the far end of something, and a person who had gone and done a thing they did not want to do, so that somebody at that far end could have a doctor.

"That's the same," he said.

Rook did not answer.

"That's the same thing we were doing. That's exactly the — my father and Odo and all of us, we were eleven days out from Meridian because there's a machine there that can see the whole of her at once. That's the same thing."

"Yes."

"Is that why you—"

"No," Rook said. "Don't do that. It's not that tidy and I won't have you making it tidy tonight, because you'll be stuck with whatever you make." He turned the Ember over. "It's the same shape. Half the people on this continent are somewhere inside that shape. It doesn't mean anything about you and me."

"But you noticed it."

"I noticed it on the loop road inside the first hour," said Rook, "and I did not enjoy it."



"What did they find?"

"One thing," Rook said. "They found one thing and they were certain of it and they spent eleven years trying to get anybody to care."

He looked out at the pale line of the left-hand bank running away north-west, and then he did the thing with his hands: he stopped turning the Ember over and held it still, in both of them, the way you hold something you have decided not to fidget with.

"Put an Ember-user in the tube," he said. "You get a picture. There's a place at the back and underneath, about the size of a walnut, and it's lit, and the rest is dark. Take him out. Put a magic-user in — somebody like Cole, somebody like Faust, somebody who's never touched a device in their life and thinks the rift reached through a hole in the sky and handed them a present."

"And it's a different place."

"It's the same place," said Rook. "It's the same place down to the last thing the machine can tell apart. Not similar. Not near. The same."

Toren had known that. He had been told it under a stone arch in the rain four weeks ago by this same man, flat, once, and he had believed it inside three sentences and had gone out at dusk and stood in the wet grass and been unable to do a single thing with it.

He had not known that somebody had photographed it.

"They had it in a picture," he said.

"They had it in about nine thousand pictures. They had it in men and women and children, in people from three regions who'd never met each other, in an old man who'd been Ember forty years and a girl of fourteen who lit a candle with her hand, and it came out the same every time for eleven years, and there is no argument anywhere in the world that beats that." Rook's voice had not changed at all. "It wasn't a theory. That's what I want you to have. Everything else they had was a theory. That one wasn't."

"What else did they have?"

"Theories. Some of them good." He shrugged one shoulder. "They had a decent one about why the ash burns clean. You know how it works — you can't take power off a person and put it in a lamp, because it's already somebody's, and it has to be lent and it has to go back, and it always costs more going back than it did coming out. Rift-things aren't anybody's. Nobody's home in them. That's the whole of the difference and it's why hunting is what everybody does for a living out here instead of just what kills them." He paused. "They had more than that. They had the beginning of a thing about how much of a person is lit and what it buys. I'm not giving you that one tonight."

"Why not?"

"Because it's four hours and you've got about two in you," said Rook, "and because I'd rather teach it to you in a field than hand it to you on a step."



The wind came round the corner of the brick box and Toren pulled his coat in, and out on the flat something small went through the grass and stopped.

"Was it you?" he said.

"Was what me."

"You said they found one thing and they were certain of it. And you said you were there six years, and you're the only person I've ever met who can — " He stopped. He tried again. "You do both of them. Everybody says one at a time. You came down a hill with light round your legs and a spear in your hand and I've been thinking about that since the day it happened, and nobody I've ever asked has heard of it. Was that them? Did they do that to you?"

Rook laughed.

It was a short, entirely unmusical sound, one breath out through the nose, and it was the first time Toren had heard anything like it from him.

"No," he said. "Nothing like it. They couldn't do anything to anybody. They were sixty people with three machines and a generator and no money, and half of what they did all day was keeping the roof on. Nobody at Homura ever made a single person able to do a single thing."

"Then how—"

"They measured," said Rook. "That's all they did. They were very good at it and they were honest about it and they never once said more than they had, and it was the best work anybody's done in this country since the Fall, and it was measuring." He turned the Ember over. "What I can do, I learned. It took years and most of it was miserable and none of it came out of a machine."

"Who taught you?"

The wind went over the top of the brick box.

"That's the other half of it," Rook said.



"There were sixty of us. About a third were magic, about a third Ember, and about a third were the other kind — people with no device and no rift in them at all who'd sat down at nineteen and started working on the inside of their own head and had got somewhere by being extremely stubborn for a decade. They're rarer than either of the other two and they're the ones nobody ever talks about, and Homura had eleven of them, which as far as I know is the most that have ever been in one building."

"Which were you?"

"Magic. My mother's side. Everybody in that valley was." He said it without any particular emphasis. "I'd never held an Ember in my life. I thought they were a superstition. I'd have told you that to your face at nineteen and been slightly pleased with myself about it, and about a third of the station thought the same about me from the other direction, and we all ate in the same room twice a day and were perfectly civil and thought each other fools."

"That's stupid," said Toren.

"It is. It's also what the whole world does, and the whole world has been doing it for seventy years, and it is the entire reason anybody bothered to build that place." Rook shifted. "And it is very hard to keep doing at close quarters, because you run out of ways to explain the other lot, and eventually somebody says something at a table and you have to actually look at them."

"Who said something?"

"She did," said Rook.



He did not say the name for a while.

He put both elbows on his knees and held the Ember out in front of him in both hands and looked at it, and Toren sat at the other end of the step and did not breathe very much.

"There was a boy there," Rook said. "Sixteen, Ember, from the east coast. Good with it — better than Tanner at the same age and Tanner's good. And they had him in the tube one morning, and they brought his picture out, and one of my lot — one of the magic side, a man I liked — looked at it and said, out loud, in the kitchen, that it was a shame the machine couldn't show the difference between a man's own power and a stick's.

"And a woman at the next table put her cup down and said, without looking up: It can. It shows there isn't one. That's what the picture is."

He stopped.

"And the man said, what's that supposed to mean. And she said, it means you've read the same nine thousand pictures I have and you've decided you didn't. And the man laughed and it turned into an argument, and the argument was the usual one and it went round the usual six corners and got nowhere, and I sat there through the whole of it not saying anything, because I had also read them, and I had also decided I hadn't."

The wind moved the grass out on the flat.

"Her name was Vera," Rook said.



Toren did not say anything. There did not seem to be a place to put anything.

"She'd been there two years longer than me. Ember. Her people were a clan off the northern coast — they'd come over three or four generations before the Fall and they'd kept nearly all of it, the language in pieces and the way they laid out a table and about two hundred words nobody else on that continent had, and then the Fall came and they kept all of that too, which is harder than keeping a town. They were the best Ember-users I have ever seen. I don't say that because of her. I say it because they trained children on the same three movements for eleven years before anybody was allowed a fourth, and it showed."

"Did she teach you?"

"She taught me the Ember." Rook turned it over once. "Poorly at first, because she'd never taught anybody who hadn't started at six, and she kept telling me things that only make sense if you've already got eleven years of it. I was twenty-two and I could throw a wall of air across fifty feet of hillside and I could not light a piece of metal in my own hand, and it took me four months, and she found the whole thing extremely funny."

"What was she like?"

He was quiet for long enough that Toren thought he had asked the wrong thing.

"She corrected people," Rook said. "All the time. Everybody. Not unkindly and never once for the pleasure of it — she just could not sit in a room and let a wrong thing stand, and she'd been like it since she was a child, and it had cost her. She talked with her hands and then she'd notice she was doing it and put them under the table. She was frightened of deep water and would not say so. She ate the crust first." He stopped, and then went on in exactly the same voice. "She was about as tall as Cole. She had a scar on the back of her left hand from a stove when she was nine and she'd tell you the story if you asked and it wasn't a good story, and she'd tell it anyway.

"And she was the only person in that building who'd worked the whole thing out, and she'd worked it out years before I got there, and she never once got any credit for it, because she was a subject and not staff and the paper had other names on it."

"Worked what out?"

"That it goes both ways," said Rook. "Everybody at Homura had the picture. The picture says the two traditions light the same place. Everybody in that building agreed with that, out loud, at meals, for eleven years. And every last one of them still went out in the afternoon and practiced the one thing they'd been doing since they were thirteen, because agreeing with something is free." He looked at the Ember in his hands. "She was the first person I ever met who tried the other door. Just — tried it. Went and sat in a field for two years and tried it, because the picture said the door was in the same house and nobody had gone and looked."

"Did it work?"

"Eventually," Rook said. "For her and then for me and for nobody else there, and it took both of us the better part of four years, and every single person in that station could have done it and not one of them did, and I have thought about that more than I have thought about anything else in my life."



They sat for a while. The brick was cold through Toren's coat. Out past the triangle of raised ground the two banks went away in their two directions and neither of them looked like anything in the dark.

"Can I ask something that isn't about any of it?" Toren said.

"Ask."

"The bread."

Rook's hands went still on the Ember.

"You put a piece of the first one on a rock," Toren said. "Every night since the road. Milo asked you the first time and you said it wasn't for anything, and then you said I know what I did, and nobody's asked you since. And Milo does it now. He's been doing it for about ten days and I'm fairly sure he doesn't know he started."

"I know he does."

"Is it a thing you're supposed to do?"

"It's a thing she did," said Rook.

He said it perfectly evenly, and he did not look up from his hands, and Toren watched a piece of the last five weeks come apart and put itself back together in a completely different shape.

"Her clan's. Every meal, the first piece off, set aside, and you don't eat it and you don't throw it away and nobody explains it to you. I asked her once what it was for. She said her grandmother had never told her either and that she'd stopped asking at about eleven." Rook turned the Ember over. "I've done it every night for nine years. I couldn't tell you what it means. I'm not sure it means anything. I know what happens if I stop."

Toren sat in the cold with his arms round his knees.

"There's more of that," he said. "Isn't there. Things you do that we all think are just how you are."

"Most of it," said Rook.



Chapter 17: Hers

"Did she have a word for it?" Toren said.

"For what?"

"For the thing you teach us. The room. Going all the way through." He had his chin on his knees. "You told Milo it hasn't got a name. You said the names are all on the doors and you'd rather not hand us one more word to be right about. But she'd have had one, wouldn't she. Two hundred words nobody else has."

Rook was quiet for a while.

"Satori," he said.

It was an ordinary sound. Toren had expected, without knowing he expected it, that it would be a large one.

"What does it mean?"

"It means you saw it. Not that you're strong and not that you've learned anything — that you looked at the thing everybody's been standing next to their whole life and you actually saw it, and it went all the way in." Rook turned the Ember over. "It's not one of the words her clan invented after the Fall. It's old. They carried it over with the rest. She said it was a word about a moment, and that the moment isn't long, and that everything before it is preparation and everything after it is housekeeping."

"Is that what you did? In the field. For four years."

"That's what she did in a field for two years, and then she told me how, and then it took me another two, and I have never once managed to say it to anybody in a way that made it happen." He put his hands flat on the Ember and stopped moving them. "I've tried it on you four different ways in a month. I'll keep trying."

"Why don't you use the word?"

"Because it's hers," said Rook, "and because I've watched what happens to a true thing when it gets a name people can be right about. That's all of it. You've had it once. Don't repeat it."

"I won't."

"I know you won't," he said, and then, in exactly the same voice: "They came in the afternoon."



"It was the sixteenth of the eighth month, nine years ago, at about half past three. Everybody was up. It wasn't dark and nobody was asleep and there wasn't any of that." Rook's hands were still on the Ember. "I was in the water room. There was a valve on the feed off the hill that had been dripping for a fortnight and I'd said I'd look at it twice and not done it, and that morning she'd said, out loud, at breakfast, in front of eleven people, that I was the only man she'd ever met who could hold a spear together with his mind and could not tighten a nut.

"So I was under the tank with a wrench.

"That's where I was. That's the whole reason I'm sitting here. There is no other reason. If she hadn't said that at breakfast I'd have been in the east building at half past three with everybody else."

Toren did not move.

"They came over the ridge on the west side and down through the gate, and they didn't come quietly and they didn't need to. There were about thirty of them. They had rifles, which almost nobody has and almost nobody has ammunition for, and they had four people who could work, and one of those was better than anybody we had.

"It took about forty minutes. I'm telling you that because everybody always thinks it takes longer.

"I was outside. I came up out of the water room into the yard about two minutes in, and I got as far as the top of the steps, and after that I was in it, and I can't give you the order of it. I've tried. I've had nine years and I can't. I've got about eleven things and none of them join up."

"You don't have to—"

"I'm not going to," said Rook. "You don't need them and I'm not handing them to a boy on a step at night."



"She was in the instrument room," he said. "Under the east building. That's where she'd have been at that hour, and she was, and I couldn't get down there for a long time, and by the time I could it had been a long time.

"She'd have gone down instead of out. I want you to understand why, because it's the only part of this I'd want anybody to know about her. There were nine thousand pictures in that room and eleven years of paper with them and she was the one who'd read all of it, and when the shooting started she went down.

"She wasn't trying to save herself and she wasn't trying to save anybody else. She'd made a judgment about which of the two things in that hill was harder to replace and she went and stood in front of it."

The wind came round the corner of the brick.

"Did she—"

"She was already gone when I got there," Rook said. "That's all of that you're getting and it's all there is. I sat down on the floor for a while. I don't know how long, and there wasn't anybody left to ask."



"They burned the paper."

He said it in the same voice, and it took Toren a moment to hear that something in it had changed.

"That's the part I'd get wrong if I let myself. They didn't take it. If they'd taken it, somebody would have it, and paper in somebody's hands is paper that can be got at again. They pulled it off the shelves and stacked it in the corridor and burned it in the corridor, which is a thing you do when you have been told to.

"Eleven years. Nine thousand pictures and the notes that went with them and four people's whole working lives. The only proof anybody has ever assembled that the two halves of this world are the same thing wearing two coats, and it went up in a passage under a hill, and there has not been one page of it anywhere on this continent since the sixteenth of the eighth month, nine years ago."

Toren said, "Who?"

"I didn't know for four years."

"But you know now."

"I know now," said Rook.

He did not say it. He turned the Ember over in his hands once, and looked out at the pale line of the left-hand bank going away north-west into nothing, and Toren, sitting three feet from him in the cold, felt the whole of the last three weeks come round and click into place like a wheel dropping into a rut: a burned wayhouse, an empty rack of cradles, three graves dug properly by people with all afternoon, and a man reading dates off a slate in a voice with nothing whatsoever in it.

"Cinder Company," Toren said.

"Yes."



"There's a second half of it and it's worse, and I'm going to give it to you plainly and then we're not going to discuss it.

"I got out of Homura with about nine other people and we scattered, because that's what you do. I went home. It took me five weeks. My village was still standing and the charter still had two years on it, and the physician still came round, which I have never worked out, because there was nobody left to send him." Rook's voice was perfectly level. "I stayed two years. I was no use to anybody and they put up with me. And in the second winter a rift-tear opened about nine miles up the valley and a hunting company came through on contract to close it, and the fighting came down into the fields below the houses, and some of it came into the houses.

"They didn't mean it. That's not me being generous, it's a fact — it was a bad night in bad ground and they were shooting at things that fold up and come at whatever's nearest and there were people where nobody thought there'd be people. I was there. I saw the whole of it and there was nothing in it that anybody did on purpose.

"And it killed my mother and my brother and my brother's two children."

Toren's hands had gone numb.

"And I did not find out for another two years," Rook said, "that it was the same company."



He stopped.

Toren sat with his back against cold brick and the wind coming round the corner and could not think of one single thing to say that would not be an insult, so he did not say anything at all, which he understood later was the only correct thing he did that night.

"I buried my own people not knowing," Rook said. "I stood in that valley and I was grateful to them. Four of them helped dig. I have shaken hands with a man who was there at Homura and I did not know it, and he did not know me, and I have thought about which of those two things is worse and I have never got an answer."

"How did you find out?"

"A tally-board," said Rook. "In a trading post, about two hundred miles from here, five years ago. Somebody had written down what they'd sold and who'd bought it and on what day, because that is what people do, and the day was the sixteenth of the eighth month, and the place was eleven miles from a bowl of hills."

The wind moved.

"I've been on that line ever since," Rook said. "And I have never once found out who paid them, and I've stopped expecting to. Somebody wanted eleven years of paper in a corridor. Somebody worked out what that paper was going to do to the way this world holds itself apart, and decided against it, and had the money to decide. I don't know their name. I'm not going to."



Toren said, "Can I see it?"

"See what?"

"Your Ember."

Rook's hands stopped.

He had been holding the thing the entire night. Toren had watched him do it since the brick step — turning it over, holding it still, putting his palms flat on it, and Toren had thought, for about three hours, that it was what a man does with his hands when he cannot put them anywhere.

Rook held it out.

Toren took it.

It was heavier than his father's. That was the first thing. It was shorter and heavier and the taper at the ends was sharper, and there was a wrap of leather round the grip, dark and gone soft with handling, and it was worn through in one place near the base — one place, about the width of a thumb, down at the bottom of the grip where no part of a hand naturally goes.

Toren turned it over.

He had a family Ember in his own coat pocket that was worn glass-smooth in a band around the middle, where four generations of Voss thumbs had sat in exactly the same place, and he had had that explained to him at a fire under a broken overpass on the last good night of his life.

He put his own thumb where the leather was gone.

It did not reach. He had to turn his wrist to do it and the whole grip went wrong in his hand.

"That's not where you hold it," he said.

"No."

"That's not where anybody holds it." Toren's voice had stopped working properly. "You'd have to be — your hand would have to be smaller, or you'd have to hold it right down at the—"

"She was about as tall as Cole," said Rook.



Toren sat with the thing in both hands.

He had seen it come down a hillside as seven feet of spear. He had seen it laid flat across his own torn palms in a road, and flat along the whole length of Wyck Tanner's opened leg for eleven minutes while a boy with a useless light held his hand steady above it. He had watched it go into a coat pocket a hundred times and had never thought about it once, in the way you do not think about a man's boots.

"You've had it since—"

"I took it off her hand," Rook said. "In the instrument room. It was the only thing I took out of that hill and I didn't think about it either, I just did it, and then I was forty miles away before I worked out I had it." He took it back, without hurry, and put it into his coat. "I'd been Ember three years by then and I had one of my own and I left it on a table. I've never been able to say why in a way that sounds like a reason."

"You teach with it."

"Yes."

"You healed Wyck with it. And me. You've been—" Toren stopped and started again. "Every single thing you've done for any of us, for five weeks. You did it with hers."

"Yes," said Rook.



It was a long time before either of them said anything.

"There's one more," Rook said eventually. "And it's the one I'd have skipped if I could, because I don't have a way of saying it that doesn't sound like a man selling something at a market."

"Say it."

"About a year after Homura I started having the same dream."

Toren waited.

"I put it down to grief. Everybody does, and everybody's mostly right, and for two years I was right." Rook had his forearms on his knees again and his hands empty and open. "It didn't stop. It's not a picture and it's never been a picture. I've never seen a face in it and I've never seen a place. It's the way it is when you wake up at somebody's house and for one second you can't think where you are, except the other way about — I'd wake up knowing exactly where I was and knowing there was a person somewhere who hadn't been born yet or hadn't been found yet, and that they were carrying a flat dark thing with cuts across the face of it, and that when they turned up it was going to be mine to answer for.

"I've had it for eight years. Not every night. Often enough."

He said the next part very plainly.

"I did not want it. I want you to have that, because in about a week you're going to start wondering whether any of this was ever about you, and I'd rather you had the true version than a kind one. For eight years I have been walking a line with a company at the end of it, and about twice a season I've woken up in the dark with somebody else's obligation sitting on my chest, and I have resented it the way you'd resent a debt you didn't take out."

"And then the sluice," said Toren.

"And then the sluice."



"You came up the hill with your hand in your pocket," Rook said, "and you put your hand in your pocket and there was a hole in it, and you went straight back down into a flooded building on the worst day of that boy's life, alone, four days after I'd told all four of you that nobody goes anywhere alone. And I went after you to haul you out by the collar.

"And I came up out of that sluice with it on my hand and I couldn't make my hands stop, and I'd have told you it was the water and the cold and eleven minutes inside a leg, and you were three feet away and you'd been watching me for four days and you knew it wasn't."

"I knew it wasn't."

"I know." Rook looked at him for the first time in some while. "I'd like one thing on the record, because you asked me a question on that bank tonight and I've been carrying it out here for a mile and haven't answered it.

"I put that pen down at Tallow Gate two days before I ever saw what you had in your pocket. I didn't know. There was nothing in that office except a woman being fair and four children in a row, and I had the cleanest way out of my life in my hand and I put it down on the desk, and nobody made me. That one's mine. It is not yours and you don't get to take it off me." He put his hands back on his knees. "So: no. It isn't your fault. The ledger is the part that isn't, and you can have it, and I'd like you to actually keep it."

Toren's eyes had gone and he did not try to do anything about them.

"And point?"

"Point's yours," said Rook. "Point was the day after the sluice."



"Why wouldn't you tell me?"

"Because I didn't know what you were," Rook said. "I've had eight years of a thing on my chest with no face on it, and then there's a boy of thirteen in a road with a torn pocket, and I can tell you the exact shape of what I felt and I could not tell you one useful thing about what it meant. Whether you were the whole of it or a piece of it. Whether I was supposed to teach you or get you to Meridian or put you somewhere with a wall round you and walk away.

"So I did the only thing I could stand behind, which was to teach you as hard and as fast as I could get away with, and keep my mouth shut about the reason, and see what turned up." He was quiet a moment. "Tanner's paid for that. I know. He's paid for it in the only currency a boy that age has, which is being fifteen and not knowing why, and he's carried it better than I'd have carried it, and I'm not going to hand it to him to make myself feel better."

"He worked most of it out."

"I know he did."

"He told me he wasn't going to ask." Toren wiped his face on his sleeve. "He said if he asked me I'd lie to him and then he'd have that as well and he's got enough."

"That," said Rook, "is the single most grown-up thing that has happened on this grade since the fifth of the month, and I'd tell him so if telling him wouldn't cost him the rest of it."



The wind dropped, the way it does an hour or two before light, and the flat went quiet.

"Can I ask one more?"

"You've been asking all night. Ask it."

"If you catch them." Toren had his arms round his knees again. "If you get all the way down that line and you catch them and it goes the way you want it to. Does that — "

He stopped, because he could not find the end of the sentence.

"Does it what," said Rook.

"I don't know. Does it fix any of it?"

Rook looked out at the left-hand bank for a long time.

"They took forty-one people out of a hill," he said. "Four of them are mine, with the valley, and it's five if I'm honest about her, and there is a version of that which I can answer. Men do it. It's an old debt and everybody agrees what it costs and there's a road for it and I've been on that road for five years and I know exactly how to walk it.

"And then there's the other thing they took, which was eleven years of paper in a corridor, and I could kill every man in that company tomorrow with my own hands and it would not put back one page."

He stood up.

He did it the way he did everything, without any announcement, and he put his hands in his pockets and looked out across the triangle of raised ground at the two banks going out of it in their two directions.

"We'll want water in the morning," he said. "There's a ditch behind the hedge and it wants boiling twice, not once, and I'll do it, and you'll sleep till somebody kicks you."

He started back along the line.

Toren stayed where he was.

He sat on a step of broken brick in the last black hour of the night, in the middle of a flat triangle of ground that six hundred people had built by hand before the Fall, with the disk warm in a pocket sewn with two rows of stitches, and looked at the left-hand bank going away north-west, and at the right-hand bank curving east out of sight behind the shoulder of the hill.

He did not know which one they were taking.

He knew, now, what was down both of them, and he was the only person camped a mile back along that grade who did.



Chapter 18: East

The barrow was loaded when Toren woke.

That was the first thing he understood, lying on his side in the grass with his coat over him and the light coming up grey behind the hedge: the canvas was on, the bundles were in, the chock was out from under the wheel and stowed on its nail, and Senna was sitting up against the front board with her blanket round her shoulders and her hair done, which she did herself, every morning, with three pins, and which took her the better part of half an hour.

Rook was standing at the shafts with his back to the camp.

"Up," he said, without turning round. "All of you. We're walking out to that junction and then I'm going to tell you something, and I'd rather do it standing on it than a mile off."



They went out along the grade in the early light with the wind coming off the flat and nobody talking.

Toren walked at the back.

He had not been at the back since the loop road. He had been at point every day for four weeks and everybody in the party had an opinion about that, and this morning he had simply fallen in behind the barrow and nobody had said anything about it, which was worse than if somebody had.

Dessa walked at the off shaft with her hands in her pockets. She had said "clear" twice since they started, both times in the same flat voice, both times without turning her head, and she had not looked at him once.

Milo dropped back to him about halfway.

"Was it bad?" he said.

"Milo—"

"No, that's not — I'm not asking what it was. I know I'm not allowed and I've had it explained to me twice by Wyck and once by Dessa and I've worked it out on my own as well, which nobody credits me for." Milo had his hands shoved in his coat and his shoulders up round his ears. "I'm asking whether it was bad. That's a different question and it's about you and I'm allowed it."

Toren walked about ten paces.

"Yes," he said.

"Right," said Milo. "Right. Okay."

And he stayed at the back with him the rest of the way, talking about absolutely nothing, about a heron he had seen and about whether you could eat a heron and about whether you would want to even if you could, and Toren understood exactly what was being done for him and did not have anywhere to put it.



Rook took the barrow up onto the middle of the triangle and set the shafts down beside the round stone pad with the four bolt-stumps standing out of it, and turned round.

In the daylight you could see the whole of it at once. The single bank came in from the south-east behind them. The left-hand line went out north-west across a flat of rough pasture, dead straight, holding its level, until the eye lost it. The right-hand line curved east through its long shallow bend and dropped, very gradually, toward the shoulder of hill and whatever was behind it.

"Left-hand line," Rook said. "It runs four days north-west to a place called Undern. It's a market town on the edge of the hill country and its whole business is ash. Every hunting company in three hundred miles brings its season's take there to be weighed, and the companies that are big enough to have a roof of their own winter there, because the weighing goes on till the ground freezes and nobody wants to be paid twice.

"Cinder Company winters at Undern. They've wintered there for six years. They came through that wayhouse four months ago going north with four casks, and everything they do between now and the frost ends up on a scale in that town."

Nobody said anything at all.

"Right-hand line," Rook said, "runs east. It comes down off this flat in about eleven miles and meets a river called the Reave, and it follows the Reave for two days, and where the Reave turns south there's an old road that's still a road, and that road goes to Meridian.
