Nineteen days at the pace we make. Twenty-two if the fords are up."

He let that sit.

Toren watched the four of them take it. He watched it go into Milo, who opened his mouth and closed it. He watched Dessa's head come round. He watched Senna Voss, in the barrow, lift her chin off her blanket and look at the right-hand bank with an expression he had never seen on her face in thirteen years and never afterward forgot, because it was not hope. It was a woman doing arithmetic about her own body and getting an answer.

And he watched Wyck Tanner, who had not moved at all, and who was looking at the left-hand line.

"You've told us where we're going," Wyck said.

"I have."

"You've never done that. Not once. Not at the Gate, not when you walked us off the loop road, not for a month." He turned his head. "Why today?"

"Because today it's a choice," said Rook, "and I'm not going to make a choice for six people and let five of them find out about it from the shape of the country."



"What's down the left one?" Wyck said. "For you. Not the town. You."

Toren's whole chest went tight.

Rook looked at him for a moment, and then answered him, straight, in about the time it takes to breathe in.

"Men who killed people I was responsible for," he said. "Nine years ago and then again seven, and the second time I didn't know it was the same men until four years after that, and I've been walking toward them ever since. That's what's down the left one. You've had a name off a wall and a burned wayhouse and me not answering you, and you can have the rest of that much."

The wind came across the triangle.

"Is that all of it?" said Wyck.

"No."

"All right," said Wyck Tanner, and did not ask for the rest.



Senna said, "Put me down."

Everybody turned.

"I'm not being dramatic, I'm being practical, and I'd like it heard by everyone standing here so that nobody has to have it out with me later." She had both hands on the front board and she had pushed herself up straighter than Toren had seen her sit in three weeks. "That right-hand line is nineteen days of walking that you don't want to do and it is entirely about me. There's no other reason for it. The boy's not sick. Tanner's leg is closing. Nobody in this barrow needs a machine except the woman in it.

"So put me down. Not here — take me down the east line as far as the river, and there'll be a place on a river, there always is, and leave me at it the way you were going to leave all of us at Tallow Gate. I'd have a roof. I'd have a winter. It's a better end than most people get and considerably better than I had coming to me on the fifth of the month."

"No," said Rook.

"You haven't thought about it."

"I've thought about it for two days and a night," he said, "which is exactly as long as I've been standing on this bank, and I want you to hear that plainly, because you're owed it and because you'd know if I lied. I thought about it. I took it the whole way down and looked at it. And the answer's no."

"Why?"

"Because you'd do it," Rook said. "You'd get out of that barrow at a landing on the Reave and you'd send the boy on and you would not make one single sound about it, and every one of them would spend the rest of their lives knowing they went on. That is not a cost you get to pay by yourself. You've been trying to hand it round since the road and I'm not taking any."

Senna Voss sat in the barrow with her hands on the board.

"That's the same answer you gave the warden," she said.

"It's the same answer I gave the warden."



He picked up the shafts.

He did it without any ceremony at all, the way he did it every morning, and the barrow came up out of the grass, and he turned it.

He turned it east.

That was the whole of it. There was no line said and no look given and nothing to see except a man swinging two wooden shafts through a quarter-turn and starting to walk, and Toren stood on the triangle and watched him do it and felt something go over in him that he could not have described to anybody.

They had gone perhaps twenty paces when Wyck said, "Rook."

He stopped.

"The four months," Wyck said. "From the board. That was four months when we found it, and that was ten days ago."

"Yes."

"And nineteen days east, and whatever's at the other end, and then back."

"It's a year," Rook said. "Give or take. Undern's four days from here and it'll be four days from here when I come back to it, and by then they'll have wintered and split and hired on and half of them will be in a different company with a different name on the ledger, and after a year it isn't a trail. It's a rumor about a rumor, and I've walked those, and they don't come to anything."

"So you might not get them."

"I might not get them."

Wyck stood there in the middle of the junction with his stick planted and his bad leg braced and looked at the man for a long moment.

"Right," he said.

And he started walking east, at the back, at his own speed, and that was the end of it.



It was a good day and Toren could not feel any part of it.

The right-hand line came off the flat in a long easy fall, and the country softened as it went, and by the middle of the afternoon there were hedges with things growing in them and a smell in the air that Toren did not recognize for nearly an hour and then placed, with a jolt, as cut hay.

Somebody was farming out there. Somebody was farming within a mile of this bank.

Dessa said "clear" every ten minutes in her flat voice and did not speak to him.

At about four, she fell back and walked beside him for a hundred paces without saying anything, and then said, "I'm not angry at you."

"You are."

"I'm angry, and it isn't at you, and it took me all day to get those two things apart so don't make me do it again." She had her hands in her pockets and her eyes front. "I'm angry because he took you out there and not me. And I've had all day to look at that and it's the ugliest thing I've ever found in myself, because my mother went up a bank for a boy who isn't you and I've never once been angry about that, and I've been angry about this since dusk yesterday."

"Dessa—"

"I'm his second," she said. "Nobody's ever said it out loud. He puts me on the tailboard when somebody's going to get hurt and he tells me to say a thing before the person says it, and he asks me for distances before he asks anybody for anything, and that's — that's mine, that's the one thing out here that's mine." She stopped and started again. "And he walked past me in the dark to get to you."

Toren walked.

"It isn't about who's worth more," he said.

"I know."

"It's about a thing I've got in my pocket that I never asked for."

Dessa's head came round.

"I know that too," she said. "That's the other reason I'm not angry at you."



They made camp at dusk in the lee of the bank with hay-smell coming up out of the dark, and Milo cooked.

He got the stone hot and mixed the meal and put the first flat cake down and turned it when it needed turning, and when it came off he broke it in two and then broke a piece off the corner of one half and put the piece on a flat stone at the edge of the coals, and set it aside.

Then he handed the rest round in his order. Senna. Wyck, because of the leg. Whoever was on watch. Everybody else.

He did it the way he put the lid back on a crock.

Toren sat with his bread in his hands and looked at the piece on the rock.

He knew what it was now. He knew whose it was and what clan it had come out of and that a woman had asked her own grandmother about it once and been given nothing, and had stopped asking at about eleven, and had gone on doing it anyway for the whole of a life that had ended in a room under a hill on the sixteenth of the eighth month, nine years ago, at about half past three in the afternoon.

And Milo Faust, who was thirteen and three months and had not the faintest idea, did it every night because he had watched somebody do it and his hands had picked it up on their own.

Across the fire, Rook looked at the piece of bread on the rock.

He looked at it for about three seconds. Then he looked away and ate his own and said nothing, exactly as he had every night for five weeks, and Toren sat with his own bread going cold in his hands and found that he could not eat it and could not put it down and could not say one word to anybody about why.



He went out past the fire at nine to drill and got nothing at all.

Not four inches. Not a flicker. He stood in the dark grass with his father's Ember in his fist and went to the back of the house and the house was shut, and he stood there for a quarter of an hour and then came back in.

Rook was at the tailboard with a boot off.

"Nothing," Toren said.

"No."

"Not one—"

"No. Not today, and probably not tomorrow." Rook did not look up. "You had two feet of it three days ago and you held it while you were walking and you're not going to get near that again for a few days, and when it comes back it'll be where it was, and you'll be frightened between now and then that it won't. That's the whole of it. Go and sit down."

"Is that because of last night?"

"It's because you're thirteen and you were up till four in the morning and somebody put something heavy in your arms." Rook worked the boot back on. "Sleep."

"I can't tell them," Toren said.

Rook stopped.

"No."

"Not any of it. Not the — not even the part about the bread." Toren's voice had gone somewhere strange. "Milo does it every night. He's been doing it for a fortnight. I could tell him what it is in one sentence and it'd cost nobody anything, and I can't, because the second I give him one true thing he'll want the next one and I'll have to start lying to him, and then it's — "

"Then it's what he's got," said Rook. "Instead of what he had."

"Yes."

"That's right. That's exactly right, and you got there in a day, and it took me a lot longer." He stood up. "I'm not going to tell you what to do with it. I want you to notice that I'm not. You can hand the whole of it to Tanner tonight if you decide to and I won't say a word about it tomorrow, and it'll be yours, and that's the actual difference between this morning and yesterday."

"That's not fair."

"No," said Rook. "You'll find it isn't."



Wyck was on the first watch at the top of the bank, sitting on his coat with his leg out and his stick across his knees, and Toren went and sat down next to him because there was nowhere else in the world to go.

They sat for a while with the hay-smell coming up and the fire behind them going down.

"You've got it, then," Wyck said.

Toren did not answer.

"That's not a question and I'm not asking. I want that clear." Wyck had his eyes out on the dark. "You went out to that junction with him in the middle of the night and you came back at four in the morning and you haven't eaten anything all day and you can't look at Faust. So you've got it. Whatever it is. He's given you the whole thing and you've got it now and nobody else on this bank ever will."

"Wyck."

"I'm going to tell you something and then I'm going to shut up about it for good," said Wyck Tanner. "Five weeks ago I thought it was about which of us was better. Then I thought it was about which of us he liked. Then I worked out it was about a thing in your pocket and I stopped asking, and I've been fairly pleased with myself about that ever since, because it was the only grown-up thing I've done since my aunt died and I knew it while I was doing it, which is the best part."

He shifted the leg with both hands.

"And then this morning at that junction he turned the cart east," he said. "And he'd have gone west. Every single thing he's done since the loop road says he'd have gone west, and I've been watching him do it for a month and I'd have put my hand in a fire on it. And he turned it east because of an old woman he met five weeks ago in a road."

"Yes."

"So I've been walking all day thinking about how much that cost him," said Wyck, "and about how I'll never know the number. And about how you do."

The fire went down another notch behind them.

"That's what it's like from here," Wyck said. "That's all. I said it in the birch and I'm saying it again with the other half on: you've got something, and now you've got the rest of it as well, and every one of us is still walking along behind you. And it's fine. I want you to hear the fine part. I'm not building anything out of it this time."

He got his stick under him and stood up, the careful way, in two parts, and looked out at the dark where the country went east.

"But you're going to be carrying it on your own from here," he said, "and I've done five weeks of that, and it's worse than the not knowing. I'd tell you to put it down if there were anywhere to put it."

He went off along the top of the bank to walk the line.

Toren Voss sat in the grass with his hand flat over two rows of stitches and the hay-smell coming up out of the fields and a piece of bread going cold on a stone by a dying fire, and did not put it down, and did not take his hand away.



Chapter 19: Greenmill

There was a fourth beat in his grandmother's cough now.

Toren had heard it for the first time two nights before the junction and had said nothing, and had spent every night since lying on his side in the grass with his eyes open, waiting for it. It came at some point in every hour. Three small dry sounds and then a pause long enough to believe in, and then a fourth, lower, coming up from somewhere further down.

He had not told anybody. He had worked out, somewhere in the last week, that this was a thing he was going to be doing a great deal of.

"Stop listening to me," Senna said, without opening her eyes.

"I'm not."

"You're walking on the wrong side of this barrow." She pulled the blanket up. "You've been on the off shaft since the Gate. You're on the near one because that's the ear."

Toren went round to the off shaft, and Milo, on the near one, gave him a look and did not say anything, which from Milo Faust was worth more than most people's speeches.



The grade ended at the river at about eleven in the morning on the second day east.

It did not taper off or run down into the ground. It went straight out over the water and stopped, in the way a sentence stops when the man saying it has been shot. Forty feet of embankment, and then a squared stone abutment with iron stubs sheared off flush across the top of it. Then the Reave, brown and wide and moving. Then two stone piers standing up out of the middle of it with nothing on them, and the same abutment again on the far bank with grass growing out of the joints.

Toren stood on the end of the world they had been walking along for six weeks and looked at the gap.

"They took the bridge as well," said Milo.

"They didn't take it," Rook said. "It went. Iron holds a bridge up. Everything else is just heavy."

He set the shafts down and rolled his shoulders once and looked downstream, east, where the north bank ran on in a long flat shelf of willow and sedge with the water beside it.

"Line's finished," he said. "From here the road's the river, and in two days the river turns and there's a road that's still a road. Everybody drink something. We're going down the bank."



They went downstream all afternoon with the water on their right hand.

It was not like the grade and Toren felt the difference in his shoulders before he could say what it was. The grade had been a made thing — flat, straight, indifferent, running through the country without belonging to any of it. The riverbank belonged to the river. It bent when the water bent. It went soft where the water had been at it and hard where it hadn't, and twice they had to take the barrow up into the field above because a cut bank had fallen in, and both times it cost them a quarter hour and Rook said nothing about it.

Toren drilled while he walked, because he could now.

That was the other thing about the afternoon, and he did not trust it. His Edge had been gone for four days after the junction, entirely, not a flicker, exactly as Rook had said it would be and exactly as Toren had been unable to believe. And then on the first morning east it had come back, two feet of steady white out of his father's Ember, and it had not gone anywhere since. He walked eight miles down the bank of the Reave with the light burning in his fist and his eyes on the water and his mind on his grandmother's cough, and the light stayed on.

Rook, forty feet ahead in the traces, did not turn round and did not mention it.



Greenmill announced itself by smell, which Toren did not identify for a full minute and then did, and the identification went through him like cold water.

Bread.

"Oh," said Milo, in a completely different voice. "Oh, that's—"

"Don't run," said Rook.

"I'm not running."

"You're about to."

There was a mill on a race and eleven houses behind it and a smoke-house at the end of a row of drying frames, and a track came down out of the fields and ended at the water, and there were people in it. Not many. A man on the mill step with his sleeves rolled. Two women doing something to a frame of split fish. Three children on the bank throwing things at the river, who stopped throwing things at the river and stood up.

It was the first settlement any of them had seen since a woman in reading glasses had walked them to the outside of a pair of doors in a fallen fuel tank and told them the page was closed but not torn out.

Toren looked at Dessa. Dessa was looking at the drying frames with an expression he recognized from a great distance, because it was the one she had worn in the tunnel at Tallow Gate while a warden weighed jars, and it was the face of somebody watching a whole ordinary world go on being ordinary.

"Straighten up," said Rook. "All of you. Walk like people who've been somewhere."

"We have been somewhere," said Wyck.

"Then it shouldn't be hard."



The miller was called Pell Hoy and he came down off his step and looked at the barrow before he looked at any of them, which Toren thought was probably the correct order.

"That's a road cart," he said.

"It's a quarry barrow."

"It's a quarry barrow with a woman in it." Hoy put his hands on his hips. "How far?"

"Coldwater," Rook said. "Six weeks, near enough."

Hoy whistled one note and did not ask any of the four questions Toren could see arriving behind his face, which was, Toren decided, the second correct thing he had done.

They had a bed for Senna. That was all they had and they gave it without any negotiating at all — a corner of the mill's grain loft with a real mattress on a real frame, and a woman called Marda Wain who had been the nearest thing to a wound-woman in Greenmill for thirty years and who came and knelt by the barrow before anybody had asked her to.

She got Senna's wrist in her fingers and put her head down near the old woman's chest and stayed there, listening, for a long time.

"Father's word," she said at last, "look at her."

"I'm looking at me all day," said Senna. "Say it out."

"Reactor."

"Twenty years ago, and I've had it said to me by better than you, no offense. Can you do anything?"

"No," said Marda Wain.

"Good. Say that first next time, it saves everybody's afternoon."

The woman laughed, which surprised all of them, and then sat back on her heels and looked at Senna Voss for a while with something on her face that was not pity.

"Meridian," she said.

"Meridian."

"It's seventeen days on from here if the fords are down." She got up. "You'll want the honey. I'll not argue about it, so don't start."



They ate in the mill yard at dusk with about half of Greenmill sitting round the edges of it watching them do it, which was uncomfortable for approximately four minutes and then stopped being uncomfortable at all, because Milo Faust happened to them.

Toren could not afterward reconstruct how it went. It was not that Milo said anything clever. He asked the children what they had been throwing at the river and they told him, and then he asked whether it worked, and it turned out that it did not work. And then he was down on the bank with the three of them and a fourth who had come from somewhere, showing them how to make a flat stone go across water instead of into it, which he could not do either. About twenty minutes later one of the children did it four times and made a sound like a kettle, and the whole yard turned round.

"He's going to want to stay," Dessa said.

"He's not."

"I know he's not. I'm telling you he's going to want to." She had her arms round her knees and her bread untouched in her hands. "So am I, a bit. You can say it. Nobody's listening."

Toren looked across the yard at the mill and the drying frames and the smoke coming flat off the smoke-house roof, and at the loft window where his grandmother was lying on a mattress on a frame for the first time in six weeks.

"A bit," he said.



The company came up in the talk at about the time the light went.

It came up the way weather comes up, as the thing everybody had already been not-quite-saying, and it came out of Pell Hoy, who had drunk two mugs of something and had got comfortable.

"You'll not go up past the quarry," he said. "Not the north side. Not for a fortnight yet."

"Why's that," said Rook.

"Tear." Hoy said it the way a man says rats. "Opened in the spring up in the old cut, and we had a bad summer off it — lost two off the top field in the sixth month and near lost the whole of the hay. So we put in for a company."

"You put in."

"All of us. Eleven houses and the mill and the two farms up the hill, and it took the lot of us to make the fee, and I'll tell you plainly it took the lot of us and then some." He turned the mug round. "Three weeks they've been up there. They know the work. They've had two casks of Flask off it already and one of them was the best weighing anybody's seen out of this valley, and when the tear's shut we get the half of the ash that's in the contract, and we've a paper on it, and the paper's good."

"Who weighs it?"

"They do."

"Who buys it?"

"They do that as well." Hoy looked at him. "That's how it's done."

"It is," Rook agreed. "Where do they winter?"

Toren had his bread in his hand and did not move any part of himself.

He had been listening to this man ask questions for six weeks. He had listened to him ask a warden about a charter and a wheelwright about a road and a woman at a ford about the depth of it, and every single one of those questions had come out in exactly the same shape: flat, short, in the middle of a sentence about something else, so that the person answering never once noticed they had been asked anything.

This one came out the same way. It was the third in a row and it was the only one he had actually wanted.

"Undern," said Pell Hoy. "Same as all of them. Weighing goes on till the ground freezes."

"It does," said Rook, and drank his water, and asked about the fords.



Wyck found Toren at the woodpile afterward.

"Don't," Toren said.

"I'm not going to." Wyck leaned his stick against the stack and started handing him splits, one at a time, so that Toren could stack them with the hand that worked. "I'm going to say one thing and then I'm going to go to bed, because there's a roof and I'd like to lie under one."

"All right."

"Three questions," said Wyck. "Weighs, buys, winters. And he wanted the third one and he put two in front of it so it wouldn't sit on its own." He handed over another split. "I've watched him do that eleven times since the Gate and I've never once seen him do it about something that didn't matter."

Toren stacked the wood.

"I know," he said.

"Good," said Wyck Tanner, and took his stick, and went to lie under a roof.



Dessa came and got him an hour later with the fire down and most of Greenmill asleep.

"Come up the bank with me."

"Now?"

"I've been holding since we came in," she said, "and there's a thing at the top of the field that I've had my hand on for two hours, and I'd rather look at it with somebody than by myself."

They went up the track past the last house, and past a gate, and out onto the shoulder of the hill where the grass was cropped short by something and the whole valley lay below them in the dark with the river going through it pale.

The graves were under the wall at the top corner.

There were three of them.

Toren stood in the wet grass and felt six weeks come round and click into place the way a wheel drops into a rut, and he did not have to be told and did not have to ask, and neither of them said anything for a long moment.

The earth was fresh. Not this week — the grass had started — but this season. Somebody had dug them to a decent depth in hard ground. Somebody had squared the corners. Somebody had set a flat stone upright at the head of each one and packed it round with smaller stones so it would stand through a winter.

"They're not written on," Dessa said.



Chapter 20: The Contract

Rook went up to the top field at first light and looked at the graves for about as long as it takes to fill a can, and came back down, and said they were leaving after breakfast.

"She's had one night," said Dessa.

"She's had one night."

"She could have two. He offered. He said—"

"I know what he offered." Rook was rolling the canvas. "Get her down the stair with Faust on the other side and don't let her do the last three steps herself, she'll try."

That was all he said about it. Toren, who had watched this man refuse a warden and burn a slate that would not burn, went and got the honey jar off the loft shelf and did not ask. He had learned about four weeks ago that there was a particular flatness the man used when a thing was already decided, and a different one he used when it wasn't. This was not the second one.



The company came down into Greenmill at nine in the morning.

There were three of them and they came along the north track out of the west with the light behind them, and the first thing Toren registered was that Pell Hoy was pleased to see them.

That was the thing he could not get past, afterward. The miller came off his step wiping his hands and went out to meet them in the middle of his own yard, and one of the women left the drying frames and went and stood where she could hear, and two of the children came round the end of the smoke-house at a run and then stopped and hung back at a distance that was not fear, exactly. It was the distance you keep from something big and useful. It was how Toren had stood near his father's forge bellows at six.

They were not frightening to look at. That was the second thing.

One was a heavy young man with a mattock over his shoulder and a bad sunburn across the back of his neck. One was a woman of about thirty with a coil of cord round her body from shoulder to hip and a canvas roll on her back, and she carried it the way Odo Prentice had carried his staff, which is to say as a part of herself she had stopped noticing.

The third was older. He was perhaps sixty, narrow, grey-stubbled, in a good coat that had been mended in five places by somebody who could sew, and he walked with his hands loose at his sides and his head slightly forward, and he looked at the mill and the frames and the barrow and the six strangers in the yard in one unhurried pass, the way you look at a room you have come into before.

"Flour," he said to Hoy. "Salt if you've salt. And we'll want the hay off your top field after all, if the offer's still in it."

"It's in it."

"Then that's settled and I'll not haggle you." He got a purse out. "Four days more on the cut and then it's shut, and you'll have your paper honored inside the week."

"Four days," said Hoy, and something came out of him all at once that he had clearly been holding for three weeks, and Toren looked away from it because it was private.



Rook was at the barrow.

He had the shafts up on his shoulders. He had put them there four minutes earlier because they had been about to leave, and now he was standing in the corner of the mill yard with two lengths of ash across his back and both hands up on them, entirely still, facing half away.

He did not put them down.

That was all it was. A man stood in a yard holding a cart he was not pulling, and did not set it down, and Toren Voss, thirteen years old, with a piece of bread in his hand that he had been about to eat, understood the whole of it before one single word had been said by anybody.

He did not look at the three people in the middle of the yard. He looked at Dessa.

Dessa had gone the color of the mill wall.

Her eyes were not on the company either. They were on Rook, and her mouth was slightly open, and Toren watched her hand come up and take hold of Wyck's sleeve without any part of her noticing she had done it, and Wyck — who had been watching the older man for some seconds by then — did not shake it off.



The older man bought his flour.

He paid it into Pell Hoy's hand in coin and in a small folded packet of something that Hoy put away fast. He asked after a man called Tully who had a boat, and was told where Tully was. He said something to the woman with the cord about the weather coming round, and she said something back that made the heavy young man laugh.

And then he turned to go, and his eyes went across the yard, and they went over the barrow and past it, and came back.

He stopped walking.

The mill race went on making the noise a mill race makes. Somewhere behind the houses a dog was going at something. The woman with the cord got four paces before she noticed she was alone and turned round.

Nobody in that yard said anything at all.

Rook stood with the shafts on his shoulders and his hands up on them and looked back, and his face had nothing on it, and nothing in his body moved, and Toren, eleven feet away, could feel his own heart going in his ears.

It lasted perhaps three seconds.

Then the older man's mouth did something at one corner. It was not a smile. It was the thing a man's mouth does when he has just put a weight down on a table to see how heavy it is.

He turned his head forward again and walked on out of the yard the way he had come, and the other two came after him, and the heavy young man looked back once and had no idea what he was looking at.

"Milo," said Rook, in an entirely ordinary voice. "Get the stove."



They were out of Greenmill in six minutes and going east along the water at a pace that had Wyck's jaw locked inside the first quarter mile.

Nobody asked. That was the part Toren thought about later — that four children and an old woman were walked out of the first friendly place they had seen in six weeks at a speed that nearly broke one of them, and not one of them opened their mouth, because all five of them had been in that yard.

At about a mile Rook took the barrow up off the bank into the field, where the ground was better, and slowed, and said:

"Right. Here's the whole of it."



"That man was at Homura nine years ago on the sixteenth of the eighth month in the afternoon. I don't know his name. I have known his face for nine years and I'd know it in a fire.

"He knows mine. He's known it since about half past three that afternoon, and he's had five years of being told by people at weighings and in yards that there's a man on the road asking after his company, and now he's stood in a yard and looked at me, and he is going to walk up that valley and tell the man who pays him.

"The man who pays him has nine people, a contract he's four days off finishing, an open tear at his back, and a paper with a farmer's name on it. He is not going to have a known enemy sitting between him and his road out. He's going to come and settle it while he knows where I am, and he's going to do it tonight or tomorrow, because tomorrow I could be anywhere."

"Then we go back," said Dessa. "Greenmill. There's forty people—"

"No."

"There's forty people, there's a mill, there's a wall on the—"

"There are eleven houses and three children who throw stones at a river," Rook said, "and those people hired that company with everything they had, and if I stand in the middle of Greenmill tonight then whatever happens, happens in Greenmill." He did not raise his voice at any point. "I did that once. I wasn't the one who brought it and it happened in my mother's kitchen anyway. We're not going back."

Nobody said anything.

"We're not going north either," he said. "I want that said out loud so none of you spends today wondering. Undern's four days up that line and there'll be thirty of them in it by frost, and I'm not going, and I wasn't going at the junction and I'm not going now. We're on the east road. We're going to stay on the east road, and the only thing that's changed is that the east road now has men on it behind us, and we're going to deal with them and then we're going to keep walking."

"Can we outrun them?" said Milo.

"With a barrow. No."

"Then—"

"Then I pick the ground," said Rook, "the way I always pick the ground, and I picked it an hour ago, and it's two miles ahead of us and it's called a weir."



The old weir came up in the middle of the afternoon.

It was a low concrete dam thrown across the whole width of the Reave, seventy or eighty feet of it, with the water going over the crest in a smooth brown sheet and coming down white into a pool below that was loud enough from two hundred paces to change how people talked. On the top of it, on the downstream edge, there was a walkway — a concrete curb about a yard wide with a handrail down one side that had been iron and was now a row of stubs.

On the north bank, where they were, there was the wreck of a winding-house and a stone chute going up the hill.

On the south bank there was a mill ruin, roofless, with a sluice-house beside it that still had most of a roof and a door.

And past the sluice-house the track went on east, up over the shoulder, toward the place where the Reave turned south.

"That's the crossing," Rook said. "It's the only one for eleven miles in either direction and it's why there's a mill on both ends of it and it's why the road's here. Everybody look at it properly, because from now on it's the thing all of you are standing on."



He put Senna in the sluice-house on the south bank with the barrow backed into the corner and the door on its hinge, and he put the second axe across her knees, and she took it and laid it flat and did not make one single remark, which frightened Toren more than anything that had happened in the yard that morning.

Then he got the four of them out on the south apron in the last of the light and taught them, for twenty minutes, as fast as he had ever taught them anything.

"Men aren't rift-things. Say it."

They said it.

"Now here's what that means and there's four parts and you're going to get all four tonight whether you can hold them or not.

"One. A rift-thing comes at the nearest worked power. A man comes at what he can see and what he thinks is going to hurt him, and those are two different things, and a clever man goes for the second one first. Nothing you do with a light will pull a man off a line he's chosen. It'll only tell him where you are.

"Two. A rift-thing has to close. That's the gap we've been living in for six weeks — looking is free and moving costs them time, and every drill I've put you through since the Gate is built on buying that time. A man does not have to close. A man can hurt you from where he is standing, and one of those nine can hurt you from further away than you have ever in your life been hurt from, and there is no answer to it. There's no guard. There's no light. There's ground, and there's being behind something, and that's the entire list.

"Three. A rift-thing does not change its mind. A man does. If the man in the brown coat decides at midnight that the arithmetic's gone wrong, he will turn round and go back up that valley and finish his contract and we'll never see him again, and that is a thing you can actually work toward and it is the only kind of winning available to us tonight.

"Four." He stopped for a moment. "The diamond is for rift-things. It was built for a thing that comes at the nearest light, and it puts the one who can't fight in the middle where the nearest light is furthest from the outside, and it has kept all four of you alive for six weeks and it is the best shape I know. Tonight it is the worst place any of you could stand. There is no middle against men. The middle is the open. Spread and get behind something and stay behind it."

"Say it back," said Dessa, before he could.

"Spread and get behind something," said Wyck.

"Spread," said Milo. "Behind something."

"Spread and behind something," said Toren.



Rook looked at the four of them in the last grey light with the weir going white behind them.

"You'll do it anyway," he said.

Milo said, "Do what?"

"The diamond. Something will happen and the three of you will go to your corners and you'll put him in the middle, because I have spent six weeks putting it into your hands, and it lives in your hands now and not in your heads, and that is exactly what I wanted and it is exactly wrong for tonight." He said it perfectly evenly. "I'm telling you first. I want that on the record, because I didn't tell you first at the pumphouse and a boy has walked with a stick ever since, and I have thought about that every day."

"Then why tell us," said Wyck, "if it won't help?"

"Because knowing it is worth nothing," Rook said, "and you can't start paying for it until you know it."



Milo went out at full dark.

It was his own idea in the sense that he was the only one who could have done it, and Rook's in the sense that Rook had worked out four hours earlier that it was the thing that would decide where the fight happened. A quarter mile up the north bank, on the top of the stone chute above the winding-house, where it could be seen from the whole length of the valley and from nowhere near the sluice-house on the south side: one small pale light, sitting on a stone, unattended.

"They'll come to it," Rook said. "They'll come to it because it's the only thing in eleven miles of dark that a man made, and by the time they've been up that chute and found nothing they'll be at the weir, and they'll be at the weir on the north end with sixty feet of concrete between them and us, which is what I want, and none of that happens without you."

"It's just a light," said Milo.

"It's the only piece of this I couldn't buy anywhere else. Go and put it down and come straight back and don't stop to look at it."

He went. He came back in nineteen minutes at a jog with his hands in his sleeves and his ears red with cold and said, "It's up," and sat down against the sluice-house wall in the middle of the south apron with his knees drawn up, exactly where six weeks of drill had taught him that the middle was the safe place, and no one moved him, because no one thought of it.

Toren had two feet of white light standing steady out of his father's Ember and had not noticed for some while that it was there.

Dessa was on the south end of the walkway with her back against the buttress and her boots braced and her eyes open on nothing. She had been holding for an hour. The weir made a noise that took her hearing away entirely, which was why Rook had put her there, and she had understood that before he said it.

At twenty minutes past midnight she said, in a flat and almost bored voice, into a dark that had nothing in it:

"Nine. Coming down the north bank in two groups. Four hundred feet."



Chapter 21: The Weir

"Say the failure," Rook said.

They were on the south apron in the dark with the weir going white eight feet away and the noise of it sitting on top of everything.

"We get pushed off this end," said Wyck.

"That's it. That's the whole of it and there's nothing underneath it." Rook had his coat off and folded on the sluice-house step, which none of them had ever seen him do at night. "The barrow does not move fast and there is a door with your grandmother behind it, and if any of them get past this end of that walkway then everything after that happens in a stone room with one way out. You hold the south end or nothing else you do tonight means anything. Say it back."

"Hold the south end," said Toren.

"Hold the south end," said Milo.

"Good. Cole."

"Three hundred," said Dessa.



She gave them the whole of the approach.

She could not hear a thing. That was the reason she was there and Toren had worked it out about four seconds after Rook put her against the buttress: the weir took hearing off every person on that riverbank equally, and there was exactly one of them for whom that did not matter, and she had been sitting in a field at noon with her eyes open for six weeks learning how not to mind.

"Two hundred and eighty. Still two groups. Five in front, four behind, and the four behind are further off the water than the five."

"How much further?"

"Sixty feet up the bank. Maybe more." Her head had not moved at all. "They've stopped."

"They've seen it," said Rook.

"They've—"

"They've seen Faust's light."

Milo made a small sound.

"Now they'll send two," Rook said. "Nobody in that company is going up a stone chute in the dark without leaving somebody at the bottom of it. Cole, tell me when it's two."

Forty seconds.

"It's two," said Dessa.

Rook let out a breath that Toren had not known he was holding, which was in itself the single most alarming thing that had happened all night.

"Nineteen minutes," he said. "That's what he's just bought us. Sit down, all of you, and drink something, because you won't want to in a quarter of an hour."



They came to the north end of the walkway at about one in the morning.

Toren saw them arrive as a change in the shape of the dark and then, when one of them put up a hooded lamp, as seven men and women standing in a loose arc on the north apron sixty feet away with the whole white sheet of the weir between.

"Look at them now," Rook said, without turning his head. "You won't get another chance and you'll want to have done it.

"Brown coat is Torch. He's been Torch longer than any of you have been alive and he'll have held it at three feet since before your mothers were born, and he's not going to try for three feet tonight, because a yard of concrete doesn't want three feet. The narrow one at the back is Flame and it's magic-side and it's all sensing, and he's the reason they came down the bank in two groups instead of four. The tall one with the cord is Flame and doesn't know it, and nobody's ever told her, and I'm not going to get the chance to.

"The rest are men with tools, and men with tools have killed more people on this continent than everybody on that ladder put together, so don't get clever about it."

"And you?" said Wyck.

"Further up," said Rook.

He walked out onto the walkway about fifteen feet and stopped, where the lamp could reach him.

"Far enough," he said.

He did not shout it and it carried anyway, which was a thing Toren had seen him do twice before and had never understood.

The lamp came forward. Behind it, in the edge of its light, there was a man in a brown oilskin coat, broad through the shoulders, somewhere past fifty, with a beard cut short and square and a heavy short blade already lit in his right fist — not three feet of white but eighteen inches of it, thick, held low and easy at his hip like a tool he had been carrying all day.

"Hale Orsk," he said. "I run this detachment and the contract's in my name. I'd like it said out loud that I came down here to talk, because I'd like it said afterward."

"Say it, then."

"Whitlow says you're the one that's been on us five years." Orsk's voice was reasonable and carried the way a man's voice carries when he has spent thirty years making himself heard across open ground. "I'll tell you what I told him. I came into this company six years ago. Whatever you've got, you've got it with four men, and two of those are dead of ordinary things and one's in Undern with no legs. I've got seven people here and a paper on a tear and eleven houses depending on it, and I will take all of that up that valley in the morning and never come back down this water, and you can walk east, and that's the end of it."

"No," said Rook.

"I've not finished."

"You have. You came down here to find out what I am." Rook put his hands in his pockets. "You've found out. Go on back up the bank."

Orsk stood on the north apron for a moment.

"Whitlow," he said.

The narrow older man came into the lamplight with his hands loose at his sides and his head slightly forward.

He looked across sixty feet of moving water at the man standing on the walkway, and he did not say anything at all, and then he turned to Orsk and nodded once, slowly, the way a man agrees to something he has been arguing against.

"Right," said Hale Orsk. "That's a pity."



Toren had thought he knew what the next part would look like.

He had six weeks of ballast and birch and a flooded pump house, and under all of that he had a road on the fifth of the month, and he had built out of the whole of it a picture of what happened when a fight began: that it began, that there was a moment when the thing turned over and everybody started.

There was no moment. The lamp went out and four people came down the walkway at a run.



The walkway was a yard wide.

That was the entire tactical shape of the next two minutes and Rook had seen it from two hundred paces away that afternoon. A yard of concrete with the sheet of the weir pouring over on the upstream side and a fourteen-foot drop into the white of the tailwater pool on the other, and nowhere on any part of it for two people to stand abreast.

The first man came into Rook's range and Rook was not there.

He had gone low and left, one hand down on the curb, the hard blue-white frame coming up around his legs so fast that Toren's eyes printed it as a single bar of light — and the spear came out of the Ember already at half length because there was no room for seven feet of it, and the shortening was the strike, and the man went off the walkway into the pool.

The second one Rook put down without straightening up.

The third got a hand on him. Toren saw it happen. The man was big and had a mattock haft and he came in under the spear and got his shoulder into Rook's chest, and for one second it was two people on a yard of wet concrete, ordinary and physical and almost stupid, the way the biggest moments turn out to be.

And then there were two short blades where there had been one long one, one in each hand. Toren still did not know where the second one came from. The man went off the downstream side.

The fourth one stopped.

He stopped at about thirty feet with his boots skidding and he went back up the walkway faster than he had come down it, and Rook did not follow him.

That was the part Toren could not stop thinking about afterward. Sixty feet of open concrete and a man running away up it, and Rook stood where he was, in the middle, with the frame still burning around his legs, and let him go.

"He doesn't leave the end," Wyck said, at Toren's shoulder. He was breathing hard and he had not moved and neither had his Edge. "Look at him. He won't go past the middle."

"Why not?"

"Because we're at this end," said Wyck.



The rifle came into it eleven minutes later.

There was no warning of any kind. There was a hard flat crack from somewhere high up the north bank, arriving a long time after it should have, and a piece of the buttress six inches above Dessa's head came off it and went past Toren's ear with a sound like a wasp.

Nobody on the south apron understood what had happened.

"Down," Rook said, from thirty feet out on the walkway, and he was already coming back. "Everybody down. Behind the sluice-house wall, now, move—"

The second one took a splinter of concrete out of the curb where his foot had been.

"That's a rifle," said Wyck. He said it in the flat curious voice of a person whose mind has gone somewhere else entirely. "That's a—"

"Get behind the wall."

They got behind the wall.

Toren went down on his knees in the wet with his back against eighteen inches of mill stone and his heart going and his father's Ember still lit in his fist, and he heard, through everything, Rook saying one short sentence to himself that was not for any of them, and the sentence was: she's in the winding-house.



"Listen to me," Rook said. "All four. Fast.

"She's a hundred and ten feet away across moving water and she is up in the stone of that winding-house and she will not come down and she does not need to. Nothing I have reaches her from here. Nothing any of you have ever will. There is one thing on this river that gets to her and it's me and it takes about nine seconds, and for those nine seconds there is nobody on this end of the walkway but you.

"So here's the order and you don't get a second one. Stay behind stone. Do not go out onto that apron for any reason at all. If they come across, Tanner holds the mouth and the rest of you stay where you are and let him.

"Nine seconds. Cole, tell them."

And he went.

He did not go along the walkway.

He went over the crest of the weir, upstream side, straight across seventy feet of brown water sliding over concrete two inches deep and going faster than a man runs, with the light around his legs turning the whole sheet of it white from underneath — and Toren, with his face against the mill stone, saw it in three separate pictures, like lightning, and understood in the third one that the man had not been running on the weir at all for the last twenty feet of it.



Two of them came up out of the tailwater on the south side.

Toren never found out how they got there and Rook told him later that it was the obvious thing and that he had expected it half an hour earlier — that the pool below a weir is shallow along its edges, and that any two men with a rope and no particular objection to being cold can be across a river in four minutes if nobody is looking at them.

They came up over the lip of the apron thirty feet behind the sluice-house wall, in the dark, on the side where there was nothing.

And the four of them did the thing they had been built to do.

It took no time at all. Toren came off the wall and out and to the front because point was his, and Wyck went left because left was his, and Dessa went right, and they made the shape they had made in a yard between two fuel tanks and on a hillside in the birch and in forty minutes of standing black timber where nothing had happened, and they made it fast and clean and with nobody saying one word, and it worked.

It worked. The first man came at the front of it and got three feet of Wyck Tanner's Edge across the forearm from the left side where he was not looking, and went down. The second one turned to face that and Toren was already inside of him with two feet of white light and put it through the shaft of the thing in his hands, and the man ran.

Six seconds. It was the best six seconds of Toren Voss's life.

And Milo Faust was standing in the middle of it, eleven feet out on the open apron, where the middle goes.



The third shot came from a hundred and ten feet away, across moving water, out of a stone winding-house, from a woman Toren never saw at any point that night or afterward.

Milo sat down.

That was all Toren's eyes gave him and it was all they ever gave him. There was a sound that was not the weir, and Milo Faust sat down on the wet concrete with his legs out in front of him like somebody stopping to fix a bootlace, and put one hand flat on the ground beside his own hip, and did not get up.

Toren was seven feet away.

He got there. He got there and he had both hands on him and Milo was looking at him with his eyebrows up in an expression of enormous, uncomplicated surprise, and there was a dark place high on the left side of his coat, and Toren put his palm over it because there was nothing else in the world to do with a hand.

"Dessa," he said. And then, much louder: "Dessa—"



Up on the north bank there was a noise.

It was not loud and it did not last. It was over before Toren's head came round, and afterward he was never able to say whether he had actually heard anything at all or had simply seen the light go out of a stone window, and he did not ask.

Nine seconds, Rook had said, and it had been fourteen, because he had had to go up the outside of the winding-house wall.



Hale Orsk came down the walkway while he was still on the far side.

He came at a heavy unhurried run with eighteen inches of white in his fist, and Toren, kneeling on an apron with both hands on his friend, saw him arrive at the mouth of the walkway and did not have anything left to do about it.

Wyck Tanner sat down.

He did it deliberately. He went the last four feet to the mouth of the walkway on his stick and then he put his back against the buttress and lowered himself onto the concrete with his bad leg straight out across the gap and the good one folded under him, the careful way, in two parts — and the white light came out of his fist and kept going and did not stop at three feet.

It stopped at five.

"Five sitting," said Dessa, from somewhere behind him, in a voice with nothing in it. "You've got five sitting. He's at forty feet."

"How long?"

"Twelve seconds."

"Tell me every three."

Toren had his hands on Milo and could not turn his head and heard all of it.

He heard Hale Orsk come into the mouth of that walkway, thirty years and a company and a contract and a Torch's worth of one door lit all the way up, against a boy of fifteen sitting on the ground in his way with five feet of light across a yard of wet concrete and a drop on both sides and nothing behind him but an old woman in a stone room.

He heard the first exchange. He heard the second.

"Nine," said Dessa.

He heard Wyck make a sound he had made once before, on a hillside above a road, on the fifth of the month.

"Six."

Wyck held.



Rook came back across the weir crest and up onto the south apron and did not stop moving, and he put his left hand out.

Nothing came out of it. That was what Toren's eye insisted on, and his eye was wrong.

Fifty feet of walkway went hard and thick and wrong. The sheet of water coming over the crest beside it stood up in a line, all at once, as though a wind had come from directly above it, and Hale Orsk stopped with his forward boot off the ground like a man walking into a door in the dark.

Three seconds.

In the third second Rook's arm began to shake.

Then the pressure let go and the water came down and Rook went past Wyck Tanner into the mouth of the walkway, and it was finished in about as long as it takes to say so, and Toren did not watch any of it, because he was holding a coat shut with both hands.



Dessa got there at a run and put her hands where Toren's were and Toren took his away, and she worked.

He watched her work. He had watched her work on a hillside of broken ballast with her tongue between her teeth and on a boy who had gone through a window in Coldwater when she was fourteen, and he knew exactly what her face did when it was going, and he knew within about four seconds that it was not.

"Move your hands," said Rook.

He was already down beside them. His own hands were shaking so much that he had to put the Ember into the crook of his arm to get the coat open, and he did it anyway, and laid the thing flat and put both palms over it and held.

The weir went on making its noise.

Toren knelt on the wet concrete of an apron on the south bank of the Reave and watched a man who had put the ends of a leg together in a flooded pump house for eleven minutes, and a splintered shin in a road on the fifth of the month, hold Vera's Ember flat against Milo Faust's chest for a long time.

"That's the ends together," Rook said at last.

His voice had nothing in it whatsoever.

"There has to be somebody to do the rest."



The Riftspawn came out of the water at about half past two.

There were four of them in the pool below the weir and then six, folding and unfolding up over the lip of the apron with the seams down their chests lit like a row of coals, and there were more of them on the north bank in the dark where Cinder Company was.

Eleven people had been using worked power for an hour and a half on both sides of a river four miles below an open tear.

Nobody had to say any of that. Dessa said, "Left," in a flat voice, and Wyck got up off the concrete on his stick, and Rook took his hands off Milo's chest and stood, and after that it was work.

It took about nine minutes and Toren did almost none of it and could not afterward put any of it in order. He had two feet of white light out of his father's Ember and he had it the whole way through, and he had it while he was moving and while he was shouting and while he was doing a thing with his hands that he never told anybody about, and it did not go out one single time, and he did not notice that until two days later.

On the north bank there was a lamp, and then there was not, and the sound of Cinder Company going up that valley in the dark was audible for a long while afterward and nobody on the south apron went after it.

"Flask," said Rook, of the drift nearest him on the concrete, because that was what he said, and he said it the way a man says a thing he has said ten thousand times. "Flask. Handful. That's a Flask and a bad one."

He did not draw any of it up.

He put his palm flat on the apron for a moment, out of thirty years of habit, and then he took it off the stone and wiped it on his coat and went back and sat down on the wet ground beside Milo Faust and put a hand under the boy's head so it was not on concrete.



A quarter of a mile up the north bank, on the top of the stone chute above the winding-house, one small pale light was sitting on a stone.

It had been there for four hours. Nothing had put it out and nothing was going to. It was the only made thing in eleven miles of dark, and it did not gutter and it did not drift, and it did not need anybody at all.



Chapter 22: Milo

Milo Faust died a little before four in the morning on the south apron of the old weir, with his head on a man's hand and his coat open and four people around him, about eight hours after he had taught a child in Greenmill to make a flat stone go across water instead of into it. He was thirteen years and six months old. He did not say anything at the end and he was not frightened, and Toren was holding his hand and would not have been able to say afterward which of them had taken hold first.

Then it was morning, and there were things to do.



Rook did them.

He got up off the wet ground at about half past four and put his coat on and went round the four of them one at a time, and he did not say a word to any of them that was not an instruction.

He sent Wyck into the sluice-house to sit down and get his boot off, and then he went in after him ten minutes later and looked at the leg and came out again. He got the stove lit himself, which he had not done since Milo took the cooking over on the grade, and he put water on it and he made all three of them drink some of it, standing over them, until it was gone.

He took Dessa by both wrists and turned her hands over and looked at them and said, "You did everything. There wasn't anything else to do and you did all of it," and she said, "I know," and then about four seconds later she said, "I don't," and he kept hold of her wrists and did not let go for a while.

He went and got Milo's blanket off the barrow and did not let anybody else do it.

And at some point in the middle of all of that he came and crouched down in front of Toren, who was sitting against the mill stone with his hands on his knees, and said, "Voss."

"Yes."

"You had it on the whole way through. From when they came up the tailwater to when the last of the rift-things went into the concrete. You had two feet of it and you held it while everything in the world was happening and you did not put it out one time." Rook's face had nothing on it. "That's Flame. I'm telling you now because I'd tell you today whatever had happened, and because if I leave it a week you'll always wonder whether I only said it to give you something."

Toren looked at him.

"All right," he said.

"That's all," said Rook, and got up.

Toren had wanted that word for six weeks. He had lain awake on the ballast and in the arch and in wet grass on the grade wanting it, and had asked how long four separate times and been told not to ask, and now it had arrived and it sat in him the way a stone sits in a pocket, with no weight on either side of it at all.



His grandmother came out of the sluice-house at six in the morning on her own feet.

Nobody helped her, because nobody dared. She came out through the door with one hand on the frame and the other on the wall and her splinted leg going out stiff in front of her at each step, and she got four paces onto the apron and stopped and stood there, eighty-one years old, and looked at the blanket.

Then she looked at the three of them.

"You're all standing wrong," she said.

Toren heard himself say, "What?"

"You're standing about like people waiting for somebody to tell them where to go. I've seen it forty times and I've done it twice myself and it doesn't get better while you're doing it." She took another step and put her hand out and Toren got under it without thinking. "Right. Cole. Is there ground up on that shoulder or is it all stone?"

"It's—" Dessa's voice went. She started again. "There's ground. Above the mill ruin, where the track goes up. It's field."

"Then that's where he goes, and somebody wants to go and look at it before we all walk up there, because if it's wet at the bottom I want to know now and not at the top of the hill." Senna Voss stood on the concrete with her hand on her grandson's shoulder. "That's the first one. There's about nine and they all want doing and none of them want thinking about, and I'll say them out one at a time, and every one of you is going to be glad of it by tonight and none of you is going to be glad of it now."



They buried him at noon in the field above the mill ruin with the Reave going below and the weir making the only noise there was.

Rook dug. He did not ask any of them to and he did not stop any of them when they took turns, and Wyck took a turn that went on far too long — standing on one leg with the other braced out behind him and the stick lying in the grass, working, for thirty-five minutes, with his face doing nothing at all — and nobody said anything about that either.

Dessa said the words. She had said them for her mother on a bank above a road on the fifth of the month and had got most of the way through, and she got most of the way through again, and Senna finished them from the ground where she was sitting with her leg out, and nobody had to ask her to.

Toren could not say anything at all. He had been building toward it since first light and when it came to it there was nothing in him, and he stood at the end of the turned earth with his hands at his sides and his mouth open and no sound, and Wyck Tanner put a hand between his shoulder blades and left it there until it was over.



Afterward he went back down to the sluice-house and got his father's Ember out.

It was two o'clock in the afternoon. The sluice-house had a stone lintel over the door, eight inches deep and dressed flat on the face, seventy years of weather and a green stain down one side of it.

He lit up and got two feet of white and put it into the stone.

It was harder than he had expected and it took a great deal longer, and the light went out four times and came back four times, and he had to stop twice because his hand would not close. Nobody came and looked. Dessa came as far as the corner of the ruin once, and stood there, and went away again.

At half past four he had it. The letters were an inch high and cut poorly, and they went a finger-joint deep into dressed stone, and they said:

MILO FAUST

He stood back and looked at them for a long time.

He had seen an inch-high knife-cut line on a slate in a burned wayhouse and he had watched a man take the board off the wall with no ceremony at all and carry it out face-in against his chest and put it in a fire, and he had sat across that fire and watched slate not burn.

There were three graves in a top field at Greenmill with nothing written on them. There were three more above a road eleven days east of Coldwater with names in a warden's ledger and nothing on the ground, because there had been a shovel and no chisel and nobody had thought of it.

This would be there in a hundred years.

"That's good," said Rook, from behind him.

Toren did not turn around.

"It's crooked."

"It's in stone," said Rook.



Nobody ate until dark and then somebody had to, and it was Toren who got up and did it, because he worked out at about seven in the evening that the reason nobody had eaten was that all four of them were waiting for a thirteen-year-old boy to hand them something in a particular order, and that he was not going to, and that somebody had to break that or it would still be true in a week.

He got the stone hot and mixed the meal and put the first flat cake down and turned it when it needed turning.

When it came off he broke it in two. Then he broke a piece off the corner of one half and put the piece on a flat stone at the edge of the coals, and set it aside.

Then he handed the rest round. Senna. Wyck, because of the leg. Whoever was on watch. Everybody else.

He did it the way you put the lid back on a crock.

Across the fire, Rook looked at the piece of bread on the rock.

He looked at it for about three seconds, and his face did not change, and then he looked up and found Toren looking straight back at him, and for the first time in six weeks the man was the one who could not hold it.



Toren went up onto the shoulder at ten at night where Wyck was sitting with his leg out and his stick across his knees, and Dessa came up about four minutes later without being sent for, and sat down on the other side.

"I'm going to tell you the whole of it," Toren said.

Neither of them said anything.

"I'm not asking either of you whether you want it. I've thought about that all day and I've decided I don't get to make it a present, because if I ask you then you have to say yes and then it's yours because you chose it, and I don't want to do that to anybody again." His voice was steady, which surprised him. "It's mine and I'm putting it down. You can do what you like with it."

"Toren," said Dessa.

"There was a research station four hundred miles north of here in a bowl of hills and it was called Homura."



It took until about three in the morning.

He told it in the order he had been given it, which he understood by about the middle to be the wrong order, and he could not fix it because it was the only order he had. He told them about the machines and the tube and the nine thousand pictures, and about a charter that bought a physician for a village for six years. He told them about a man in a kitchen saying that it was a shame a machine could not show the difference between a man's own power and a stick's, and about a woman at the next table putting her cup down.

He told them her name.

He told them about two years in a field, and four more, and a word that came out of a coastal clan with two hundred words nobody else had, which he did not repeat, and told them why he was not repeating it.

He told them about the sixteenth of the eighth month nine years ago at about half past three in the afternoon, and a valve that had been dripping for a fortnight, and an instrument room, and a corridor.

He told them about the grip on Rook's Ember, worn through in one place at the bottom where no part of a hand naturally goes.

He told them about a village two years later and a contract on a tear and a bad night in bad ground, and about four people, and about how it took another two years to find out it was the same company. He told them about a tally-board in a trading post with a date on it.

And then he told them about the bread, and that was the one that finished all three of them, and it took him three goes to get it out.



The sky had started to go grey at the bottom edge.

"He does it because it's hers," Toren said. "He's done it every night for nine years and he doesn't know what it means and neither did she and neither did her grandmother. And Milo picked it up off him in four days on the grade without ever once being told, and he did it every night for six weeks, and he never knew, and I could have told him in one sentence, and I didn't."

Nobody spoke for a while.

"He'd have been unbearable," said Wyck.

Toren made a sound that was not a laugh and was in the same family.

"He'd have been completely unbearable about it," Wyck said. "He'd have told every single one of us he was doing an ancient thing. He'd have brought it up at least twice a day for the rest of his life. He'd have done it at Meridian in front of strangers." He shifted the leg with both hands. "And it would have been true, and he'd have been right, and he'd have got to be right about something for once."

Dessa had her forehead on her knees.

"I'm glad you told us," she said, into them. "I want that said now, tonight, in case it stops being true in a fortnight. I'm glad you told us and I'd have wanted it and I'm not going to take one single part of it back off you later."

"Nor me," said Wyck Tanner.

He got his stick under him and stood up, the careful way, in two parts.

"You've been carrying that on your own for four days," he said, "and I told you at the junction it was worse than the not knowing, and I want you to know I wasn't guessing."



Chapter 23: Nine Days

On the first morning, before any of the rest of it, Rook had gone across the weir four times.

He had brought the three of them up out of the tailwater pool and off the walkway and laid them out on the north apron above the flood line, side by side, with a canvas over them and a stone on each corner of it. He had done it before he lit the stove and before he went in to look at Wyck's leg, and he had not asked anybody to help and had not explained it, and Toren had watched him do it from the sluice-house door with his hands hanging and had understood, in a dull far-off way, that he was watching something he would be glad of later.

There was a fourth under the canvas by noon. Rook had gone up the north bank alone at about eleven and come down again, and he had not said one word about the winding-house then or at any point afterward, and none of them had ever asked, and Toren went the whole rest of his life without knowing the name of the woman who shot Milo Faust.



Whitlow came down the north bank at ten in the morning on the second day, alone, with his hands out from his sides and no coat on so that they could see he had nothing.

He stopped at the north end of the walkway.

"Anse Whitlow," he said, before anything else. "I'd rather you had it than not."

Rook went out fifteen feet and stopped in the same place he had stopped two nights before, and they stood on sixty feet of concrete with the weir going white between them, and for a while neither of them said anything at all.

"I'll say it before anything else," said Whitlow. "I've come for the bodies."

"They're up above the flood line under canvas. You'll want four men and a hurdle for Orsk, he's heavy."

Whitlow's head went back very slightly.

"You put them up."

"I put them up."

"Why?"

"Because they'd have gone down the water by this morning," said Rook, "and there's a hamlet four miles below us that's drinking it."



"Right," said Whitlow, after a moment. "Then here's mine, and I'd like it heard out, and then you can do as you like.

"Orsk is dead. Petrek is dead. Cadec is dead and so is the lad who came down that walkway first, and there are five of us left up that valley with a tear that isn't shut and a paper with Pell Hoy's name on it. Four days is what Orsk said and four days is what it is. Five people can do it. It'll be harder and it'll be uglier and one of us will probably get hurt doing it, and we're going to do it, because eleven houses put in everything they had.

"After that we're going up the line to Undern with what we've got and we'll weigh it and take what we're owed and the company will do what a company does at the frost, which is split and hire on and be somebody else's list by spring.

"That's nine days. Four and five. After the ninth day there's no such thing as Cinder Company anywhere on this continent until somebody writes it on a board again next summer, and I know that, and you know that, and I've known you knew it since I looked at you in a mill yard."

He put his hands down.

"So I'm going to stand here," said Anse Whitlow, "and you're going to decide, and I'd rather it happened on a walkway than in my sleep."



Toren was on the south apron eleven feet behind Rook with two feet of white light in his fist and no memory at all of having lit it.

Dessa was at his shoulder. Wyck was at the mouth of the walkway with his stick in the grass and his weight off the bad leg, and he had been standing like that for four minutes without moving, and Toren could hear him breathing.

"Five of you," said Rook.

"Five."

"Up a valley with a tear in it and no captain."

"No captain," said Whitlow, "and I'll tell you plain, I'd have had four days of arguing with Orsk about coming down here at all, and now I've got nobody to argue with and I've found I'd rather have the argument." He said it without any self-pity at all. "I'm sixty-one. I've been at this since I was nineteen and I've been with this company eleven years and there is not one single thing in that time I've done that I couldn't say at a table. Except one."

"Don't," said Rook.

"I've carried it nine years—"

"I said don't."

"Marsh," said Anse Whitlow.



Nothing happened.

That was the part Toren could not get over, standing on the concrete with a light in his fist. The weir went on making its noise. A bird went down the length of the river and turned at the bend. The man at the north end of the walkway stood with his hands down and his bare arms and the wind going through his shirt, and Rook stood fifteen feet out on the concrete with his hands in his pockets, and neither of them moved at all.

"Elias Marsh," said Whitlow. "That's what was on the list. Subject, west building, magic side. I read it off a page in a yard with the buildings going up behind me, and there were four names left on it that weren't crossed through, and yours was one of them, and I have known your face from that page and from a set of steps for nine years." His voice was perfectly level. "I've never said it out loud. Not to Orsk and not to any of them, because the day I say it, it's a thing the company owns and not a thing I do. And I've said it now because I'm standing on a river in my shirt and it's the only thing I've got that's worth anything, and because I'd like somebody who was there to have heard me say it before I go up that valley with five people and an open tear."



Rook did not say anything for a long time.

When he did, he did not say it to Whitlow. He turned his head about a quarter and spoke over his own shoulder to the three of them on the south apron, in the ordinary flat voice he used for fords and firewood and how long a leg was going to take.

"Here's the arithmetic," he said. "All of you get it and you get it now, because I'm not having anybody work it out for themselves in a month and get it wrong.

"There are five of them and one of me and they are four miles up that valley and they will be there for four days. I know exactly where. I know the ground, because I went and looked at it yesterday morning while you were all asleep, and it's a quarry cut with one way in, and I could be up there by two o'clock this afternoon.

"They would not all die. Some of them would run and I'd get most of those inside a week, because a man running from a thing like me runs where I'd run, and I've been doing this five years and I'm good at it. And the ones I didn't get here would be at Undern on the eighth or ninth day with everything they own on a scale in a market square, and I'd have them there, and after the ninth day I would never have any of them again as long as I live.

"That's what's in front of me. That's the whole company, every man who was on that ridge on the sixteenth of the eighth month, inside nine days, and I have wanted it for five years and I have walked two hundred and forty miles this season alone toward it and I have turned a cart east at a junction knowing I was giving it up, and it has come and found me anyway."

He stopped.

"And I'm not going," he said.



"Why," said Wyck Tanner.

It came out of him flat and fast and everybody heard exactly how long it had been sitting there, and Toren realized, with a jolt, that he had heard it before — in a yard in front of a burned wayhouse, in another life, about who stood at point.

Rook turned round.

"Because I'd have to leave you here," he said. "That's it. That's the only reason and there isn't a better one underneath it. There are three of you and one of you can't put weight on a leg for more than six hours and there's a woman in that sluice-house who is a fortnight from a machine that can see the whole of her at once and has been getting worse since the junction. And four miles of that valley is full of rift-things that came down on a light two nights ago and have not gone home.

"If I go up there this afternoon then you sit on this apron for a week and hope. And there is a version of this where I come back down that valley on the ninth day having done every single thing I've wanted to do for five years, and I come round that bend and there's a weir and nobody on it."

He put his hands in his pockets.

"I've stood in that exact place once already," he said. "I'm not doing it twice."



Wyck stood at the mouth of the walkway with his weight off the bad leg.

At the junction he had asked a question with the whole of his chest and had got an answer he did not want and had said right and started walking, and Toren had watched him do it and had not known, at the time, what it had cost him, because Toren had known everything else and had not known that.

He knew now.

"Right," said Wyck, and this time it was an entirely different word.



Whitlow took his dead away at noon.

He came back with four of them and a hurdle and they did it in two trips and they did not look at the south bank at all, and at the end of the second trip Whitlow stood on the north apron for a moment and put a hand up, once, not high, and then went up the bank after the others and out of sight.

"You didn't ask him," Toren said.

"No."

"He was there. He'd have known who paid—"

"He'd have known nothing." Rook was coiling the canvas cord round his hand. "A man that age in a company that size doesn't get told who bought the contract, he gets told where to stand. But he'd have given me a name, because I was standing over him on a walkway and he had five people up a valley, and a man will hand you a name to keep his teeth.

"And then I'd have had it. I'd have had a name I could not check, off a frightened man, for the rest of my life, and I would have gone and found whoever it belonged to." He put the cord in the barrow. "I told you on a step at a junction. I don't know their name. I'm not going to. That's not me being noble, Voss. That's me knowing exactly what I'm like."
