They ate at dusk on the south apron with the barrow loaded and the sluice-house swept out and the door pulled to, because they were leaving at first light and Rook did not want the morning to have any work in it.

Toren made the bread and broke the corner off the first one and put it on a flat stone at the edge of the coals.

Dessa watched him do it. She had been watching for two days, since three in the morning on a shoulder above a weir, and she was not good at not watching things yet.

Rook saw her watching.

Toren saw him see it, and saw the whole thing go across the man's face in about a second and a half — saw him understand that his own hands had been read, and by whom, and when, and saw him go back through four days and work out exactly which night it had happened on.

He did not say anything to Toren.

He looked at Dessa Cole for a moment, and then at Wyck Tanner, and then he said, to the fire:

"My name's Elias Marsh."



Nobody said anything.

That was the thing Toren felt, sitting with a piece of bread in his hands — not the name, though the name went through him like cold water and stayed. It was the shape of the quiet on the other side of it.

Because there was a place in that quiet where a voice should have gone. There was a gap about four seconds wide, immediately after, that had been filled every single time for six weeks — at a fire under an arch in the rain, in a yard between two tanks, on a ballast bed at the top of the world — by a boy with nothing in his hands who asked everything, out loud, without any idea that he was doing them all a kindness.

"Elias," said Senna Voss, from the barrow.

"Yes."

"That's a name." She resettled the blanket. "The other one isn't."

"No," said Rook. "It isn't."

"Well," said his grandmother. "You can have both. Most people only get the one."



They went east at first light.

There were two on the shafts now instead of one. That was the practical shape of it and it had been worked out the previous evening without discussion: Rook in the traces, Toren on the near shaft, Wyck on the off one for as long as the leg would take it and then not, and Dessa out front where she could stand in it.

At about the second hour Dessa came back down the line with the water can and gave it out.

Senna first. Then Wyck, because of the leg. Then whoever was on watch, which was nobody, so she skipped it and went on. Then everybody else.

She did it without any ceremony whatsoever. Nobody had told her to and nobody remarked on it, and the next morning it was Toren who did it, and the morning after that it was Wyck, and it went on being the order in which water happened for the whole of the rest of that road.



The light on the stone went out on the third night.

It had been up on the top of the chute above the winding-house since a quarter past eleven on the night of the weir. It had burned through the fight and through the morning after and through a burying and through two more days of grey weather, small and pale and entirely unsupported, needing nobody.

They were eleven miles east by then and could not have seen it.

Toren woke at some point in that night and lay on his side in the grass with his coat over him and his hand flat on a pocket with two rows of stitches in it, and listened for the fourth beat of his grandmother's cough. He did not think about the light at all.

He was never afterward able to forgive himself for that, which Rook told him, much later, was the most ordinary thing about him.



Chapter 24: The Fourth Beat

It was Toren's turn with the water can on the sixth morning off the weir, and he got it wrong.

He did the order the way it was done. Senna first, in the barrow, who took two swallows and said the water was better than it had been yesterday, which it was not. Then Wyck, because of the leg. Then he turned to hand it to whoever was on watch, and stood there for about two seconds with his arm out and nobody in front of it.

Nobody said anything. Dessa took the can out of his hand without any particular expression and drank and passed it to Rook, and they went on walking.

That was the whole of it. It happened perhaps once a day and it happened to all of them and they had all worked out, separately and without discussing it, that the correct thing to do was let it happen and keep going.



They had come off the Reave on the second morning and onto the old road, and the old road was a different country.

It had been built. That was the thing Toren kept noticing about it. It went where it wanted to go instead of where the water went, and where a hill had been in the way somebody had taken a piece out of the hill, and there were still low concrete posts standing at intervals along the verge with nothing left on top of them. It was wide enough for four carts abreast. Grass had it in the middle and small trees had the edges, but the crown of it was hard under the wheels and the barrow ran on it better than it had run on anything since the grade.

"Eleven days," Rook said, on the first afternoon. "Maybe twelve. It's a road, so it's faster, and it's a road, so everything else on it is faster too. Nobody walks ahead any more. Cole stays inside a hundred feet."

Dessa said "clear" every ten minutes, in her flat voice, and did not go ahead.



Wyck cooked, on the fourth night, and it was poor.

He got the stone too hot and the first two cakes came off burned on one face and wet in the middle, and he did the second batch with the stone pulled back off the coals and those were better and still not right. Nobody remarked on it. Senna ate hers and said nothing, which from Senna Voss was an act of enormous violence against her own nature.

He broke the corner off the first one and put it on the flat stone at the edge of the fire before he handed anything round.

Toren watched him do it. Wyck did it carefully, the way a man does a thing he has been taught and does not want to get wrong, and it was completely unlike the way Milo had done it, which had been the way you put the lid back on a crock.

Rook looked at the piece of bread on the rock for about three seconds, the way he had every night for nine years, and then ate his own and said nothing.

"Should we stop?" Dessa said, afterward, when Rook had gone out to walk the road.

"Stop what?"

"That." She did not point at it. "I want somebody else to have the thought so it isn't only mine. He did it because he'd seen it done. He didn't know what it was. We know what it is and we're doing it anyway and I can't work out whether that's the right way round or the worst possible—"

"We're not stopping," said Wyck.

"I'm not saying stop. I'm saying I've thought about stopping and I'd like that not to be a secret."

"Right," said Wyck. "It's not a secret. We're still not stopping."



On the sixth day, in the middle of the afternoon, Senna Voss put her hand on the front board of the barrow and said, "Set it down."

Rook set it down.

He did it without asking why, which Toren noticed, and which told him what was about to happen about four seconds before it happened.

"Sit," she said. "All of you. On the road, I don't care, it's dry. This will take six minutes and I've had eleven days to get it in order so don't help me with it."

They sat down on the crown of the old road in a line. Rook stayed standing with his hands in his pockets.

"There's a fourth beat in my cough," said Senna Voss.

Dessa's head came round.

"It started before the junction. It's three and then a pause and then a fourth one, lower down, and it's the sound of a thing having got into a part of me it wasn't in before, and I have had it explained to me by two people in twenty years and they both used the same words." She had her hands folded on the blanket. "My grandson has known since two nights before the junction. He has lain on his side in the grass every single night since, with his eyes open, waiting for it, and he has told nobody, and he was going to go on telling nobody all the way to the wall."

Toren looked at the road.

"Now I'm going to say the part I actually got you down here for," she said, "because that first bit's only the reason. Seven weeks ago there was a man on this road who knew a thing about a boy and wouldn't say it, and it cost a different boy the whole of a summer, and everybody standing here watched it happen and everybody standing here agreed afterward that it was a bad way to run a family. And then the minute one of you got hold of something heavy, you did exactly the same thing with it."

"That's not the same," Toren said.

"It's the identical thing, and you're thirteen, so you don't get to say it isn't." She said it without heat. "You thought you were sparing us. He thought he was sparing Tanner. Every man who has ever done it has thought he was sparing somebody, and not one of them has ever once asked the somebody."

Nobody said anything for a while. A bird went over, going south.

"I'd have wanted to know," Dessa said.

"I know you would."

"I'd have—" She stopped, and started again, and her voice was perfectly level. "I put my hands on you on the second day out of the weir and I had a rate off your breathing and I wrote it in my head and I've had it since, and I knew something was wrong with the shape of it, and I asked him"— she did not look at Toren —"and he said you were tired."

"He did," said Senna. "Well. He's learning it off a man who's had thirty years' practice and he's picked up the worst of it first, which is normal. Voss."

Toren looked up.

"I'm not angry with you," his grandmother said. "I want that heard, because you'll make it into something by tonight otherwise, and I'd rather do the work now than dig you out of it at midnight. You were carrying it because there wasn't anybody to hand it to. There is now. That's all that's changed and it's the only thing I wanted changed."



"How long," said Wyck.

He said it to Rook, not to her, and everybody understood why, and Senna let him.

"I'm not going to give you a date."

"I'm not asking for a date. I'm asking what you've got."

Rook took his hands out of his pockets.

"I've had my hands on her three times since the weir," he said. "Here's what I can actually tell you and I'll tell you the whole of it. Four weeks ago a night's sleep gave her back most of a day. Two weeks ago it gave her back about half. Nine days ago it gave her back an hour and a half and she spent the hour and a half organising a burial, and she has not got any of it back since.

"That's not a number and it's not a date. That's a slope, and I've watched enough of them to tell you that a slope like that one does not flatten out on its own, and that the distance between where she is now and the place where she stops being able to take a road is shorter than eleven days is long."

"Then we go faster," said Toren.

"We go exactly the speed we're going," Rook said. "If I push that barrow another mile a day I'll get her there four days earlier in a condition where the machine can see the whole of her and nobody can do one useful thing about what it shows them. She's not a cask of ash. You don't get her there sooner by carrying her harder."

Senna Voss said, "That's the first sensible thing anybody's said on this road in a week."

"You're welcome."

"I didn't say thank you."



They went on, and made four more miles before dark, and camped in the lee of a bank where the road had been cut through a rise.

It was a better evening than it had any right to be. That was the thing Toren could not account for afterward. Dessa got the stove going and did not do it in Milo's order and did not do it in her own, she did it in the order that was fastest, and complained about the wind; and Wyck said something about her cooking that was genuinely unkind and genuinely funny; and Senna made all four of them tell her what the road had looked like that day, one at a time, because she had been facing backward in a barrow since the fifth of the month and had seen the whole of this journey going away from her.

Toren sat with his back against the bank and found, at about nine in the evening, that his chest had come loose for the first time in five days, and that he did not have to decide whether that was allowed.

Then Rook stood up and said, "Voss. Out here."



They went forty paces down the road to where it was flat and open and the grass was short.

"Right," Rook said. "Put it out."

Toren lit up and got two feet of white standing steady out of his father's Ember, which he could now do on a bad day in a wind while thinking about something else.

"Good. Now put it out."

He put it out.

"You've had that thing since the fifth of the month and you've used one half of it," said Rook. "Tonight you start on the other half, and you're going to be worse at it than you have been at anything since the road, and I want you to hear that from me now instead of finding it out on your own in about ninety seconds."

Toren's mouth went dry.

"Stride."

"Stride."

"Why now?"

"Because it doesn't sit in your hand," Rook said. "Edge sits in your hand, and when you drop it, you've dropped a light. Stride sits in your legs. When you drop that you go down at whatever speed you were doing, on whatever's under you, and if you happen to be eleven feet in the air at the time you go down from eleven feet.

"There is no version of putting that in a boy who loses the first one when a bird gets up. So I've had it in my pocket for seven weeks waiting on you, and at that weir you held two feet of white through the worst ninety minutes of your life and did not put it out one single time, and that's the whole of the reason it's tonight and not tomorrow and not in the spring."



It was not like anything.

That was the first thing and it was the only useful thing Toren learned in the first hour. Edge had always started as a reach — a going-to, a thing he did with the front of himself. Stride did not want the front of him at all. It wanted a place low down and behind, under the hinge of the hip, and it wanted him to not be holding on with his feet, and his feet had been holding on since he learned to walk.

He got it on the ninth try and it lasted about a second and a half and put him flat on his back in the road.

"Good," said Rook.

"That's not good."

"That's the right kind of bad. Again."

He got it eleven more times and went down eleven more times, and by the eighth of those he had learned to take the fall on his shoulder instead of his elbow, which was a thing his body had worked out entirely without him. His shins were skinned. There was grit in the heel of his right hand, which was the hand that did not fully close and which now would not fully open either.

At about eleven at night he sat down on the verge with his forearms across his knees and could not get up for a while.

"Will it come faster than the other one did?"

"No idea," said Rook, sitting down on the concrete post opposite. "Tanner's had one for two years and can't do it at all. Cole hasn't got anything to do it with. I did it in four months and I was twenty-six and I had somebody standing in a field telling me what I looked like from the outside, which is worth more than anything I've said to you tonight."

"Vera."

"Vera."

Toren turned his father's Ember over in his hands. The band around the middle was worn glass-smooth where four generations of Voss thumbs had sat, and his own thumb had started, very slightly, to find the same place without being asked.

"Can you do both at once?"

"Yes."

"Both. Edge and Stride. Together."

"Yes," said Rook.

"How?"

Rook did not answer for a moment.

"That's the one I can't say," he said.

"You say everything. You've said—"

"I've said everything I can put in a sentence. I've told you what the room is and what the door is and why you were thanking a stick, and none of it ever once made the light come, and you know that better than anybody alive because you stood in wet grass for a fortnight agreeing with me and getting nothing." He had his hands on his knees. "This one's worse than that. I've had it since before your father could walk and I have never got it into words, not for her, not for you, not for myself at two in the morning. Everybody I've ever watched do it did it in the middle of something going wrong, and every single one of them was asked afterward how, and every single one of them said the same thing, which is nothing useful."

"Then what do I do?"

"You go on doing the thing you did tonight, poorly, every night, until your legs stop arguing," said Rook. "And then one day something will happen too fast for you to pick, and you'll find out whether you stopped believing that you had to."



He could not sleep.

He lay on his side in the grass with his coat over him, on the bank of a made road with the bad hand curled against his chest, and listened, and got three small dry sounds and a pause long enough to believe in, and then the fourth, lower, coming up from somewhere further down.

After a while he took the disk out of the pocket with two rows of stitches in it and held it flat on his palm in the dark.

Nine narrow channels ran across the face of it. He had been doing this since the fifth of the month — looking at them and trying to make the pattern into a shape, and failing, and looking away, and looking back and finding it had rearranged itself, which he knew perfectly well was not true. He had asked Dessa once to look at it and she had said it's nine lines, Toren, and had not seen anything else in it at all.

It was warm the way a room you had lived in was warm.

Seven weeks ago his father had knelt in wet grass and closed his fingers over it one at a time and told him to put it in a river if it ever came to it, and Toren had said yes, and had been lying, and both of them had known.

He put it back in the pocket and lay with his hand flat over the stitches.

Eleven days.



In the morning Rook stood at the shafts and looked south down the road before he picked them up.

"One thing before we go," he said. "So nobody has to hear it for the first time when it matters.

"The last three days of this road are the worst ground any of you have walked and the worst I've walked in six years. Meridian sits where it sits because there's a building in it that nobody could move, and everything that's happened to this continent since has happened around it, and the tears in that country have been opening since before I was born. That's not a story. That's why there's a wall there and not somewhere nicer.

"You'll know when we're in it. Everybody knows when they're in it. And when we are, the drilling stops, and the lights stop, and everything I've spent seven weeks putting in your hands goes in your pockets and stays there until I say otherwise."

"Why?" said Wyck.

"Work it out."

Wyck worked it out. Toren watched him do it, and watched his face go.

"Because they come at the nearest one," Wyck said.

"Because they come at the nearest one," said Rook, and picked up the shafts.



Chapter 25: Standing Ash

By the third night Toren could hold Stride standing still for five seconds, and could not take one step in it.

That was the shape of the problem and it did not get any better for saying it out loud. The frame came up his legs the colour of a cold morning, hip to heel, and it held him — he could feel the road through it the way you feel the floor through a chair — and the instant he asked either foot to leave the ground the whole thing went off like a lamp in a draught and he went down.

"You're telling your legs to run," said Rook, from the verge.

"I'm telling my legs to walk."

"You're telling them. That's the whole of it." He had his coat collar up and had not moved in an hour. "Do it again and this time don't decide which foot."

Toren did it again and went down on his shoulder, which by now he could do without thinking about it, and lay in the grass looking up at a sky the colour of a cook pot.

"That was worse," Rook said.

"I know it was worse."

"Good. Again."



On the fourth morning they met the haulers coming north.

There were nine of them with three carts and two dogs, and they had the carts loaded wrong — heavy at the back, tools on top where they should have been under, rope thrown on in a heap instead of coiled. Toren registered all of that before he registered a single face, and understood about a half-second later that he had registered it because Rook had spent seven weeks teaching him to look at a load and tell how much time the people packing it had thought they had.

They came up the crown of the road at a pace that was faster than good for the dogs.

The woman in front was about fifty, broad, grey-haired, with a hauler's shoulders and a strip of cloth bound round one hand.

"Rell," she said, which Toren took a moment to understand was her name.

"Marsh."

"You're going south."

"We are."

"Don't."

Rook set the shafts down.

"Say it out," he said, "and I'll hear the whole of it, and then I'll tell you what I'm going to do, and neither of us has to argue about it."



Rell put her bound hand on the side of her cart.

"We had a paper on the Drownings," she said. "Old quarry, nine mile short of the lamps, flooded these thirty years, and there's a tear in the lip of it that's been open since the spring and the Register put it out to contract in the sixth month and we took it. Eleven of us. We've been in that ground since the eighth month and we've had a good season off it — eight casks up the road in five loads and every one of them weighed honest at the gate.

"Four days ago Corb and Idony went down into the workings after a pair of Flask and didn't come up. We went down after them the next morning, all of us, in daylight, which I want said, because I've had it said to me twice already this week that we went at night."

"Did you find them?"

"We found where they went to ash," said Rell. "Both of them. On the floor of the workings, side by side, in the open. And I want you to hear the part that I've not been able to get anybody at the lamps to hear yet, because it's the only part that matters and it sounds like nothing.

"There wasn't enough of it."

Rook's face did not change.

"Say that again."

"There wasn't enough of it," said Rell. "Two grown people go to ash and you get what you get, and I've been at this twenty-two years and I know what a person leaves on the ground. There was about a hand of it each. Grey, cold, sat up on the surface like flour on a table. And it hadn't gone in. It sat there for as long as we stood and looked at it and it never went in."

"Ash goes in," said Dessa.

"Ash goes in," Rell agreed. "It goes in in about four minutes and it goes in on stone and it goes in on wet and I have watched it go into the deck of a boat. That did not go in." She took her hand off the cart. "So we came up, and we came off the contract, and we've left eight days' work and a good rope in the bottom of that hole, and I'll be paying the Register back for the rest of my life, and Mother's mercy, I'd do it again tomorrow."



"What's the gate like?" said Rook.

It came out flat and short and in the middle of nothing, and Toren watched Rell answer it without ever once noticing she had been asked anything.

"Tight. Tighter than the spring. They've had two hundred come up that road since the harvest and they're taking about one in three and they want everything written down twice." She jerked her chin at the barrow. "You've got a sick one. That'll get you a hearing and it won't get you a bed, so don't walk up there thinking it will."

"And if we go round the workings?"

"Two days east and you'll be in the fen and you'll want four days, and there's nothing in the fen but the fen." She looked at him for a second longer than she needed to. "You're not going round."

"No."

"No," said Rell. "I could see it off you." She got her hand back on the cart and put her weight into it. "The tip road across the middle's good stone and it's twelve foot wide and it's eighty yard end to end. If it were me I'd be on it at noon with the light on top of me and I'd not stop in the middle for anything on this earth. That's all I've got and I've given you the whole of it."

"You have," said Rook. "Thank you."

She got four paces and stopped and looked back.

"There were eleven of us," she said.



They went south.

Nobody said anything about it for about a mile. Then Wyck said, "Two days round," and Rook said, "And four in the fen, and she's got eleven days in her and I'm not spending six of them in standing water," and that was the whole of the conversation and everybody had known how it was going to go before it started.

Senna slept most of that afternoon, which she had begun to do.



The country went bad the way weather comes on.

Toren could not put a milepost on it afterward and neither could anybody else. There was no line to cross. It was simply that at some point on the fifth day he noticed the road had gone quiet in a way that had nothing to do with sound, and then noticed that he had been noticing it for an hour.

The trees along the verge stopped. Not all at once and not in a line — they thinned, and then there were only the small hard ones, and then there were none of those either and the road ran out across open ground with nothing on it but grass the colour of a dirty sheep.

The grass was wrong too. It grew in patches with bare ground between, and the bare ground was not earth. It was pale grey and very fine and it took a bootprint and kept it.

"Don't kick it up," said Rook.

"What is it?"

"It's four years of a hunting ground," he said. "This is the north lip of the workings and everything anybody's killed up here since the tear opened has gone into this ground. It's full. That's all that is. A ground gets to a point where it won't take any more and then it sits on top."

"It's not doing anything," said Wyck.

"No. It's ash, and ash is the leftovers of a thing with nobody home, and it does not do anything at all." Rook kept walking. "It's a nuisance, is what it is. Keep out of it and don't breathe it and don't harvest off this ground, because whatever you pull up here will be four years old and mixed with dirt and it'll weigh dirty at the gate."

Dessa had stopped.



She was standing in the middle of the road with her chin up and her eyes open on nothing at all, and she stayed that way for long enough that Wyck went back for her.

"Cole."

"Give me a minute."

"Have you got something?"

"No," said Dessa. "That's what I'm telling you. I haven't got anything and I can't work out whether that's because there's nothing or because there's too much." Her hands had come up, and she was holding them out from her sides a little, the way a person does on ice. "It's like — right. All right. You know when a room's got a lot of people talking in it, and you can't pick out one voice, and you'd say the room was loud. This isn't loud. This is a room where everybody's saying the same word."

"Range?"

"I've got eighty feet," she said. "Eighty. I had three hundred at the weir."

Rook set the shafts down.

"Say that again."

"Eighty feet and it isn't dropping off with distance, it's just stopping. I put it out and it goes eighty feet and it hits the same thing everywhere at once." She brought her hands down. "I'm not broken. I want that said before anybody says it kindly at me. The thing I do still works. There's nothing left in this country for it to work on."

"You're not broken," said Rook. "You're standing in a field full of shouting and I should have told you it was coming and I didn't, because I've never been out here with a sensor and I didn't know." He said it flatly and immediately, without any apparatus round it, which Toren had noticed he did about once a month and which was worth more than an apology. "Right. New rule, and it's the whole of the next three days.

"Everything goes in your pockets. Embers away. Nobody lights anything for any reason. Nobody drills, nobody practises, nobody puts up so much as an inch of it to see whether they still can. Cole keeps her eighty feet and that's the only worked power on this road until I say different, and I'm allowing it because she can't put it down any more than she can stop hearing."

"And if something comes?" said Toren.

"Then it comes at the nearest one, and the nearest one will be her, and that is exactly why she walks in the middle from now on and none of you argue with me about it once."



Toren put his father's Ember in his coat at about four in the afternoon on the fifth day and did not take it out again.

He had not been without it in his hand for any waking hour in three weeks. He had drilled while walking, while eating, while arguing, while pulling a shaft; two feet of white standing out of his fist had become as much a part of the road as the road. His hand felt like somebody else's for the rest of that day.

He understood, at about dusk, that he was frightened in a way he had not been since the pumphouse, and that the reason was not the country. It was that Rook had taken the tool away.



They found the Flask on the morning of the sixth day.

It was lying half off the crown of the road with its limbs folded under it in the wrong number of places, already gone, and the shape of it was still there in the grey on the ground the way the shape of a burned beam stays in a fire.

Rook crouched down beside it for some time.

"How long?" said Wyck.

"Day. Day and a half." He put a finger into it and took it out and looked at what was on the finger. "That's a Flask. That's a good clean Flask and a fair one, and somebody put it down properly with one stroke from the left side."

"Then why's it still here?"

"Because whoever did it didn't take the ash," said Rook.

He stood up and looked north along the road, and then south, and then out across the grey ground.

"And there's not a Flask's worth on this road," he said. "There's about a Handful. So they took some and left the rest, which is the single stupidest thing I have ever heard of anybody doing in this country, and there is no version of a hunter who kills a Flask with one stroke and then harvests it like a child picking blackberries."

"Rell's people?" said Dessa.

"Rell's people went north four days ago with three carts and they'd never have left it." He was still looking out at the grey. "Somebody's out here working poorly. That's what it is. Somebody's out here who's frightened, and frightened people harvest poorly, and I'd rather it were that than anything else I can think of."

Toren, standing eleven feet away with his hands in his coat, looked at the ground where the ash had not gone in.

Then he looked at the ground beside it.

There was a patch there, about four feet across, where the grey was gone. Not scuffed and not blown — gone, clean down to wet dark earth with grass starting in it, and it had a soft edge all the way round like a puddle drying.

"Rook."

"Yes."

"It's gone in there."

Rook came over and looked at it and put the flat of his hand down on the dark ground and held it there, and after a moment he took it off and wiped it on his coat.

"Good," he said. "That's the ground still working. That's what I wanted to see."

They went on south.

Toren looked back once, from about two hundred paces, because something about the shape of it had not sat right, and from two hundred paces he could see four more patches of dark ground between the Flask and the lip of the workings, all about the same size, all clean down to the earth.

They were in a line.

He thought about mentioning it and did not, because he could not have said what he would be mentioning, and because he had been wrong out loud twice that week already.

He was never afterward able to decide whether it would have made any difference.



They camped that night in the angle of a concrete wall that had been a weighbridge house, with no fire, eating cold, with the barrow backed into the corner and the four of them lying across the open side of it.

Senna was awake.

"Say it," she said, to the dark.

"Say what."

"Whatever it is you've been keeping behind your teeth since noon, Elias. You've done nine miles with a jaw like a gate bolt."

Rook was quiet for a moment.

"There's a thing in the Drownings," he said. "I've thought so since the road and I've been certain since the Flask, and I've spent the afternoon trying to find a way that I'm wrong, and I can't.

"Two people who knew their work went down in daylight and left a hand of ash each. There's a Flask on the road with one stroke through it and most of it gone. There's a ground full to the top for four years and then five clean patches in a line running down to the lip. And there's a woman who's been at this twenty-two years who walked off the best contract of her life and is going to spend the rest of it paying the Register back, and who looked at me on that road and told me not to go, and I have met exactly four people in my life who would have done that."

"And?"

"And it's not a Flask and it's not a lot of Flask," said Rook. "That's a Barrel."

Nobody moved.

Toren lay on his side in the dark and found that the word had gone into him in a different place from where words usually went. He had heard it every week since Tallow Gate. It was what a warden said over a scale. It was a grade and a price and a thing men argued about in yards, and in seven weeks he had never once heard it used about something that was still alive.

"You've seen one," said Wyck.

"I've seen two," said Rook. "One at nineteen, from about a mile off, going away, and I have never been so glad of anything. And one at thirty-four, with eleven other people, and four of them were better at this than I am and two of them didn't walk out."

"What is it?"

"I don't know," said Rook. "That's the honest answer and you're all going to get the honest answer tonight because tomorrow you'll get orders instead. Nobody knows what any of them are. Homura had eleven years and nine thousand pictures and the best it ever got to was a guess, and the guess was that the things that come through aren't creatures at all — that they're the stuff itself, the same stuff that's in your hands and in her hands and in that ground, come through a hole with nothing to hold it in a shape and nobody home inside it.

"And the guess went on: that the different kinds are different kinds because power does different things when people work it, and these are those same things gone loose with nobody doing them." He shifted against the wall. "Which means every one of them is a thing somebody, somewhere, does on purpose. And when you meet a new one, the useful question is never what is it. It's what does it look like a person doing."

"And that one?"

"I'll tell you tomorrow," said Rook, "when I've seen it."



At first light he stood up on the wall of the weighbridge house and looked south, and the rest of them came up after him.

The road ran on for two miles across the grey ground and then went over a lip and down.

Past the lip there was a great open bite out of the country, a mile and a half across, with sheer grey faces stepping down into it and standing water in the bottom of it the colour of the sky. A spine of stone ran straight out across the middle of the water from the near side to the far side, pale and narrow and perfectly level, with the flooded workings forty feet deep on both sides of it.

And nine miles beyond the far lip, small and low and quite clear in the flat morning air, there was a ring of lights.

They were not in a wall. That was the thing Toren could not make sit right, and he stood on the concrete for a long time working at it. They were out in the open ground, on poles, in a circle perhaps half a mile across with nothing inside it and nothing beside it, and they had been burning all night in empty country with nobody anywhere near them.

"What are those?" said Dessa.

"Those are Meridian," said Rook.



Chapter 26: The Drownings

"Say the failure," Rook said.

"Anybody stops on the stone," said Toren.

He said it before anyone else had drawn breath and he said it flat, and Rook looked at him for about a second and a half and then went on as if he had not.

"That's it. That's the whole of it and there's nothing underneath it.

"That road out there is eighty yards long and twelve feet wide and it is the only clean ground within four miles. Everything on this side of it is ash to a depth of about a foot, and everything on the far side is the same, and between the two of them there is a spine of quarry stone with forty feet of water on either hand and nothing in it and nothing under it and nothing for anything to come up out of.

"We go over that at noon in the middle of the day with the light straight down. We do not go fast, because going fast on that road with a barrow puts a wheel over the edge and then we have lost this before anything has even happened. We go at a walk, and we go the whole eighty yards, and nobody stops."

He put a finger up.

"And on the way down to it, and on the way off the far end, nobody lights anything and nobody kills anything. If a Flask comes at you, you give it ground. If it follows you, you give it more ground. If it puts a hand on you, you go down and you cover your head and you let it, and one of us will come and take it off you, and it will hurt and you will keep your arm."

"That's not—" Wyck stopped.

"Say it."

"That's not a thing you can promise a person they'll do."

"No," said Rook. "It isn't."



They came down off the lip at nine in the morning.

The haul road went down the side of the workings in four long slants with a turn at each end, and it had been cut for carts, and forty years of nothing had not done much to it. The grey came down with them. It lay in the corners of the turns in drifts a foot deep and it lay thin across the open faces, and where a wheel went through it, it did not puff up and hang the way dust does. It sat where the wheel put it.

Toren walked at the near shaft with both hands on the wood and his father's Ember in his coat pocket, and made himself look at the country.

That was Rook's rule from the second week and it had never once been relaxed: you look at ground before you are standing on it, because on it you will only ever see the four feet in front of you. So he looked. He made himself take the whole of it, the way he had taken a walkway and a weir.

The floor of the workings was a flat grey pan a quarter of a mile across with standing water over most of it and bare stone in patches where the water had gone. The tip road came off the near side at the bottom of the last slant and went out across it dead straight, built up eight or ten feet above the water on a spine of stacked block, pale and hard-edged, with no rail of any kind.

At the far end it ran up onto the south bank and there was a second haul road going up and out, and past that, over the lip, nine miles of open country and a ring of lights that were not burning now because it was morning.

And on the near side, between the bottom of the slant and the start of the stone, there was about a hundred and eighty yards of open pan.

Grey. Unbroken. Four years deep.

"There's the hundred and eighty," said Rook, without being asked. "That's the whole of it. Everything before that and everything after is somebody else's problem. That hundred and eighty yards is ours."



They waited at the bottom of the last slant for two hours, in the shadow of the face, and did not talk much.

Senna slept for some of it and woke and did not ask anything. Wyck sat with his bad leg out and worked the knee with both thumbs, the way he did before anything, which he had started doing at Tallow Gate and had never once been teased about by anybody.

Dessa stood.

"Anything?"

"Eighty feet of the same word," she said. "I'll tell you the second it changes."

At about half past eleven Toren said, quietly, to nobody in particular: "We'd have sent Milo up the far bank."

Wyck's thumbs stopped.

"With the light," Toren said. "On a stone at the top of the south lip, a quarter mile off the road, where anything coming out of that water would have it in front of it the whole way. And we'd have walked across this in a queue with our hands in our pockets and never seen a thing."

"Yes," said Wyck. "We would."

Neither of them said anything else about it. Rook, eleven feet away with his back against the stone face and his hat over his eyes, did not move at all, and Toren could not afterward say whether he had been listening.



At noon Rook stood up and picked up the shafts and they went out onto the pan.

It was better than Toren had let himself expect for about ninety seconds.

The grey was firm underfoot, packed hard by four years of carts, and the barrow ran on it; and the sun was straight down out of a white sky and there were no shadows anywhere at all; and the stone of the tip road was a clean pale line in front of them getting closer at the rate a person walks.

At a hundred and ten yards Dessa said, "Six."

She said it in the flat voice.

"Where."

"North-west. Out of the water at the edge of the pan, where it's shallow." Her chin had come up. "They're not coming at us. They're going up the slant."

"Behind us?"

"Behind us."

"Then we keep walking," said Rook, "and nobody looks round."

Toren kept walking and did not look round and discovered that not looking round took about as much of him as everything else he had ever been asked to do.

At eighty yards Dessa said, "Two of them have turned."

"Distance."

"Two hundred. Coming on the pan." A pause of three seconds. "They're faster on this than they were at the weir."

"Of course they are," said Rook. "It's a floor."



At fifty yards the first of them came up on the right side.

It was a Flask. Toren had its whole shape at once — slate-grey and wet-looking with the light going off it wrong, every limb with one joint too many so that it did not step but folded and unfolded forward, and the seam down its chest lit up dull like a coal under ash.

It came in at the barrow's right rear quarter, which is to say at Wyck, who was on the off shaft with his stick hung on the board because both his hands were on the wood.

He gave it ground. He did it exactly as he had been told, without one instant of argument: he let go of the shaft and went four steps left behind the barrow and put the whole width of it between them, and the thing folded round the back of the barrow after him.

And then it went past him.

Toren saw it happen from eleven feet away with the shafts in his hands. The thing came round the tailboard and it did not follow Wyck at all, it went in over the tailboard, and there was an old woman in a barrow with a splinted leg and no way of getting anywhere, and she was the nearest thing in the world with nothing in front of her.

Wyck Tanner put three feet of white light through it from below the ribs.

It came apart. The ash went up in a soft grey wash over the tailboard and over Senna's blanket and over Wyck's forearms, and he stood there with his weight all on the good leg and his mouth open and his Edge still out, and it did not go down. It hung. It sat on the air for a moment like flour thrown in a kitchen and then it settled onto the grey that was already there and lay on top of it.

"I had to," Wyck said.

"You had to," said Rook.

"She was—"

"You had to. Put it out and get back on the shaft and walk."

Wyck put it out. He got back on the shaft. They walked.

Forty yards.



At twenty-five yards the ash went sideways.

That was all it did. There was no sound and nothing moved on the pan and the light did not change; the grey wash lying on the ground behind the barrow simply drew off to the left, all of it, at about the speed water goes down a drain, in a thin line running north-west across the pan and away.

"Rook," said Dessa.

"I see it."

"I've lost the word."

"What?"

"The word. The everywhere one." Her hands had come up from her sides. "It's gone. There's nothing in eighty feet. There's nothing in eighty feet in any direction and there hasn't been for four seconds and that is not—"

The pan opened.



It did not come up the way the others came up.

The others came out of water or out of cover, folding, one part of them at a time, and you could watch it happen and know what you were looking at before it finished. This did not arrive. Sixty feet off the left side of the barrow a circle of the grey floor about four yards across went soft, and sank, and then stood up.

It stood up grey-white, the colour of the ground, and it kept standing up, and by the time it had stopped it was higher than the barrow was long.

It had the joints. That was the only thing about it Toren recognized and it was the worst thing about it, because it had them everywhere — not one extra to a limb but a dozen, twenty, more than his eye could hold, so that nothing on it folded and unfolded and the whole of it flowed instead, like a sack of water with too many corners. It had no seam down the chest. It was lit all through, dull and even, the way a bank of coals is lit under its own ash, and the light moved inside it slowly and did not come to the surface anywhere.

It had no eyes. It put itself round to face them without turning.

"Down," said Rook.

"What is it—"

"Down."

They went down. Toren went flat on the grey with his cheek in it and the shaft across his back and his father's Ember in his coat under his own chest, and Dessa went down beside him with her arm over her head, and Wyck did not get down because Wyck could not get down fast, and stood at the tailboard with his hands on the wood and his face perfectly white.

Rook put the shafts down and walked out from the barrow at an angle, eleven paces, twelve, and stopped.

"Right," he said, in an entirely ordinary voice. "Now I know what it looks like a person doing."



Toren, with his face in the ash, heard him say it, and it took until the end of that day for him to work out what he had heard.

He had watched a man do it in a road on the fifth of the month, in the worst hour of his life, kneeling over a woman with a broken leg: a flat palm laid down on the ground with the grey still crawling in it, and the ground giving it back up, and the light coming off the earth into a hand.

He had watched him do it eleven times since.

Every kill they had ever made, every Flask and every Handful, had gone into whatever ground it fell on and stayed there. Four years of a hunting ground. Two people in the bottom of a working, with a hand of ash left where there should have been a great deal more.

There was something out here doing the only trick Rook had that the rest of them could not learn, and it had been doing it on this pan since the spring, and it had no hands and nobody home.



The next part took perhaps forty seconds and Toren got it in pieces.

Rook went at it. He went at it from the left with the frame coming up around his legs so fast that the grey went out from under him in a sheet, and the spear came out of Vera's Ember at half length because he was inside four feet of it before it had finished turning, and he put the whole of it through the thing at the height of a man's chest and took it out the other side.

The Breach came apart.

It came apart the way they all came apart — the shape let go all at once and the whole of it went down into grey, four yards of it, a fall of ash like a wall of grain going over, and Toren's chest did something violent and stupid that was almost relief.

And then the grey on the floor of the pan drew in, and it stood up again, fifteen feet to the left, and it was the same size it had been.

Rook was already moving. He did it again, from the other side, and it came apart again, and it stood up again eleven feet further on toward the tip road, and this time it was very slightly smaller and Toren did not notice that at all.

"It doesn't—" Dessa's voice had gone up. "It doesn't die—"

"It dies," said Rook. He had come back to the barrow at a flat run and got the shafts up in one motion. "It dies exactly the way everything else out here dies. It just doesn't stay down while it's standing in a field of itself."

"Then—"

"Then we do the thing we were already doing," said Rook, "faster."



Twenty-five yards of grey to the stone, and the thing was between them and it.

That was the arithmetic and everybody standing on that pan had it at the same moment. It had come apart twice and each time it had stood up nearer the tip road than it had gone down, and it had not done that on purpose because it could not do anything on purpose, and it did not matter in the least that it could not.

"Cole," said Rook. "Take the near shaft. Tanner, the off. Voss, on the tailboard and push and do not stop pushing whatever you hear.

"You are going for the stone. You are going for the stone if I go down and you are going for the stone if somebody screams and you are going for the stone if the whole of this pan comes up behind you, and when your feet are on it you keep walking, because this is only the first half."

"What's the second half?" said Wyck.

"The second half is eighty yards," said Rook, and went.



Toren pushed.

He had the tailboard in both hands and his boots in four years of ash and his grandmother eighteen inches in front of his face, facing backward, watching the whole of it happen over his shoulder, and he pushed, and the wheels turned, and it was the slowest thing he had ever been part of.

Behind him and to the left there was a man doing something enormous.

He could not see it. He got it in noise and in what the light did on the grey in front of him — a wash of white going up and going out, and going up again, and the flat hard sound Rook's spear made going into something, and the long soft roar of four yards of ash falling over. Three times. Four.

Each time the roar came, Toren pushed harder, because each time the roar came it meant the thing was down for about two seconds.

On the fourth one he heard Rook shout something that was not a word.

"He's put it in the water," Senna said.

"What?"

"He's driven it off the west side into the water and it's gone in and it hasn't come out." His grandmother had both hands on the front board and she was up off the blankets on her elbows, eighty-one years old, looking backward down a quarter mile of open pan. "Don't look round. Push."

Toren pushed.

The wheels went from grey onto stone with a change of sound like a door shutting, and all four of them were on the tip road.



Rook came on last, backward, at a walk, with the frame still burning round his legs and the spear at half length in his right hand.

"It'll come," he said. "It's not done. Keep walking."

"Why did you—"

"Because it can't do that on stone." He was breathing like a man who has been carrying something up a hill. "It goes down into a ground and it comes up out of a ground and both of those need a ground. There isn't one out here. Eighty yards of block and water on both hands and it has got nothing to go into and nothing to come out of, and if it wants us it has to walk, and it is enormous, and it will be slow." He put a hand flat on the parapet block for a second and took it off again. "That's what the road's for. That's what it's been for since I saw it off the weighbridge yesterday morning."

"Is that certain?" said Dessa.

"No," said Rook. "It's the best I've got and I've had a day to make it better and I couldn't."

They walked.



Twenty yards out along the stone, Toren looked down over the edge.

He had not meant to. The water was eight or ten feet below the road and it went down from there into a green-black nothing and there was old machinery in it — the top of a gantry standing up out of the surface about forty feet off, red-brown, with a bird on it.

There was ash on the water.

It lay on the surface in a wide grey film, not sinking, spreading slowly out from a place near the west bank and moving toward the road, and Toren watched it for two whole seconds before anything in him understood what he was looking at.

"Rook—"

The stone came up.

It came up out of the road itself, out of the packed grey and grit that forty years of carts had ground into the joints between the blocks, eleven feet ahead of the barrow — not four yards of it this time but half that, thin and wrong-shaped and already sliding apart at the edges, and it did not matter, because it was directly in front of the near wheel and there was water on both sides.

Dessa went off the road.

She did not fall. She was on the near shaft and it swung, and she let go of it because holding it would have taken the barrow with her, and she went over the edge sideways into forty feet of water with her arms out, and the last thing Toren saw of her was her face, which had nothing on it but a decision.



"Go round it!" Rook was coming up the road at a run. "Voss — the shaft — go round—"

There was no round. There was twelve feet of stone and a thing standing in nine of it.

Wyck Tanner came off the off shaft and forward and put three feet of white light into the side of it, and it came apart, and there was grey all over the road, and it started to draw in again.

"Don't let it lie!" Rook shouted. "Kick it in the water — kick it off—"

Wyck kicked it off. He did it on one leg, holding the parapet block with both hands, sweeping the grey off the edge of the road with the side of his boot in long flat strokes, and where the grey went into the water it went out flat and thin and stopped being anything.

And then he did it again, further up, moving backward along the stone on the stick with three feet of light still standing out of his fist — moving, and holding it, which he had never in his life been able to do and did not notice he was doing until four days later.



Behind them, at the near end of the road, the pan stood up.

Toren had the shafts. He had both of them, alone, because Dessa was in the water and Wyck was eleven feet ahead of him sweeping ash off stone with his boot, and he had the barrow moving at the pace one thirteen-year-old boy can move a loaded barrow on the flat, which was a walk, which was not enough.

At the near end of the tip road the whole of it came up together, all of it at once, taller than it had been on the pan because it had taken back everything it had lost — and it came onto the stone.

It came on at about the pace of a man walking quickly.

Rook turned round on the road and went back at it and Toren heard him go, and that was the last help there was going to be, because sixty yards behind him one man was standing in twelve feet of gap with an old woman's Ember, and forty yards in front of him, where the tip road ran up onto the south bank and the second haul road began, a Flask came up out of the shallows and folded itself into the only way off.



Nine seconds. He worked it out afterward and it was probably eleven.

He had the shafts in both hands. He had two feet of Edge in his coat pocket, four inches of reach away, and the thing on the bank in front of him was an ordinary Flask, a plain one, the kind he had put down twice at the weir and once in the birch, and he could have gone through it on any other afternoon of his life.

To do that he had to let go of the shafts.

If he let go of the shafts the barrow stopped, and if the barrow stopped he was standing still on eighty yards of stone with a thing behind him that was coming at the speed of a man walking quickly.

To not let go of the shafts he had to move faster than he could move.

He had Stride. He had six nights of it and five seconds of it standing still and not one single step, and it was in his legs, and his hands were full, and the two of them lived in the same place and you got one.

You pick, his father had said, in the wet grass under a broken overpass on the last good night of his life, with the fire behind him and the whole valley going dark blue. One at a time. Never both. You pick, and you live with what you picked until you can put it down and pick again.

Why never both?

Because that's not how it works.

And then the other thing. The thing Garrick Voss had said next, across the top of the fire's heat, in a voice Toren had not understood at the time and had carried in his coat for eight weeks without ever once taking it out and looking at it.

That's what I was told, too.



He did not reach and he did not ask and he did not decide which foot.

He stopped picking.

The white in his fist did not go out, because it was not in his fist, and it never had been. The frame came up his legs hip to heel and it did not take anything with it on the way. The shafts went light in his hands the way a full bucket goes light when somebody else takes the handle.

He was never afterward able to tell anybody what it was like, because from the inside it was not like anything at all. There was a very large quiet room at the back of a house he had been living in for thirteen years, and he had been standing in the doorway of it since the fifth of the month, and he walked in.

Then he was doing forty yards of stone with a loaded barrow behind him and his grandmother shouting something he could not hear.



He took the Flask on the run and he took it wrong.

That was the honest account of it and it was the one he gave, every time he was ever asked, for the rest of his life. He did not have a stroke ready and he did not pick a side and he went in too high, and two feet of white light went through the shoulder of the thing instead of the middle of it, and it came apart anyway because they come apart, and the ash went up and over him and into his mouth and his eyes, and he did not stop and could not have.

The wheels came off the stone onto the ramp of the south haul road.

He got four more paces up the slope and the frame went out.

It did not fade. It went out the way a lamp goes out, all of it at once, and his legs were legs again and they had nothing whatsoever in them, and he went down on the ramp between the shafts with the barrow running back on him and did not have one thing left to do about it.

The barrow stopped.

Wyck Tanner had come up the ramp behind him on a stick and got both hands on the tailboard and put his whole weight into it, standing on one leg, and he held it, and he was shouting, and Toren could not hear any of that either.



The last of it happened sixty yards away and Toren watched it lying on his side on a quarry ramp with his cheek on the stone.

The Breach came up the tip road and Rook did not let it past.

That was all it was. A man on twelve feet of pale block, going backward, and a thing the height of a house coming on at the speed of a man walking quickly, and the man putting white light into it every time it got inside four feet — and every time, the thing coming apart, and going down, and having nothing to go down into.

It came apart eleven times on that road.

It stood up eleven times, and each time it stood up smaller, because each time a quarter of what it was went off the edges of eighty yards of stone into forty feet of standing water, and water is not a ground, and there is nothing in it to come back out of.

On the eleventh it stood up about the size of a dog.

Rook put the spear through it and it went to ash on the blocks and it lay there, warm and grey, with the light crawling in it, and it did not go anywhere, because there was nowhere for it to go.

Then Rook put a hand on the parapet and sat down.



Dessa came up out of the water on the east side of the south bank at about twenty past one, with her coat gone and one boot gone and a cut across the back of her head, having swum sixty yards in her clothes and climbed a quarry face, and the first thing she did on getting to the top of it was look for the barrow, and the second thing she did was start running.

Rook was still sitting on the parapet block when she got to him. He had his left arm across his front and his hand inside his coat, and the coat was wet through from the bottom of the ribs down on that side, and it had gone through the coat and out onto the stone.

"Move your hand," said Dessa Cole.

He moved his hand.

She worked for a long time. Toren, who by then had been got up the ramp and propped against a wheel and could see out of about half of one eye, watched her do it and could not have said one useful thing about it. What he did see, and remembered, was the moment about four minutes in when she stopped and sat back on her heels and put both palms flat on her own knees and breathed twice, and then went back in — and did not go and lie down afterward, because that had stopped being something that happened to her a month ago on a riverbank.

"That's the ends together," she said at last.

"That'll do."

"That is not the ends together. That is the ends nearly together and the rest of it wants somebody with a table and a light." Her voice was completely level and her hands were shaking. "Why can't you close it?"

"Because there isn't any left," said Rook.

"There's—" She looked round at the road. There was a great deal of ash on it. There was more ash on eighty yards of quarry block than any of them had ever seen in one place in their lives, more than Braid had had in her whole tunnel, lying in drifts against the parapet and refusing, still, to go anywhere.

"There it is. It's right there. Take it up, you've done it a hundred—"

"No," said Rook.

"Why?"

"Because that is exactly what it did," he said, "and I have spent the last hour watching what happens to something that stands on a road and draws ash up out of the ground with nobody home inside it, and I am going to sit here and bleed instead."



Nobody moved for a while after that.

Senna Voss said, from the barrow, "Elias."

"I'm here."

"Grade it."

"What?"

"Grade it," said his grandmother. "You've been grading things at me since the fifth of the month. Somebody in this family is going to stand on that road and say the word out loud and it is not going to be one of the children."

Rook turned his head and looked at eighty yards of stone with the grey lying on it in drifts.

"That's a Barrel," he said.

"Say the rest."

"That's two casks. It's two casks and it's clean and it's the best weighing anybody will see out of this country in ten years, and it's nine miles from a gate that buys." He put his head back against the parapet. "And I'd give the whole of it to have a table and a light."



They came up onto the south lip at about five in the afternoon: an old woman in a barrow, a boy of fifteen on a stick pulling it with a girl who had lost a boot, a man walking behind holding his own side, and Toren Voss riding on the tailboard because he could not be made to stand up.

The sun went down in front of them, out across nine miles of flat open country.

And while they watched, in a wide ring half a mile across, out in the empty ground with nobody near them and nothing inside them, one at a time and then all together, the lamps came on.



Chapter 27: Intake

They took about half a cask off the tip road and left the rest.

It was all they could carry. They had one canvas and two empty supply bundles and a barrow with a woman in it, and they filled what they had by scraping the drifts off the quarry block with a tin plate and the flat of a boot sole, and it took forty minutes, and Rook sat on the parapet the whole time and gave instructions and did not lift anything.

"There's a cask and a half on that road," said Wyck.

"There is."

"Somebody'll come and take it."

"Somebody will," said Rook. "Good luck to them. I've had enough off it."



They walked all night, because Rook would not stop.

He set the pace himself for the first two miles and then stopped setting it and let Dessa set it, which he had never done, and nobody said anything about that either. Toren rode the tailboard for four miles and then got off it and walked, poorly, and was allowed to.

At about three in the morning Rook fell back and walked beside him for a while.

"Voss."

"Yes."

"You had both of them on that ramp."

Toren did not say anything. He had known it since about two seconds after it stopped, and he had been carrying it beside him for eleven hours the way you carry a thing you cannot look at while you are moving.

"Nine seconds," said Rook. "Maybe eleven. You came forty yards of stone with a loaded barrow and you took a Flask on the way past and you did it poorly, and the frame was up your legs the whole of it and there were two feet of white in your hand the whole of it, and I watched you do it from sixty yards with something on top of me and I want you to know that I saw it, because I'd have been sorry all my life if I hadn't."

"What is it?"

"What's what?"

"What's it called," said Toren.

"It isn't called anything," said Rook.

They walked about thirty paces.

"People have got a word for what it looks like from the outside," he said. "I'm not giving it to you and I'll tell you exactly why, and then you can be angry about it for a week like you were about the other one. It's because the word describes what somebody watching you can see, and you'd start trying to be it. You'd go out on a verge every night for a year trying to get back to a thing you did once when you had no choice, and you'd get nothing, and you'd decide you'd lost it.

"So here's what you're getting instead, and it's true and it's the whole of it.

"You're Flame. You were Flame at that weir and you're Flame now and nothing that happened on that road moved you a rung. What you did wasn't a bigger amount of what you had. It was a different thing, and it isn't on the ladder anywhere, and you may not do it again for a year and you may do it on Thursday, and either of those is normal."

"Then what was it?"

"It was you stopping," said Rook.

"Stopping what?"

"Picking." He had his hands in his pockets, which he could only do with one of them. "You've been told since you were six that it's one at a time. Your father was told. I was told. Everybody who has ever held one of those things has been told, and it is a perfectly good rule for anybody who believes the thing in their hand is doing the work, because a stick can only be one shape at once.

"You didn't stop believing it tonight. You stopped believing it about five weeks ago under a stone arch in the rain and you've agreed with me about it every day since. Tonight was the first time in your life you were too busy to remember."



The lamp line came up on them at about half past four.

Toren had been looking at it for three hours as a low ring of small orange sparks on a black plain, and he had built a whole idea of it in his head on the walk — poles and oil and somebody with a ladder — and the idea did not survive the first one they came within fifty feet of.

It was a post of welded pipe about nine feet high with a flat iron plate bolted across the top of it, and standing on the plate, unsupported, needing nothing, was a light.

It was about the size of a fist. It was pale and steady and it did not gutter and it did not drift and there was nothing under it and nothing in it, and it sat on a cold iron plate nine feet in the air in the middle of empty ground at half past four in the morning, and it did not need anybody at all.

Toren stopped walking.

"Voss," said Rook, from ahead.

He could not make his legs do anything.

There were two hundred of them. He could see the whole ring from here, a circle of small pale lights half a mile across with a black wall inside it and nothing between, and every single one of them was sitting on a plate on a post out in the open country where anything that came looking for worked power would find it first.

"They're all his," he said.

"They're not his."

"They're his." His voice had gone. "That's the — he did that on a stone above a winding-house. He did it on a fence rail on the grade and he did it on the arch and everybody in Coldwater told him for two years it was the most useless thing anybody had ever come out of a Kindling with, and it's—"

"I know what it is," said Rook.

"There's two hundred of them."

"There's two hundred and forty and there's a trade for it and they've had it here forty years, and they send people out at dusk to set them and out at dawn to bring the posts in and look at the ground, and it is the single most dangerous job on this continent and every one of the people doing it is thirteen to nineteen years old and can do one thing." He came back and stood next to Toren in the dark. "Nobody ever told him. There wasn't anybody in Coldwater to tell him. That's all that is and it's not any smaller for being ordinary."

"Would they have taken him?"

"Yes," said Rook.

Toren stood in the grass and looked at two hundred and forty lights burning in empty country.

"Don't," said Rook.

"I'm not."

"You are. You're standing there doing the arithmetic where he's alive and it's four months from now and he's out here on a post line with a bag over his shoulder being pleased with himself, and I'd like you to put it down, because I have done that arithmetic about a woman four thousand times and it has never once come out." He put his good hand on Toren's shoulder, which he had done perhaps twice in eight weeks. "Walk. There's a gate."



The gate was a cut through a berm eleven feet high faced with broken concrete, with a ditch in front of it and two sets of doors, and between the sets of doors there was a yard about the size of a mill yard, roofed, with lamps in it that were only lamps.

They were put in the yard and the outer doors were shut behind them and they stood there for twenty minutes.

Nobody was rude. That was the thing Toren had not expected and could not get his hands round. A young man brought out a bench and set it against the wall for Senna and did not say anything to anybody; a woman came and looked at Rook's side through his coat without touching it and went away again at a run; somebody brought water in a bucket with a tin dipper, and the water was clean and cold and there was no limit on it.

And nothing happened, and nothing went on happening, and the inner doors stayed shut.

"They're looking at us," said Dessa.

"They're looking at us."

"From where?"

"From about four places," said Rook, "and I'd leave off finding out which."



The proctor's name was Nye and he came out at ten past five with three ledgers and a clerk and a folding table.

He was about forty, narrow, with reading glasses on a cord and ink on the side of his hand, and he set the table up himself and squared it to the wall before he sat down at it, and Toren, watching him do it, had the sudden violent sensation of standing in a tunnel under a fallen fuel tank with his father four days dead.

"Right," said Proctor Nye. "This takes about an hour and I'm going to do the whole of it even though one of you is bleeding, and I'll tell you why now so we don't have to argue about it in twenty minutes. The physician is standing on the other side of that door. She does not come through it until this is done. That is not me being hard, it's the rule, and the rule exists because the last time somebody made an exception at this gate, forty people died inside a fortnight." He opened the first ledger. "Names."

They gave their names.

He wrote down Coldwater and the date and the milepost. He wrote down Senna Voss, eighty-one; Toren Voss, thirteen; Dessa Cole, fifteen; Wyck Tanner, fifteen. He asked Dessa how to spell Cole, and Dessa said, "C, O, L, E," in a voice that did something at the end of it, and Nye looked up once and then went back to writing.

"And you."

"Elias Marsh."

"Marsh." The pen went along. "Age."

"Forty-one."

"Origin."

"A village called Ferrow, about four hundred and ten miles north. It's on your charter list and it's been lapsed nine years."

Nye wrote that down too.



"Kindled status, each of you, and I'll want it plain. Door and stage."

"Ember, Flame," said Wyck.

"Magic side," said Dessa. "Sensing mostly. Some closing. Flame."

"Ember, Flame," said Toren.

"Nothing," said Senna Voss, from the bench, "and I'd like it noted that I've had eighty-one years of people finding that disappointing."

"Noted," said Nye, and wrote it, and then looked at Rook.

"Ember and magic," said Rook. "Both."

The clerk's pen stopped. Nye's did not.

"Stage."

"Further up."

"I need a word, Mr Marsh."

"Then put down that I wouldn't give you one," said Rook, "and put down that I've been asked at this gate and asked properly, and that I've had nine years of not answering it and I'm not going to start at five in the morning with a hole in me. You'll get a better answer off your own physician in about an hour than you'll get off me."

Nye looked at him for two seconds and then wrote, in a small neat hand, declined, and went on.



"Devices."

"Three Embers," said Rook.

"Put them on the table."

Wyck put his on the table. Toren put his father's on the table, the grey rod the length of his forearm, worn glass-smooth in a band round the middle where four generations of Voss thumbs had sat in exactly the same place, and it made a small sound on the wood.

Rook took Vera's out of his coat and put it down beside them.

"They go into the gate store and they're tagged and they stay there," said Nye. "You get a receipt. You get them back when you leave, or on a call-out, and not otherwise, and there is no version of this where anybody works power inside that wall. I'll say the reason once, because everybody wants it and nobody believes it until they're told. There are four thousand people behind that berm and one of the reasons they're alive is that from out on that plain, this place is dark. The lights are out there. We put them out there. We spend seven or eight young people a year keeping them out there." He wrote the three Embers into the ledger. "You light anything inside the wall and you are a lamp in the middle of four thousand people. That's all it is. It isn't about what you are."

"It's a bit about what we are," said Wyck.

"Yes," said Proctor Nye. "It is a bit."



"Last item, and then the physician. Anything else on any of you that's Kindled work, rift-made, pre-Fall, or that you can't account for. Tools, objects, anything you're carrying that you can't explain."

Nobody said anything.

Toren stood in a roofed yard at twenty past five in the morning with his hand flat over two rows of stitches and understood, with complete clarity, that he could say no.

He could say no and the man would write nothing and they would open the door and his grandmother would go in. Nobody in that yard would ever know. Rook would know and Rook would not say one word about it ever, because Rook had put the whole of it in his hands at a junction and told him it was his.

He thought about his father's hands closing his fingers over it in wet grass, one at a time.

You put it in a river and you don't tell anyone where.

"I've got something," he said.



He put it on the table.

It was about the size of the round bottom of a cup and it was heavier than it had any business being, and it was some dark metal that was not quite any colour, and nine narrow channels ran across the face of it in a pattern that would not resolve.

Proctor Nye looked at it for some time without touching it.

"What is it?"

"I don't know."

"What does it do?"

"Nothing," said Toren. "It's never done anything. It's been in my family five generations and not one of us has ever got a single useful thing out of it."

"Where's it from?"

"I don't know that either."

Nye took his glasses off.

"Son," he said, "I've had thirty-one people through this gate in the last four days and eleven of them told me they were carrying a thing that would change the world, and every one of those eleven is now sitting in the outer sheds waiting on a hearing, because I write down what they tell me and then somebody senior comes and is disappointed. You've told me you've got a lump of metal that does nothing and you don't know where it's from. That's a worse answer and it's going to cost you a great deal more, and I'd like to know whether you understand that before I put the pen on the page."

"Yes," said Toren.

"Why are you telling me?"

Toren thought about it properly, because the man had asked properly.

"Because there's a ledger at Tallow Gate with my father's name in it," he said, "and the woman who wrote it told us it comes here twice a year and doesn't get lost. And I've spent eight weeks finding out what it costs to be the only person who knows something."

Nye put his glasses back on.

He wrote for about a minute and a half. Then he set the pen down, and picked up a small brass bell off the corner of the table, and rang it twice.



The inner doors opened at half past five and after that it went very fast.

The physician was a broad short woman of about fifty-five called Kell, and she went to Rook first because Rook was the one bleeding, and Rook said, "The old woman," and Kell said, "The old woman has had twenty years of it and you've had eleven hours, don't be a child," and got his coat open.

Senna went in on a wheeled chair with two people pushing it.

Toren went as far as the inner doors with her and was stopped there, and his grandmother reached out and got hold of his sleeve and pulled him down and said, into his ear, very clearly:

"Straighten up. Walk like people who've been somewhere."

And then she was gone down a corridor with light in it, electric light, steady and white and coming out of the ceiling, more light than Toren Voss had seen in the whole of his life put together.



Registrar Helen Quist came into the yard at six.

She was about sixty, tall, in a grey coat, with a face that did not do anything, and she had Nye's page in her hand and she read it once more standing up before she said a word to anybody.

"Cole," she said. "Tanner. Voss. Marsh."

"That's us," said Wyck.

"I know it's you." She folded the page. "I'm going to say three things and I'd like them heard in order.

"First. The woman is admitted. That's done and it isn't a favour and it isn't reversible and nobody in this yard has to be pleasant to me about it. She'll be seen properly this morning and somebody will come out and tell you what they've found, and it will be the truth, and you will not like all of it.

"Second." She looked at the four of them. "I've had two hundred and eleven people at that gate since the harvest. Nine days ago I had a family off the fen, eleven of them, grandmother down to an infant, and when my proctor told them to stand apart and put their hands where he could see them it took four minutes and they got it wrong twice and one of them cried.

"My people put you in that yard at four in the morning and shut a door on you. You went to the four corners of it in about a second and a half. The girl took the side with no light on it. The lame one put himself between the bench and the doors. Nobody looked at anybody and nobody said one word, and then you all stood still for twenty minutes like people who have stood still before."

"We're not a company," said Rook.

"No," said Registrar Quist. "You're worse than a company. A company I can write to."



"Third," she said.

She held up the folded page.

"You came up the south haul road last night out of a country that put eleven experienced haulers to flight inside a week, with two casualties, one non-ambulant, and half a cask of Barrel-grade on a handcart. I've had a runner down to the Drownings since first light and he came back an hour ago and told me there's a cask and a half of it lying on the tip road in drifts and eleven separate places on eighty yards of stone where something enormous went down and got up again.

"So I have four Kindled, one of them a man who won't give me a stage, a child of thirteen, and a dead Barrel, and I have to decide by tonight what the five of you are.
